## Page 1


Macroeconomic Risks from Maritime
Trade Disruptions
Vipin P. Veetil∗
Fathimath S. Vemmarath†
July 2026
Abstract
This paper develops a model of maritime chokepoint closures in which interrupting a
shipping passage produces losses that are not measured, or even bounded, by the value of the
trade that transits it. They stem from disruptions in the flow of intermediate goods that are
complementary in downstream production. Re-matching displaced trade on the buyer and seller
sides of the market limits the damage but cannot eliminate it. Across countries, the incidence
is heavy-tailed, and it reaches economies whose cargo never crosses the passage, not only
those that route their trade through it. The two ends of a severed corridor lose unequally, the
exporting side by three to five times as economic geography funnels commodity-concentrated
sellers through a single passage while their buyers re-source. Joint closures depart from the sum
of their parts: the Middle East scenario is sub-additive, while the East Asia and Russia–Europe
scenarios are super-additive.
JEL Codes: D57, D85, F14, F17, F51, L91.
Key Words: Maritime chokepoints; Production networks; Trade disruptions; Supply-chain risk.
∗Economics Area, Indian Institute of Management Kozhikode.
†Indian Statistical Institute, Kolkata.

Introduction
A quarter millennium ago, Smith (1776) placed at the foundation of economics the proposition
that the division of labour is limited by the extent of the market. And he was careful to say what it
is that extends the market: “As by means of water-carriage a more extensive market is opened to
every sort of industry than what land-carriage alone can afford it, so it is upon the sea-coast, and
along the banks of navigable rivers, that industry of every kind naturally begins to subdivide and
improve itself.” The world economy of our day is Smith’s observation made planetary. The greater
part of international trade travels by sea. And what the ships carry is no longer principally wine
and wool for final consumption but the intermediate inputs of a division of labour stretched across
continents: crude for refineries, feedstocks for chemical works, ores for smelters, components for
assembly lines. The sea, however, does not spread this commerce evenly over its surface. Cost and
geography funnel it through a small number of narrow passages, a canal here, a strait there, which
means that the division of labour of an entire planet is made to pass, at certain points, through
channels a few miles wide.
The world has lately been reminded of what this implies. In March 2021 a single container
ship ran aground in the Suez Canal and held up something close to 1/10 of world seaborne trade
for six days. Two years later drones and missiles in the Bab-el-Mandeb emptied the Red Sea of
container traffic and pushed the Asia–Europe trades around the Cape of Good Hope. Then a
drought drew down the reservoirs that feed the Panama Canal and rationed its locks. As for the
Strait of Hormuz, a war-risk premium has hung over it for nearly forty years, and in March 2026
the risk it priced finally arrived: the strait closed to nearly 90% of its usual traffic. Each of these
episodes was geographically minute: a canal, a strait, a few hundred miles of contested water. But
each generated economic consequences out of all proportion to its geography. The reason is that a
maritime chokepoint is not a place where goods are merely priced or delayed. It is the needle’s eye
through which the supply of intermediate inputs to the world’s producers must pass: the narrowest
point of Smith’s extended market, and the command of the narrow seas that Mahan (1890) taught a
century of strategists to prize.
What would it cost the world economy if one of these passages closed, and how are these losses
distributed among the nations of the world? This question sits at the center of a growing policy
concern on supply-chain security, yet reliable answers are wanting. The number that appears in
nearly every assessment (1/5 of the world’s oil transits Hormuz, 1/10 of its seaborne trade transits
Suez) is the value of the trade that crosses the passage. But whether that volume gauges the cost of
closure is a theory-dependent proposition. In an economy without a production network, where a
severed shipment is simply a shipment lost, the transiting value is the loss. In an economy whose
producers are bound to one another by intermediate inputs, it is not. A Korean refinery cannot run
on the crude it cannot receive, and a German chemical works cannot run on the Asian feedstocks
2


## Page 3


that do not arrive. The conventional gauge counts the severed shipment once, at the strait. The
economy though loses it again at every downstream producer for whom it was a necessary input,
and may not lose it at all where a surviving supplier can be found. Put differently, the map from
transiting trade to macroeconomic loss is drawn by the production network. And it is neither
proportional to the transiting value nor even monotone in it: a country that ships little through a
chokepoint can be devastated by its closure, and a country that ships a great deal can be barely
touched.
At bottom this is a statement about equilibrium, and it is what makes a counterfactual exper-
iment necessary. The trade that crosses a strait is not a fixed inventory of shipments waiting to
be tallied. It is an equilibrium object, i.e. the outcome of every producer’s sourcing and selling
optimal decisions taken under the present configuration of routes. Closing the strait changes that
configuration, and the network rewires in response. Buyers re-source toward the suppliers they
can still reach, severed suppliers push idle output onto the customers that remain, the order book
rebalances on both sides at once, with these decisions being guided by the complementarity be-
tween inputs. The equilibrium the economy settles into afterwards is therefore not the benchmark
equilibrium with the exposed links deleted. Put differently, the pre-shock flows are generated by
the very configuration the closure dismantles, and cannot serve as a sufficient statistic for the loss
their disappearance sets in motion. Pre-shock flows can locate the cut, but only the post-shock fixed
point can locate the loss. Computing that post-shock equilibrium is precisely what we undertake in
this paper. We build a structural counterfactual that closes each maritime chokepoint in turn and
assigns the disrupted flows to the country-sector links of the world input-output table. We then
compute the activity lost in every economy once the disruption has worked its way through the net-
work. The exercise covers twelve chokepoints studied one at a time, and three politically coherent
joint closures (Middle East, East Asia, and Russia–Europe) drawn from recognizable geopolitical
fault lines. The model is the short-run production-network specification of Bhattathiripad and
Veetil (2026), developed there for trade sanctions and adapted here to maritime closure.
3


## Page 4


Figure 1: The twelve maritime chokepoints studied in the paper. A terminal gate, the sole sea
exit of an enclosed basin, admits no detour and is drawn as a square; a bypassable gate can be
circumvented by a longer voyage and is drawn as a circle. The three principal detours are shown
as dashed arcs: around the Cape of Good Hope for the Suez–Bab-el-Mandeb–Gibraltar corridor,
around Cape Horn for Panama, and through the Lombok and Sunda Straits for Malacca. This
terminal–bypassable contrast is the geographic content of the reroutability matrix of Section 3 and
Online Appendix OA.2.
The counterfactual rests on four premises, and they are best introduced as the stages in the
life of a closure. The first concerns where a closure can cut. Seaborne goods travel on least-cost
routes through specific passages, which means that the set of intermediate-input links a closure
can sever is determined by route geography, not chosen by the model. Where a longer voyage
around the obstacle exists (Lombok and Sunda for Malacca, the Cape of Good Hope for Suez) part
of each severed shipment can be salvaged at a cost that rises with the detour’s share of the voyage.
A terminal gate such as Hormuz, the sole exit of an enclosed basin, admits no such salvage. In
practice, a maritime closure is never total: convoying, naval escort, war-risk-priced but still-sailing
tonnage, and gray-market reflagging keep a residual flow moving through even a contested passage.
And the leakage-residual differs systematically by what is shipped. We therefore let each closed
link retain a leakage share of its benchmark flow rather than vanish outright. The residual is large.
When war-risk premia closed the Red Sea to container traffic in 2023–24, box transits fell by nine-
tenths at the trough while more than 1/2 of the tankers kept sailing under attack. For a determined,
sustained closure (the counterfactual the paper studies, one the historical record bounds only from
below), we take as our baseline the severe uniform reading in which each closed link retains a tenth
of its benchmark flow, energy included. We report the milder commodity-differentiated reading
(45% retention on a severed energy flow) as a robustness exercise.1
1The exceptions are the physical obstructions: the 1967–75 Suez closure and the six-day Ever Given grounding drove
4


## Page 5


The remaining parts of our model govern what the economy does with the trade a closure
displaces. Displaced trade re-matches on both sides of the market at once: buyers redirect
lost demand toward surviving suppliers of the same input, and severed suppliers redirect idle
output toward the buyers they can still reach. We assume that the re-matching is neither free
nor unbounded: a fraction of the displaced mass is unrecoverable within the disruption horizon.
And no producer expands beyond its pre-shock scale, for ports, refineries, terminals, and trade
relationships are fixed in the short run. Finally, we incorporate the role of complementarity between
inputs by punishing deviations from pre-shock optimal combinations. In the model therefore
a closure severs input links net of detours and leakage, reallocates orders under frictions and
capacity constraints, and leaves some production bundles incomplete. The resulting productivity
and delivery losses cascade downstream until the economy reaches a new equilibrium. The paper
reports the country-level gap between that equilibrium and the benchmark. The inputs into the
model are disciplined by route geography, observed bilateral flows, historical disruption episodes,
and parameters from the short-run production-network literature. Incidence is mapped from
routes and trade flows, leakage is estimated from eleven past disruptions, and re-matching and
complementarity are modeled through capacity-constrained balancing and CES production. 2
Three findings emerge from our analysis. The first is that chokepoint exposure is heavy-tailed
across countries. Closing the Strait of Hormuz alone costs the world about 0.6% of value added.
But the loss is borne very unevenly: the United Arab Emirates loses about 17% of its GDP, Saudi
Arabia and the remaining Gulf states about 10% each, while most of the world loses well under
1%. The tail is not confined to the Gulf. Poland, for instance, loses about 10% of its GDP behind a
closed Danish Straits (its seaborne fuel and materials feed power generation and construction). A
small number of economies lose a large share of GDP under their single most binding closure, and
the great majority face only modest losses.
Our second finding is an exporter–importer asymmetry. The two ends of a severed corridor
do not share its loss evenly. The supplier-side economy loses, in proportional terms, several times
what the buyer-side economy loses, and by an order of magnitude or more at the energy gates. The
balancing treats the two sides symmetrically; the asymmetry emerges from economic geography.
More specifically, the RAS balancing gives buyers and sellers the same formal opportunity to
re-match: buyers can redirect lost demand to surviving same-sector suppliers, and sellers can
the residual to zero, where war-risk closures, the empirically dominant mode, have not. We read the leakage as the
steady-state share of benchmark trade that the residual channels sustain.
2Two ingredients carry the identification logic. First, the capacity-capped RAS re-matching is the feasible order
book closest to the pre-shock pattern of transactions, moving existing relationships only as much as the caps require.
Because it is neutral between buyers and suppliers, the division of a corridor’s loss between its two ends can be read as
measurement rather than convention. By contrast, under a one-sided Leontief or Ghosh inverse, that division is imposed
by the accounting. Second, the post-closure allocation is a triangular fixed point: supplier-level fulfillment rates solve a
delivery loop on their own, and activity follows by a single linear solve. The benchmark-selected equilibrium exists
unconditionally, is the equilibrium of least loss, is the unique equilibrium with every country-sector active, and nests
the benchmark when there is no closure.
5


## Page 6


redirect idle output to surviving same-sector buyers. The map is therefore not tilted toward
importers or exporters. But economic geography and the original benchmark trade network are
tilted. Chokepoint suppliers are often commodity-concentrated exporters funneled through one
corridor: Gulf energy through Hormuz, Caspian/Black Sea exports through the Turkish Straits,
Baltic energy and materials through the Danish Straits. Once the corridor closes, they have few
off-corridor same-sector buyers onto which displaced output can be placed. Importers, by contrast,
are usually more diversified across suppliers and typically do not find themselves behind narrow
geographies.
Behind the first two findings sits an exact accounting. At the post-closure equilibrium the
model admits a four-channel identity (Section 4.7). It apportions every country’s loss among direct
severance of its trade, network-propagation of its partners’ contraction, and the CES bundle penalty
on autonomous output and on intermediate sales. The decomposition shows that the two sides of a
chokepoint lose for structurally different reasons. Exporters lose because their market is physically
severed and the unplaced remainder is written off. Importers lose because reallocation cannot fully
reconstitute the input mix their technologies were built around: Japan’s about 0.5% Hormuz loss
arrives without a single Japanese shipment crossing the strait. The same accounting locates each
economy on a spectrum from second-hand to direct loss. The United States’s residual loss is almost
entirely network-propagation, while African economies, commodity-narrow on the export side and
import-dependent downstream, absorb the bulk of their losses directly. And there is no separate
capacity-rationing channel to apportion: under the flow balancing the order-book cap coincides
with each supplier’s delivery ceiling, so the physical constraint is spent at the re-matching stage
and post-closure demand never exceeds capacity (a lemma, not a finding of the decomposition).
Our third finding concerns joint closures. A regional scenario can cost the world more or less
than the sum of its single-closure parts. Which of the two occurs is governed by the sectoral
structure of what the constituent closures sever. When they impair distinct sectoral inputs at
common downstream buyers, the complementarity of those inputs compounds the damage and the
joint loss exceeds the sum of its parts. When they impair overlapping flows on shared corridors,
the closures largely duplicate one another and the joint loss falls short of the sum. The Middle
Eastern scenario falls on the sub-additive side: Suez and Bab-el-Mandeb share the Cape detour and
Hormuz has already severed the Gulf oil the other two would carry, so the joint costs about 0.9%
of world value added against a constituent sum near 1.1%. The East Asian and Europe–Russia
scenarios fall on the super-additive side, their distinct-route gates starving common buyers of
complementary inputs at once, though both are modest in level: the East Asian joint costs under
0.2% and the Europe–Russia joint about 0.9%. The split has a clean theoretical anatomy. In the
linear part of the model the world loss is provably sub-additive: a flow lost to one closure is not
lost again at the next, so the mass a joint closure disrupts is the union, not the sum, of what its
6


## Page 7


constituents disrupt, the logic of the Bonferroni inequality. Any super-additivity must therefore be
carried by the nonlinear channel, and we give a condition, distinct complementary inputs starved
at a common buyer, under which the CES cascade provably overturns the linear sub-additivity.
Two further properties of the equilibrium are worth flagging. Closing more passages need not
hurt every producer: the re-matching leans the displaced demand of severed suppliers onto the
survivors, and under the East Asian joint a handful of country-sectors end up busier than under
one of its constituent single closures, though the gains are trace-sized. And the Middle East joint
crosses a frontier beyond which the model admits no differentiated equilibrium at all; we read that
refusal to produce a number as a finding rather than a failure.
1.1
Related literature
We are not the first to study the economic consequences of the maritime closures. What distin-
guishes our approach is that we ask counterfactual questions, whereas much of the literature before
us has focused on analyzing the consequences of past closure events. Perhaps the richest of these
are ‘natural experiment’ studies that analyze closures and expansions of certain maritime pathways
to estimate their global welfare effects and certain distance elasticities. These include Feyrer’s
study of the 1967–75 Suez closure, Hugot and Umana-Dajud’s analysis of the openings of Suez and
Panama, Heiland et al.’s work on the 2016 Panama Canal expansion, and Qu et al.’s study of the
2021 Suez blockage. These papers together show that maritime geography matters for welfare well
beyond their local regions. Perhaps the studies that come closest to ours in spirit are K¨anzig and
Raghavan (2026) and Meza et al. (2022). K¨anzig and Raghavan estimate the aggregate effects of
supply-chain shocks centered on Suez and Panama, finding effects on shipping rates, production,
prices, and sectoral outcomes. Their object is a reduced-form response to realized shocks. Ours
is a structural counterfactual. We ask how a hypothetical closure cuts intermediate-input links,
how much of the lost trade can be recovered, and how the remaining shortfall propagates through
production. Meza et al. is a counterfactual study somewhat like our own but they limit themselves
to LNG trade flows.3
Insofar as our counterfactual approach runs through the distortions of the input-output table,
it is naturally related to the modern literature on the propagation of shocks through production
networks. The closest kin within that literature are the studies of sizeable natural disasters, like
Carvalho et al.’s study of the T¯ohoku earthquake. The benchmark result here is Hulten’s theorem,
which claims that the network collapses into a sufficient statistic. In an efficient economy the
first-order effect of a small productivity shock on aggregate output is the affected producer’s
sales share, so the whole input-output structure reduces to a single network-weighted number. A
3See also Verschuur, Lumma and Hall (2025) work on measuring Germany’s exposure to six chokepoints across
products, sectors, and partners. They rely primarily on direct effects through measures like lost trade volume.
7


## Page 8


chokepoint closure lies beyond that benchmark twice over. It is not the marginal shock the theorem
differentiates against but the discrete severance of a set of links, so its cost is the full nonlinear
response of the economy, not a derivative. And the short-run economy it strikes is not the efficient
one the theorem assumes: its cross-sector inputs are complements rather than substitutes and
its capacities are fixed, so the envelope logic that isolates the first-order term breaks down and
higher-order terms come to carry the loss.
Many of the empirically significant results of our paper are, in this sense, a ‘beyond Hulten’
phenomenon, an instance of Baqaee and Farhi’s more general claim that frictions and nonlinearities
shape the aggregate effects of microeconomic shocks (Baqaee and Farhi, 2019). The super-additivity
of joint closures is one such nonlinearity, a direct consequence of the CES technology and the
capacity constraint; the divergence between mean and tail exposure is another. More generally,
our results underline the idea that systemic risk is embedded in the topology of the network
of economic relations, not only in the country-sectors or the flows between them. Mean and
tail exposure, in particular, do not share an origin. Mean risk reflects the average incidence of
chokepoint-dependent trade, whereas tail risk reflects the particular closure that severs a country’s
least substitutable input corridor (Adrian and Brunnermeier, 2016; Acharya et al., 2017).
Finally, because a chokepoint is a point at which the web of world production can be deliberately
cut, our exercise connects to the literature on economic statecraft and weaponized interdependence.
The asymmetry we compute between the two ends of a severed corridor is a quantitative instance of
the influence effect that Hirschman (1945) placed at the center of the politics of trade. Later work by
Keohane and Nye (1977), Baldwin (1985), Farrell and Newman (2019), and Clayton, Maggiori and
Schreger (2024) developed it into an account of how central positions in economic networks become
instruments of coercion. Where that work is concerned chiefly with financial, informational, and
technological chokepoints, ours restores the term to its original, physical referent (the narrow seas)
and prices the loss that closing one of them would inflict. In this it is a companion to Bhattathiripad
and Veetil (2026), whose short-run production-network model we adapt, and which turns the same
two-sided balancing to the measurement of national power under trade sanctions.
1.2
Code, data, and replication
All code, data, and results behind the paper are public. We have tried to make both replication and
extension as low-cost as we could. The source repository (https://bitbucket.org/VipinVeetil/
chokepoints) holds the model, the full data-processing pipeline, and the scripts that regenerate
every number, table, and figure below. The code is released under the MIT license. The calibrated
model inputs (the input-output operators for the 87 economies and 50 sectors of our 2019 bench-
mark) are archived on Zenodo (DOI: 10.5281/zenodo.21263267) under a CC BY 4.0 license. We
do not redistribute the two proprietary primary sources, the GTAP 10 Data Base and the OECD
8


## Page 9


Inter-Country Input-Output tables. But the repository documents how to obtain them and rebuild
the inputs from scratch. To lower the cost of replication further still, we distribute the model itself
as the maritime-choke package on the Python Package Index. The single command pip install
maritime-choke installs the solver and all its dependencies. On first use it fetches the calibrated
inputs automatically. A user therefore needs no prior configuration of a scientific-computing
environment to reproduce our results or run scenarios of their own. The package also carries an
interactive interface, launched with maritime-choke run, that opens in a web browser. There a
user can close any subset of the twelve chokepoints, vary the structural parameters, and solve the
model forward, reading the resulting value-added losses off a world map disaggregated by country
and by sector. Every output is written to disk. These tools let researchers and policymakers study
disruptions well beyond the ones we consider here.
1.3
Organization of the paper
The paper proceeds as follows. Section 2 sets up the short-run production-network model and the
benchmark-selected equilibrium that defines the post-closure counterfactual. It also summarizes
the extended input-output matrix that resolves the chokepoint-relevant economies hidden inside
the OECD Rest-of-World composite. Section 3 builds the chokepoint incidence matrix from route
geography, the set of seaborne sectors, and observed bilateral flows. Section 4 reports the master
exposure matrix, establishes the heavy tail and the exporter–importer asymmetry, and decomposes
each country’s loss into its economic channels. Section 5 extends the analysis to the three joint
scenarios and states the sub- and super-additivity results that discipline them. Section 6 takes
the assembled model to the data, identifying the 2023–24 Red Sea closure off the cross-section of
country-sector losses the network predicts. The appendices carry the supporting material: Online
Appendix OA.1 constructs the extended input-output matrix. Online Appendix OA.2 documents
the reroutability matrices. Online Appendix OA.3 the numerical stability of the iteration and the
phase transition that bounds it. Appendix A collects the proofs deferred from the body. Online
Appendix OA.4 reports the robustness of the findings across the calibration rectangle. Online
Appendix OA.5 estimates the model’s three structural parameters: the leakage from historical
disruption episodes, and the substitution and reallocation frictions from the production-function
and supply-chain literatures. And Appendix B catalogs the notation.
2
The Model
The model is a world input–output economy pushed off its benchmark by the closure of a passage:
routes are severed, the market rewires its order book around the cut, and production absorbs what
the rewriting cannot recover. We build it in that order.
9

Let C denote the set of countries, with G := |C|, and S the set of sectors, with N := |S|. A
node of the world production network is a country-sector i ∈CS := C × S, so that |CS| = GN. We
write i = (c,s) interchangeably, where c = c(i) is the country of node i and s = σ(i) is its sector.
Rows are sellers and columns are buyers. Let zij ≥0 be the benchmark intermediate flow from
seller country-sector i to buyer country-sector j, collected in the matrix Z, and let µj := P
i zij be
buyer j’s total intermediate-input purchases. Whenever µj > 0, the benchmark input-share matrix
A = [aij] is defined by aij := zij/µj, so that each column of A describes the composition of a buyer’s
intermediate-input bundle and sums to one.
Let xi be benchmark gross activity of country-sector i, and let fi ≥0 be the part of demand
for i’s output that is not generated by intermediate purchases inside the network, including final
demand and other non-intermediate uses. Let 1 −βj := µj/xj be the share of country-sector j’s
activity that generates intermediate input demand, with βj ∈(0,1), and collect these shares in the
diagonal matrix β := diag(β1,...,βGN). The two primitives describe the two sides of a node’s ledger,
βi its purchases and fi the demand for its output, and at benchmark they are tied by the world
accounting identity P

(1)
The term aij(1 −βj)xj is the intermediate demand that buyer j directs to seller i, and because each
column of A(I −β) sums to 1 −βj < 1, propagation through the network is dissipative. Dissipation
has a plain economic reading: every producer spends only part of its revenue on intermediates, so
a dollar of demand placed anywhere sets off a chain of upstream orders that fades geometrically
rather than echoing without end. Empirically, Z is the extended matrix Z∗of Online Appendix OA.1.
That matrix is the OECD Inter-Country Input-Output table at reference year 2019,4 with its Rest-
of-World composite split into seven maritime-relevant blocks using GTAP 10 bilateral shares, for
G∗= 87 economies at N = 50 sectors.
2.1
Chokepoint closure and route reroutability
Let K be the set of maritime chokepoints, with K := |K|. A chokepoint matters for the model only
through the benchmark flows that pass through it. For a chokepoint k ∈K, define γk,ij ∈{0,1} to
equal one if the benchmark flow from seller country-sector i to buyer country-sector j transits
chokepoint k, and zero otherwise.5
4We anchor on 2019, the last pre-pandemic reference year, because the benchmark should describe the world economy
in its ordinary state, and every later ICIO vintage is contaminated for our purpose: 2020 is the pandemic cross-section,
2021 the rebound and container-market dislocation, and 2022 carries the full-scale war in Ukraine, which rearranged
the very energy flows whose severance the counterfactuals study. The closures are therefore read against the last
normal-times network (Online Appendix OA.1).
5We construct these incidence indicators in Online Appendix OA.1 from route geography, sector restrictions, and
observed intermediate-flow data.
10

A counterfactual scenario is a set Q ⊆K of closed chokepoints together with the route geography,
and we write ω = (Q,E) once the reroutability profile E is defined below. Post-closure objects carry
ω as a superscript. We fix one such scenario and suppress it from the notation until losses are
compared across scenarios. The consequences of a closure are not uniform across trading pairs,
because a severed shipment can sometimes be salvaged by a longer voyage around the obstacle. A
reroutability matrix, constructed from route geography in Online Appendix OA.1, summarizes this
margin. For each chokepoint k and ordered country pair (c,c′), let ek,cc′ ∈[0,1] denote the share of
a benchmark flow from country c to country c′ that is lost when chokepoint k is closed, after the
shipment has exhausted any detour on its own route. The entry equals one when no viable detour
exists and zero when the route can be fully rerouted around k. The matrix is homogeneous across
sectors, since reroutability is a property of maritime geography rather than of the commodity
carried. For each chokepoint k, collect these shares in the G × G reroutability matrix Ek = [ek,cc′],
and we write E for the full profile (Ek)k∈K.
The fraction of link i →j disrupted under scenario Q is
dij := 1 −

.
(2)
The indicator γk,ij selects the flows whose benchmark route transits chokepoint k, and the entry
ek,c(i)c(j) grades the loss on those flows by the reroutability of the corresponding country pair. A flow
that transits no closed chokepoint has dij = 0, and a flow that transits a single closed chokepoint on
an unreroutable country pair has dij = 1. Our choice of the product form, rather than a sum, is
deliberate: a flow that transits two closed chokepoints is not removed twice. Its disrupted fraction
is instead the union 1 −(1 −e1)(1 −e2) of the loss events along its route, whereas the sum e1 + e2
would double-count the overlap and can exceed one.
We give the model three distinct margins of adjustment, and it is worth stating each one plainly
before the formal development. Reroutability is substitution across transport routes: a severed
shipment may still reach the same buyer by a longer voyage, the margin E grades. Reallocation
is substitution within an input sector, across suppliers: a buyer whose link is severed redirects
demand to surviving same-sector suppliers, and a severed supplier places idle output on surviving
same-sector buyers. The incompleteness of this adjustment is the friction τ. Complementarity
operates across input sectors: a missing sectoral input cannot be made good by buying more of a
different sector’s input, a force we let a CES bundle factor below penalize through an exponent
ρ < 0. We use “substitution” only with one of these qualifiers attached, so that the three margins
remain cleanly separated throughout.
A final ingredient remains. Closures are, in practice, rarely complete. Blockades are imperfectly
enforced, and the residual that keeps moving differs systematically by what is shipped (Online
11


## Page 12


Appendix OA.5.1). We therefore let each closed link retain a commodity-specific leakage. This
parameter does double duty in our framework: it is at once an economic primitive, describing
the residual flow that persists through a contested passage, and the regularizer that keeps the
balancing below well posed.
Assumption 1 (Closure leakage). For each shipped sector s ∈SM there is a leakage fraction δs ∈(0, ¯δ],
with 0 < ¯δ < 1 fixed, such that a link i →j whose geographic disruption under scenario Q is dij retains,
after closure, the share 1 −(1 −δσ(i))dij of its benchmark weight.
Here SM ⊂S is the set of seaborne goods sectors, the sectors whose output physically travels
by ship. A fully exposed link (dij = 1) thus keeps the residual δσ(i) aij, and a clear link (dij = 0) is
untouched. The leakage is indexed by the seller’s sector because it is a property of the shipped
good. In every observed episode, tankers persist through a contested passage while container lines
withdraw (Online Appendix OA.5.1).6 Every result below depends on the leakage only through the
support preservation that δs > 0 delivers.

2.2
Two-sided reallocation and the intended-order matrix
A closure forces adjustment on both ends of the market at once: the buyer looks for the missing
input elsewhere, and the supplier looks for a new outlet for output it can still produce. Letting only
the buyer re-optimize is the demand-driven, Leontief reading of the network, in which a severed
exporter’s lost sales simply vanish. Its transpose is the supply-driven, Ghosh reading, in which a
severed importer’s lost inputs vanish (Dietzenbacher, 1997). Each pole traps one side of the market
6The baseline calibration accordingly sets a uniform δs ≡0.10 for all seaborne goods; the commodity-differentiated
reading, δs = 0.45 for the energy sectors (B05, B06, C19) with δs = 0.10 elsewhere, is reported as a robustness exercise in
Online Appendix OA.4.

For a study whose central empirical object is precisely how unevenly the two ends of a severed
corridor share the loss, that is the wrong property. We therefore let both margins adjust at once, on
the relationships that survive the closure, by balancing the disrupted transactions matrix bZ (the
benchmark intermediate-flow matrix carried through the closure, bzij := zij

balancing we use is the classical RAS or biproportional adjustment of input–output analysis (Stone

to the transactions table itself. It is the unique nonnegative matrix on the surviving support that
respects both short-run caps at once, with no buyer sourcing beyond its intermediate budget
and no supplier shipping beyond its benchmark sales, something neither one-sided inverse can
do. Following Bhattathiripad and Veetil (2026), a single reallocation friction τ ∈[0,1] applies
symmetrically to the two margins: a fraction 1 −τ of each side’s displaced mass is reconstituted as
orders on surviving counterparties, and the residual τ is not recovered.
We organize reallocation within input sectors, because distinct input sectors are complements
while the country-sectors supplying a given input are mutual substitutes: one source of crude oil
stands in for another. The two-sided reallocation therefore stays inside the sector on both margins,
the entire construction is block-diagonal in the sector index, and the cross-sector input mix is left
for the CES technology below to penalize.

The column totals are exactly those of a one-sided Leontief rescaling. What the two-sided
balancing changes is the distribution of each buyer’s recovered mass across surviving suppliers,
solved jointly with those suppliers’ own row targets. It also restores severed suppliers’ rows, the
channel through which a cut exporter places idle output on the customers it can still reach. A
convenient scalar summary is the intended retention ratio c(ω)
sj /(µjαsj) = 1 −τℓsj/αsj, the share of

The balancing also admits a decision-theoretic foundation. On each disrupted block it is
the projection of the disrupted transactions matrix bZ onto the margin constraints in generalized
Kullback–Leibler divergence. The scalings ui and vj are therefore the exponentiated shadow prices
of seller i’s sales-flow (capacity) cap and buyer j’s budget (Remark 2 in Online Appendix OA.3).
The post-closure order book is the least-surprising feasible reorganization of trade, deviating from
an existing relationship only as far as the caps force it to. We wish to be clear about what we do
and do not claim. We deliberately claim nothing stronger than this minimal-revision property,
neither efficient re-matching nor market-clearing of the displaced mass; the balancing enforces the
two capacity constraints and nothing else.
The balancing itself is symmetric between its two margins, so the division of a corridor’s loss
between its ends is informative about the trade data rather than about the map. The next lemma
makes this precise.

not generated by the map. The lemma thereby converts the exporter–importer asymmetry from a

downstream fixed point stable.
Proposition 1 (Well-posedness and stability under leakage). Fix ω, τ ∈[0,1], and δ ∈(0, ¯δ] as in
Assumption 1.
(i) (Unconditional existence and uniqueness.) supp(bA) = supp(A), so each disrupted sector block
inherits the benchmark pattern’s full support and indecomposability. The balancing of Definition 1
therefore exists and is unique up to the gauge (u,v) 7→(λu,λ−1v), with strictly positive scalings.
(ii) (Geometric rate.) The Sinkhorn iteration converges on every block. On fully supported blocks it is
a strict contraction in the Hilbert projective metric, at a rate that degrades only logarithmically in
1/δ and degenerates exactly at the full-severance limit δ = 0.
Positive leakage thus buys the existence, uniqueness, and convergence guarantees that full
severance forfeits, though at a contraction rate that slows as enforcement tightens (Online Ap-
pendix OA.3). The degradation of the rate is itself economics: as the passage tightens, the balancing
must push the same capped totals through an ever-thinner residual channel, and the scalings
that accomplish it grow without bound. The balancing also delivers a retention floor that we use
repeatedly below.8 The matrix Aω describes what trade would be if every surviving supplier could
deliver in full. What is actually delivered is governed by the fulfillment factor hi ∈[0,1], the fraction
of the orders directed to supplier i that i delivers, which the next two subsections determine.
2.3
Realized retention, the CES bundle factor, and the output multiplier
Reallocation changes both the scale and the composition of a buyer’s intended input bundle. What
enters production, however, is the realized bundle, which falls short of the intended one wherever
a supplier cannot fill all the orders directed to it. We therefore index the CES bundle factor by the
vector h = (h1,...,hGN) of the fulfillment factors just introduced, so that the factor is determined
inside the post-closure equilibrium rather than fixed in advance by Aω.

One further step maps this composite efficiency into a multiplier on output. Production per

Remark 1 (Unit pass-through is degenerate). The lower curvature bound ϱ > −∞is not a technical
convenience; at the excluded pole the equilibrium is degenerate. Because ef (ω)
sj
is linear in h and the
weighted power mean is positively homogeneous of degree one, eκ(ω)(ch) = ceκ(ω)(h) identically. At

admits, with any solution h∗, every scaled copy ch∗, c ∈[0,1]: a continuum of equilibria in which

closure reaching the whole network, collapse as the only limit. Every maintained ϱ repairs this by
the same mechanism that makes benchmark propagation dissipative: along the uniform ray the
map becomes c 7→Gj(c), strictly above the diagonal on (0,1) with local gain eej(c) < 1, so the non-
intermediate share damps the delivery loop and the benchmark is the unique stable ray level. The
anchor is state-dependent: eej rises toward one as bundles degrade, so the same complementarity
that amplifies deep shocks thins the margin by which the loop is damped; the cushion condition
of Proposition 3 below is the quantitative residue of that tension. See Bhattathiripad and Veetil
(2026) for the boundary degeneracy made precise.
2.4
Capacity, fulfillment, and the post-disruption equilibrium
The counterfactual scenarios we study are short run. We impose the single restriction that no
country-sector’s activity exceed its benchmark level, and assume nothing else about the adjustment.
The interpretation is that capacity, logistics, labor, shipping, and supplier relationships take longer
than the disruption horizon to expand, so a producer cannot scale up to exploit the demand that
a closure diverts toward it. Nor is the autonomous-demand component fi a protected floor on
activity, since a producer whose realized input bundle has been degraded by the disruption loses
the ability to produce for non-intermediate uses along with the ability to produce for intermediate
buyers. The productivity penalty eλ(ω)
i
of (12) therefore applies uniformly to the supplier’s overall
output stream, and the admissible activity vector lives in the box [0,x] rather than [f,x].
19


## Page 20


We impose capacity where it acts economically: in the targets of the balancing, and only there.
The row targets (6) restore every seller to at most its benchmark sales flow, so the rationing of an
over-subscribed supplier is performed by the balancing itself; the supplier-side scalings of (8) are
exactly the proportional rationing multipliers that spread a severed seller’s recoverable output
across its retained customers. No rationing operator appears at the equilibrium stage, and no
projection onto the box is imposed: the ceiling of the short run is spent once, at the re-matching
stage, and is inherited by everything downstream.

Two readings of the lemma deserve emphasis. First, the capacity content sits in the targets,
not in the balancing algorithm. Biproportional balancing is margin-agnostic: in its classical table-
updating use the prescribed margins routinely exceed those of the reference matrix (Stone and
Brown, 1962; Bacharach, 1970), and fed a row target above ¯di it would balance to it without
complaint. The ceiling of the short run is the modeling decision, made in (5)–(6), to restore at most
benchmark margins, cut by the friction. The parameters set the depth of the cut below the ceiling,
never its sign, so (15) holds at every (τ,δ) in the maintained ranges, on any benchmark network.
Second, the cap constrains totals, not links: individual flows can and do rise above benchmark
under a closure, which is exactly the trade-redirection channel that Lemma 4 below exploits.9

The cushion condition is where the economics of the outer curvature surfaces in the theory. It
asks that the bundle degradation the re-matching leaves behind at full fulfillment not be so deep
that the delivery loop’s gain, at the shadow share eei rather than the benchmark cost share, can
sustain a self-fulfilling spiral. The threshold (1 −βi)−1/ϱ rises from zero toward one as ϱ falls from
the constant-share anchor toward the degenerate pole, recovering the unconditional interiority of

scenario from Aω alone; the solver reports violations ex ante, and every computed equilibrium is
verified interior ex post (Online Appendix OA.3). Uniqueness needs none of this.

3
Mapping Chokepoints onto Trade Flows
A chokepoint enters the model only through the benchmark flows whose routes cross it, which
means that the empirical content of a closure is the set of country-sector links it severs. The task
of this section is to build that set. The object we construct is the chokepoint incidence matrix Γ ,
which records, for every chokepoint and every bilateral intermediate flow in Z∗, whether the flow
physically transits the passage. The construction proceeds from geography to trade. We first fix
the set of passages that qualify as chokepoints, then restrict attention to the sectors whose output
actually travels by sea, and finally assign every bilateral flow to the chokepoints its least-cost route
crosses. Throughout this section and the rest of the paper we work with the extended economy set
C∗= {1,...,G∗}, G∗= 87. The extension is forced by the data. The OECD Inter-Country Input-Output
table, the source of the benchmark flows, individually identifies 80 economies and pools all others
into a single Rest-of-World composite. And the composite conceals exactly the economies whose
trade is most chokepoint-dependent: the Gulf states apart from Saudi Arabia and the UAE (Qatar,
10LW and λc are gross value-added losses, not welfare. A welfare metric would credit the consumption value of
the expenditure switching, inventory rundown, and price adjustment that the short-run quantity model deliberately
excludes, and would weigh the distributional incidence the proportional losses only describe. On both counts the
value-added loss is the natural upper-envelope companion to the unbuffered reading we maintain throughout, and we
make no welfare claims beyond it.
26


## Page 27


Kuwait, Oman, Bahrain, Iran), the Red Sea rim, and much of Africa. We resolve them by combining
two datasets. The OECD table supplies the levels, and the GTAP 10 database, whose 141 regions
identify these economies individually, supplies the bilateral shares by which each Rest-of-World
cell is split into seven maritime-relevant blocks.11 Gulf-state exporters (OtherGulf), North African
LNG suppliers (OtherNorthAfrica), and East African transit economies (OtherEastAfrica) thus
appear as distinct rows and columns of Z∗rather than disappearing into an undifferentiated rest of
the world.
3.1
Chokepoints versus detour routes
Not every narrow passage is a chokepoint. A maritime passage enters K as a chokepoint only if
it carries positive benchmark trade mass for which no within-route alternative exists. Formally,
passage k is a chokepoint when there is a non-trivial set of country pairs (c,c′) such that k lies on
the least-cost route p∗(c,c′) defined in equation (35), and every alternative route from c to c′ that
avoids k carries a strictly positive detour cost. The reroutability entry ek,cc′ of Section 2.1 is then
strictly positive on that set. A passage that lies on no least-cost route for any significant flow in
Z∗is instead a detour route. That such a passage carries no benchmark mass is not an empirical
finding but a consequence of the routing rule of Section 3.3. Every benchmark flow travels its
least-cost path, and a detour is by definition the dominated alternative, the route through the
primary passage being shorter on every corridor the detour could serve. This is why the assignment
places no flow on a detour while the primary passage is open.12 Economically, a detour route is an
outside option rather than a conduit. Its capacity is slack at the benchmark, its value is contingent
on the closure of its paired chokepoint, and it accordingly enters the model not as a column of
Γ but through the detour distance ∆k that prices the reroutability ek,cc′ (Online Appendix OA.2).
Detour routes therefore do not themselves enter K in the single-closure exercise, but they matter
for joint closures, where chokepoints that share a detour form an equivalence class and pay for the
detour only once.
The distinction between chokepoints and detours also separates the model’s two substitution
margins. The within-sector reallocation of Definition 1, which reconstitutes the recoverable share
(1−τ)ℓsj of displaced demand as orders on surviving suppliers, operates across chokepoints. A buyer
who loses an input flow through one closed passage re-sources from same-sector suppliers whose
own routes transit different passages. The reroutability ek,c(i)c(j) operates within a chokepoint’s
route set: it grades how much of a given supplier’s shipment can be salvaged by a longer voyage
11Levels are anchored at the ICIO reference year 2019, and because GTAP contributes only the within-composite
shares, re-aggregating the seven blocks recovers the OECD table exactly. Online Appendix OA.1 sets out the construction,
the sector concordance, and the consistency checks.
12The assignment idealizes the data rather than counting ships. The small normal-times traffic that does use these
passages, deep-draft tankers exceeding Malacca’s limit taking Lombok, for instance, is folded into the primary corridor’s
flows.
27


## Page 28


around the closed passage on the same buyer–seller route. The chokepoint set K is thus the set of
geographic objects against which τ acts, and the detour-route set is the set against which ek,cc′ acts.
Substitution toward new suppliers reachable by unaffected routes is enabled, not foreclosed, by
this distinction. A Japanese refiner that loses Saudi crude through Hormuz reallocates demand
to Russian ESPO, US Light Sweet, Brazilian, and West African crude, all of which reach Japan
by routes that bypass Hormuz. That substitution is the reallocation margin of Definition 1 and
recovers (1 −τ) of the displaced mass. What the buyer cannot do, for a given supplier–buyer pair,
is send that specific supplier’s shipment by a different physical route around the closed passage.
That decision belongs to the supplier’s vessel and is captured by ek,c(i)c(j).
Applied to the extended matrix, the rule yields K = 12 chokepoints and six detour routes. Four
of the twelve are terminal gates, the sole sea exits of enclosed basins: the Strait of Hormuz for the
Persian Gulf, the Turkish Straits for the Black Sea, the Danish Straits (including the Øresund) for
the Baltic, and the Kerch Strait for the Sea of Azov. The remaining eight lie on through-routes with
a longer way around. On the Asia–Europe corridor these are the Suez Canal, the Bab-el-Mandeb /
Gulf of Aden passage, and the Strait of Gibraltar. On the Pacific arc they are the Malacca–Singapore
corridor and the Taiwan–Luzon Corridor (the Taiwan and Luzon passages carry the same East-Asia
outbound flows at the regional resolution of Z∗and are pooled). The Panama Canal, the Strait
of Dover, and the Korea Strait complete the eight. The six detour routes are the Cape of Good
Hope (for Suez, Bab-el-Mandeb, and Gibraltar), Cape Horn (for Panama), the Lombok and Sunda
Straits (for Malacca–Singapore), and the Mozambique Channel (overflow for the Cape detour). The
Arctic Northern Sea Route together with the Bering Strait, the emerging Asia–Europe and Pacific
alternatives, completes the set.

The restriction applies to the seller’s sector only, and the asymmetry is deliberate: it is the
shipped object, the seller’s output, that transits the strait. Whether a tanker of Gulf LNG passes
Hormuz does not depend on whether its cargo is bought by a refinery (C19, a goods sector) or by a
power utility (D, a service-classified sector), because the buyer’s ISIC classification is irrelevant at
the water. A closure therefore severs every seaborne-goods link on an exposed route, whoever the
buyer is: crude into refining, LNG into power generation, fuel into transport services, materials
into construction.

3.3
Geographic routing rules
Routing, we emphasize, is determined by physical geography rather than estimated from data.
Let P(c,c′) denote the set of maritime routes from the primary export port of economy c to the
primary import port of economy c′, and let p∗(c,c′) ∈P(c,c′) be the least-cost route minimizing
sailing distance and transit time. Let Gk ⊂R2 be the geographic boundary of chokepoint k. The
routing indicator
rk(c,c′) = 1p∗(c,c′) ∩Gk , ∅
(35)
equals one exactly when the least-cost route from c to c′ crosses the passage. Two disciplines keep
the assignment conservative. First, all ships follow the globally least-cost route in the undisrupted
baseline, with no precautionary rerouting prior to disruption. Second, for energy commodities
(B06, C19) and bulk goods (B07, B08, C24A, C24B, C10T12) a unique primary route exists, so rk(c,c′)
is deterministic. For manufactured goods with multiple potential ports (C26, C29), by contrast,
we assign rk(c,c′) = 1 only when the majority of bilateral trade value uses the route crossing
chokepoint k, based on shipping-lane data and port geography. Table 2 records the resulting
assignments for the most economically significant economy-pair groups.

largest single bilateral intermediate flow in the dataset, travel from Keelung through the Taiwan

many such assignments possible at all. Kuwaiti crude to China now appears as z∗
OtherGulf B06,CHN C19
rather than dissolving into an undifferentiated ROW row that could not be cleanly assigned, and
the same holds for Qatari LNG bound for Europe and for East African flows through the Indian
Ocean.
A single flow may transit several chokepoints, and on the long corridors this is the norm
rather than the exception. Saudi crude bound for Germany, z∗
SAU B06,DEU C19, crosses Hormuz, Bab-el-
Mandeb, and Suez on one voyage, so γHormuz,m = γBab,m = γSuez,m = 1. And East Asian electronics
bound for Northwest Europe stack the Taiwan Strait, Malacca, Bab-el-Mandeb, Suez, and Gibraltar
the same way (Table OA.2.3). The rows of Γ therefore overlap, with the same flow m appearing
in the incidence row of every gate on its route, and the overlap has two consequences. The first
is an accounting caution: per-chokepoint exposure tallies cannot be summed, because a flow at
risk at three gates is one flow at risk, not three. Suez and Bab-el-Mandeb are the limiting case.
At the region resolution of the routing, every flow that transits one transits the other, so their
incidence rows coincide and the two produce identical single-closure losses in Section 4. The
second consequence concerns compound scenarios: gates in series on one route are alternative
occasions for the same loss, not independent hazards, for a shipment severed at Hormuz cannot
be severed again at Suez. Closing any two of these passages therefore disrupts the flow only once,
which is what the union form of the disrupted fraction in equation (2) enforces. And Section 5.1
31


## Page 32


tightens the union further for gates that share a physical detour, as Bab-el-Mandeb and Suez share
the Cape of Good Hope.
3.5
The chokepoint incidence matrix
Collecting the assignments yields the chokepoint incidence matrix
Γ ∈{0,1}K×M,

and (7). The matrix inherits a natural G∗× G∗block structure, with Γ (c,c′) ∈{0,1}K×N 2
M collecting the
incidences between economy pair (c,c′). A block is identically zero whenever no route between the
pair crosses any chokepoint (Germany to France, for instance, for the energy gates).
The matrix that results is highly sparse. Its density,
P
k,m γk,m
KM
,
where K = 12 is the number of chokepoints and M the number of non-zero seaborne bilateral
flows, is roughly 2–5%, and the sparsity is informative rather than incidental. Most intermediate
trade flows pass through no particular chokepoint, so a closure disrupts a small but economically
consequential fraction of the world’s production-network links. Which fraction is disrupted, and
how consequential it proves to be, is what the rest of the paper computes.
3.6
Partial closures
What does a “closed” strait actually look like? Not an empty one. When war-risk premia drove
the container trade from the Red Sea in 2023–24, the lines, answerable to schedule reliability and
corporate insurers, withdrew almost at once, and box transits fell by roughly 90% at the trough.
The tankers stayed: self-insured or war-risk-priced tonnage kept nearly 1/2 of the corridor’s oil
moving under active attack. The same split recurs across the historical record: through the Tanker
War of the 1980s, Gulf oil kept flowing despite some 450 ship attacks (Online Appendix OA.5.1). A
closure, in short, is a matter of degree, and the degree differs systematically by what is shipped:
boxes flee, tankers persist.
The model encodes this fact in the leakage of Assumption 1. Each closed link retains the
share δs of its benchmark flow, indexed by the shipped commodity. The baseline analysis sets
32


## Page 33


the two values the historical record identifies (Online Appendix OA.5.1). These are δs = 0.10 for
containerized and general cargo, the trough residual of the Red Sea episode, and δs = 0.45 for the
energy commodities B05, B06, and C19, the persistence the tanker record shows even under attack.
Closure intensity and geographic exposure thus enter the model separately, and the division of
labor is worth stating. The reroutability entries ek,cc′ grade how much of each corridor’s flow is
exposed to the passage in the first place, a fact of geography, while the leakage δs grades how
hard the closed passage bites on the flow so exposed, a fact of enforcement. Severity is monotone
in the leakage: a more determined enforcement is a smaller δs, and a leakier closure disrupts
less. A strictly positive δs is also what keeps the two-sided balancing and the fixed point well
posed (Proposition 1). Online Appendix OA.4 reports world and country losses across the leakage
variants, including the commodity-differentiated reading with δenergy = 0.45 on the energy sectors,
and the uniform baseline δ ≡0.10 is held fixed throughout the empirical analysis.
4
Empirical Findings
We now take the model to the data, closing each of the twelve chokepoints in turn on the extended
intermediate-flow matrix Z∗under the incidence specification of Section 3. The central empirical
object is the master exposure matrix Λ = [λc({k})]c∈C∗,k∈K of value-added losses, the GDP analogue of
equation (33). We report Λ rather than the gross-activity matrix Φ as the main measure because λc
is the welfare-relevant proportional decline in country c’s GDP and is comparable across economies.
The gross-activity matrix Φ is reported in parallel for sectoral and validation purposes.
Three findings organize the section. First, the cross-country distribution of exposure is heavy-
tailed: a closed Hormuz costs the world about 0.6% of value added, the United Arab Emirates 17%
of its GDP and Saudi Arabia 10%, while most economies lose well under 1%. Second, supplier-side
economies lose several times more, in proportional terms, than the buyers at the other end of the
same corridor; the gap is about 17-fold at Hormuz. Third, the four-channel decomposition shows
that the two sides of a chokepoint lose for structurally different reasons. Exporters lose because
their market is physically severed, importers because the input bundles they assemble become less
productive once the severed flows go missing. We take these in turn, after fixing the calibration.
4.1
Calibration
The model has four structural parameters beyond the data and the reroutability profile: the
reallocation friction τ ∈[0,1], the inner CES substitution parameter ρ ∈[ρ,ρ] ⊂(−∞,0), the outer-
nest curvature ϱ ∈[ϱ,ϱ] ⊂(−∞,0), and the closure leakage δ of Assumption 1. The fulfillment-factor
specification of Section 2.4 introduces a nonlinear feedback in h through the realized retention
ratios. And the delivery-loop rate condition tightens as ρ becomes more negative, because the
33


## Page 34


CES Lipschitz factor diverges as realized retention falls. We estimate them from evidence external
to the chokepoint problem in Online Appendix OA.5. The leakage is estimated, commodity by
commodity, from the transit record of eleven historical maritime-disruption episodes (Online
Appendix OA.5.1). The substitution elasticity comes from the structural production-function
literature (Online Appendix OA.5.2), and the reallocation friction from the firm-level supply-chain
recovery-rate literature (Online Appendix OA.5.3). The baseline calibration is the center of mass of
those independently estimated intervals. The rest of this subsection summarizes the evidence and
fixes the calibration carried through the rest of the paper.
We adopt the baseline calibration (τ,ρ,ϱ) = (0.3,−1,−1) with a uniform closure leakage δ = 0.10
on all seaborne goods (Section 3.6); the commodity-differentiated reading, with δenergy = 0.45 on the
energy sectors B05/B06/C19 and δ = 0.10 elsewhere, is reported as a robustness exercise in Online
Appendix OA.4. We read the resulting Λ as the unbuffered steady-state exposure of the production
network to a complete, sustained closure of each chokepoint. The model has no inventory state,
no strategic-reserve drawdown, no bypass-pipeline mobilization, and no price-induced demand
substitution. Every period of disruption therefore translates one-for-one into output loss, and the
reported λc({k}) is the upper envelope of the cost that the topology of supply makes possible before
any real-world buffer kicks in.13
Table 3 summarizes the calibration and the evidence behind it, parameter by parameter;
Online Appendix OA.5 details the sources and the mapping into the model. The friction τ = 0.3
means that, at the moment a sourcing link is severed, 70% of the displaced same-sector input
demand is reconstituted as orders on surviving suppliers, and 30% is not recovered. The natural-
experiment evidence on short-run supply-chain recovery, from the firms cut off by the 2011 T¯ohoku
earthquake to the pandemic-era disruptions, places the one-year unrecovered share at 30–40% of
pre-disruption input flows. The cross-disruption centroid is near 0.35, and the baseline sits at the
more-recoverable edge of that band. The inner CES exponent ρ = −1 sets the complementarity
across the sectoral input bundle, with ρ = 0 the Cobb–Douglas boundary and more negative
values penalizing a missing input more severely. The outer-nest curvature ϱ = −1 plays the same
role between the intermediate composite and the non-intermediate block, so both nests carry an
elasticity of substitution σ = 1/(1 −ρ) = 0.5. The structural production-function estimates place
the firm-level short-run exponent far lower, at ρ ≲−4, and the network-macro literature works
with ρ ∈[−4,−1] as its short-run range. We adopt the upper edge of that interval, for two reasons
set out in Online Appendix OA.5.2. First, the exponent is read at country-sector rather than firm
13The buffers are substantial. Strategic-reserve coverage alone runs to 90–110 days of net-import use for OECD
oil-importers under the IEA’s stock-holding obligations (International Energy Agency, 2024; U.S. Energy Information
Administration, 2024b). And the Saudi-Arabian East–West and ADNOC Habshan–Fujairah pipelines together bypass
roughly 1/4 to 1/3 of normal Hormuz transit. A buffered short-run reading would deflate the effective τ for the crude-oil
sector by roughly 0.7 for plausible 90-day shocks. The figures below are not deflated, and they should be read as the
ceiling of unbuffered exposure rather than as a point prediction of short-run impact.
34


## Page 35


granularity, and aggregation across products and producers moves it toward zero relative to the
micro estimates. Second, the unbuffered-steady-state reading we maintain requires the substitution
channel to operate freely once an input is reconstituted. A more negative ρ would amplify the CES
penalty even on benchmark bundles, though the non-intermediate cushion of the outer nest keeps
the equilibrium finite and differentiated across the admissible range.

The empirical patterns of cross-country exposure reported below are robust to (τ,ρ) choices
throughout the rectangle τ ∈[0.2,0.5], ρ ∈[−1,−0.25], the union of the empirical intervals of
Table 3. Only the level of world value-added loss shifts, while the ranking of chokepoints and the
geographic pattern of country exposure are invariant, and Online Appendix OA.4.5 audits the
four main findings across the rectangle and finds each preserved at every corner. Under the two-
nest rule the model returns a finite, differentiated equilibrium throughout: the non-intermediate
35

cushion damps the delivery loop below unit gain, so no single or joint closure tips into a saturating
cascade within the empirically admissible range. The numerical stability of the damped-Picard
iteration across the full sweep ρ ∈{−1,−2,−3,−5,−10} is documented in Online Appendix OA.3.
And the corners adjacent to the baseline, (τ,ρ) = (0.2,−1) and (0.4,−1), are reported in Online
Appendix OA.4 as sensitivity checks.
The fulfillment-factor specification is what propagates the shock through the supply chain. A
producer whose own input bundle has been contracted by the closure delivers a proportionally
smaller fraction of its orders, which in turn reduces realized retention at its downstream buyers,
and so on down the chain. Bundle-efficiency loss acts on the supplier’s deliveries through the
output multiplier, H(ω)

), and the cascade across the network is carried by the
equilibrium fulfillment vector eh, at a gain the shadow intermediate share caps below one.
4.2
The master exposure matrix
Three features of Λ stand out once its 87 × 12 entries are ranked by total exposure P
k λc({k}).
First, the top rows are dominated by ROW-block aggregates and Gulf-state exporters whose entire
seaboard sits on or immediately behind a chokepoint: OtherCentralAsia, OtherEastAfrica, Oth-
erGulf, OtherNorthAfrica, OtherRestOfWorld, ARE, OtherWestAfrica, OtherSouthAsia. Second,
the dominant cells of the matrix concentrate in four of the twelve columns, namely Hormuz, the
Turkish Straits, Suez, and Bab-el-Mandeb. Third, the terminal chokepoints (Hormuz, Danish,
Turkish, Kerch) cut deepest into their adjacent economies, while the bypass chokepoints (Suez,
BAM/Aden, Gibraltar, Malacca–Singapore, Panama, Taiwan–Luzon, Dover, Korea) generate broader
but shallower exposure. Large exposure is rare. Fewer than one in ten of the 1,044 matrix entries
exceed 1% of GDP, and cells exceeding 10% are confined to a handful of country–chokepoint pairs,
mostly Gulf–Hormuz, Caspian–Turkish, and Baltic–Danish.
Table 4 reports the world value-added loss under each single closure, together with the top-five
contributing economies as shares of that loss. Hormuz dominates at about 0.6% of world GDP:
though a passage twenty-one nautical miles wide at its narrowest point carries 1/20 of the world’s
value added in its keeping, rerouting and re-sourcing hold the loss from closing it well below the
trade at stake. The Turkish Straits follow at about 0.4%. Gibraltar (about 0.4%), the Danish Straits
(about 0.4%), and the Suez Canal and Bab-el-Mandeb / Gulf of Aden (about 0.3% each) cluster just
below, in a 0.3–0.4% band. The Pacific-basin closures (Panama, Malacca–Singapore, Taiwan–Luzon)
sit at 0.2% or below because their reroutability is high. The Lombok–Sunda and north-of-Japan
detours add small distances relative to the benchmark voyage. Malacca–Singapore, the Taiwan–
Luzon Corridor, Korea, Kerch, and Dover all sit at or below 0.1%. Each carries benchmark trade
mass concentrated on a small country group, so their world-aggregate losses are modest even
though their country-level signatures, mapped below, are distinct. The dominance of Hormuz
36


## Page 37


has a direct economic origin, which is worth spelling out. Gulf exporters of crude oil, refined
products, and petrochemical feedstocks supply refining, chemicals, and metals worldwide. A
Hormuz closure removes that supply on routes for which within-sector substitutes are scarce in
the short run, and the bundle-efficiency penalty at downstream buyers cascades through the world
refining and petrochemical chain.

Figure 2: Country-level GDP loss λc({k}) on a world map for each of the twelve single-chokepoint

The heatmap sorts economies into three tiers. At the insulated end, the United States is the
least exposed major economy. Its largest single-chokepoint loss is about 0.2% of GDP (the Panama
Canal), and every other closure costs it materially less. A large, sectorally diversified economy, self-
sufficient in primary energy since the shale revolution and served by rail and pipeline alternatives,
carries little intermediate-input mass through K, and the τ = 0.3 reallocation recovers most of what
it does. Its residual loss is almost entirely second-order propagation (Figure 8).
Western Europe and Russia sit in the middle, insulated by overland infrastructure, the Nor-
wegian, North African, and Russian pipelines, dense rail and road freight, and the Rhine–Main
system, which absorbs flows that would otherwise transit a chokepoint. Germany, the United
Kingdom, France, and Switzerland stay at or under about 1.2% under every closure, each peaking
on Gibraltar: Germany at about 1.2%, France and Switzerland at about 1, and the United Kingdom
at about 0.5. The Netherlands, meanwhile, reaches about 1% on Gibraltar through Rotterdam’s
transhipment role. Italy and Spain are the exceptions. Gibraltar costs them about 2% each, because
their Mediterranean trade has no inland substitute on the short-run horizon. Russia stays in low
single digits on every closure, peaking at about 2.6% on the Danish Straits through its Baltic-port
exports at Primorsk and Ust-Luga. Pipelines (Druzhba, Power of Siberia, ESPO, CPC) and Arctic
and Far-East ports carry most of its trade off the chokepoint set. The named economies most
exposed are instead the Baltic and Black-Sea rims. Poland loses 10% on the Danish Straits, its
38


## Page 39


coal-and-fuel imports and seaborne materials feeding power generation and construction, with
Estonia (7), Lithuania (6), Finland (6), and Sweden (6) behind it. Ukraine (9), Bulgaria (9), Turkey
(7), and Romania (7) are the Turkish-Straits counterparts.
Africa anchors the exposed end together with the Gulf. The African ROW aggregates lose
up to about 3% of GDP under their binding closures, and several individual economies sit close
behind (Tunisia and the Democratic Republic of Congo about 2%, South Africa about 1% and
Egypt about 0.5%, each under its binding closure). The numerical exercise adds what geography
alone does not, for African exposure is the purest case of the exporter-side geometry behind the
asymmetry of Section 4.6. On the export side, an African economy typically sells one or two
primary commodities, crude from the Gulf of Guinea, copper and cobalt from the Congo basin,
ores and agricultural staples elsewhere, to a diversified world market through a single corridor.
When that corridor closes, the buyers at the far end re-source within sector with comparative ease,
since other suppliers of crude or copper abound off the corridor. The African seller, by contrast,
finds few off-corridor same-sector buyers onto which the balancing can push its displaced output,
and the friction writes the remainder off. On the import side the same concentration appears in
mirror image. The fuel, chemicals, machinery, and processed food that African downstream sectors
use as inputs arrive from a handful of suppliers along the same few corridors. The within-sector
reallocation that recovers 70% of displaced trade for diversified economies therefore recovers far
less here. And the bundle-efficiency factor eκ(ω)
i
collapses for producers whose remaining suppliers
cannot match the lost input mix. Caught on both sides of its trade, the continent’s losses stack
across several closures at once rather than concentrating on one gate.14 Figure 9 below quantifies
the direct share by economy and closure.
4.3
Country-level exposure geography
Each closure also draws its own map, and five regional structures emerge in the country-level
exposure painted in Figure 2. The Strait of Hormuz panel shows a sharp Persian Gulf concentration,
with ARE at 17%, the OtherGulf block covering Iran, Iraq, Kuwait, Qatar, Oman and Bahrain at
11%, and SAU at 10%. A thinner secondary tail runs through East and West Africa (OtherEastAfrica
and OtherWestAfrica at about 3% each), which import refined products, fuels, capital goods, and
chemicals on the same Indian-Ocean routes. The Turkish Straits panel concentrates in the Caspian
and Black Sea rim, led by OtherCentralAsia at 13%, with Ukraine and Bulgaria at about 9% and
14The concentration the model reads off Z∗has an independent counterpart in the trade statistics: UNCTAD classifies
more than 4/5 of African economies as commodity-dependent, with over 60% of merchandise-export revenue from
primary commodities (United Nations Conference on Trade and Development, 2023). And most of the continent’s
long-haul trade moves on a small number of liner services that funnel through the Suez–Bab-el-Mandeb corridor toward
Europe and through Indian-Ocean hubs toward Asia. Nothing in the model is Africa-specific: the exposure is the
bipartite geometry of concentrated supply meeting diversified demand, evaluated at the African rows and columns of
Z∗.
39


## Page 40


Turkey and Romania at about 7%. The Danish Straits panel paints the Baltic littoral, from Poland
at 10% through Estonia (7), Lithuania (6), Finland (6), and Sweden (6). Suez and Bab-el-Mandeb
produce identical patterns, since the two chokepoints sit on the same Asia–Europe corridor and
share the Cape of Good Hope as their detour, with broad but moderately deep effects on North
Africa, the Caspian rim, East Africa, and South Asia. The Panama Canal panel, finally, produces a
diffuse pattern, with the Pacific facade of Latin America (Chile, Peru) as its named-economy cases.
The five patterns share a grammar, and it is the classification of Section 3.1. Each closure’s map
has a depth, how much the worst-hit economies lose, and a breadth, how many economies register
a visible loss, and the terminal–bypass distinction pins both. A terminal gate carries e = 1 on its
whole support: everything trapped behind it is lost up to the leakage. Its panel is therefore deep
and narrow, with double-digit losses on the enclosed basin’s rim and little beyond it, the buyers
at the far end re-sourcing within sector and escaping. A bypassable gate with a short detour is
the opposite corner. At the Taiwan–Luzon Corridor or Malacca the detour adds a few hundred to
a thousand nautical miles to voyages an order of magnitude longer. So e is small on the routes
that carry most of the exposed mass, and even a fully exposed flow loses only a sliver of its value.
And because a large share of the world’s long-haul routes touch these passages, the panel is broad
and shallow, many economies losing a little and none losing much. The intermediate gates, Suez,
Bab-el-Mandeb, and Gibraltar on the Cape detour and Panama on Cape Horn, sit between the
corners, with e between 1/5 and 2/3, and their panels paint the corridor itself at moderate depth.
The two dimensions respond to different geographic data, depth to the detour distance ∆k through
the reroutability rule and breadth to the number of economies whose least-cost routes cross the
gate, and neither implies the other. Kerch is the clarifying case: a terminal gate on a tiny basin,
it sits in the bottom band of Table 4 by world loss, yet what it costs falls almost entirely on the
Azov rim. Size and shape are different properties, the one scaling with the trade mass at stake,
the other readable from the detour map before the model is solved. What the equilibrium adds to
this geographic skeleton is the escape of the off-corridor world through within-sector re-matching,
and the capture of distant, exposed buyers through the complementarity of their inputs. This is
how Japan, an economy nowhere near the Gulf, comes to carry a visible cell on the Hormuz panel
despite shipping nothing through the strait.

The two panels say different things, and the difference is itself a finding. At the country level
(panel (a)) the rank-size curve is concave throughout. No single power law fits the cross-section:
the middle ranks carry a local coefficient of ˆζ = 1.0 (s.e. 0.2, R2 = 0.96) over ranks 5–40. And the
four worst-hit economies detach below the fitted line, losing more than even a power law drawn
15The seven constructed Other* blocks are excluded from the figure and the estimates. Each pools many small
economies, so its position in the size distribution is an artifact of our aggregation rather than a measurement of any
economy’s exposure. Restoring the blocks barely moves the coefficients: 1.1 (0.3) at the country level and 1.2 (0.05) in
