## Page 42


through the middle of the distribution would predict. With 80 observations these estimates are
descriptive, not inferential, and we do not read ˆζ < 2 as proof of an infinite second moment. What
the cross-section does establish is the weaker, and economically operative, statement. Aggregate
chokepoint exposure is concentrated in a small number of catastrophically exposed economies, with
a substantial second tier of ROW blocks and Eastern European economies also bearing double-digit
losses.
At the country-sector level (panel (b)) the distribution acquires what the country cross-section
is too small to show, namely a genuine scaling window. Over ranks 100 through 1,500, losses
between 3 and 23% of node activity, the rank-size relation is log-linear with ˆζ = 1.2 (s.e. 0.05)
and R2 = 0.97: a genuine Pareto segment, with a tail exponent close to Zipf’s unit benchmark and
well on the infinite-variance side of two. The window coefficient is robust in the directions that
matter. The Hormuz closure alone, rather than the worst-case envelope, returns 0.9 (0.03) with
R2 = 0.99, so the window is a property of each closure and not an artifact of the max operator. And
the estimate does not depend on where the line is drawn: a log-binned CCDF fit over the same loss
range returns 1.3 (0.07). Above the window the curve steepens, and the steepening is mechanical
rather than informative. The loss λ is bounded by one, the worst nodes (Gulf mining and refining
under a Hormuz closure) already stand at 0.48, and a bounded variable cannot carry a power law
into its endpoint. Estimates confined to the extreme top of the node distribution therefore overstate
the thinness of the tail, which is why we anchor the tail statement on the window rather than on
a threshold-based tail index. That the country coefficient sits below the node coefficient is the
direction that aggregation of positively dependent node losses within countries would produce,
though at 80 observations we do not press the comparison.
One composition caveat disciplines the reading. Suez and Bab-el-Mandeb are identical columns
of Λ by construction, since they sit on the same corridor at the region resolution of the routing, so
cross-chokepoint statistics built on the twelve columns double-weight one corridor. The worst-case
exposure is invariant to a duplicated column, so the rank-size estimates are unaffected, and the
mean-based statistics below are computed on the eleven distinct corridors. A likelihood comparison
of Pareto against lognormal at the country cross-section does not separate the two at this sample
size, and the node-level window, though well fit, spans only half a decade of loss. The honest
statement of the evidence is accordingly this: the data identify a deep, consequential upper tail,
with a node-level scaling window that is genuinely Pareto, though nearer the infinite-variance
boundary than Zipf’s law, and a country cross-section that is heavy-tailed without being power-law.
The heavy-tail finding has a structural origin worth stating carefully, because it is a composition
of two concentrations, not a generic fat-tail mechanism. A country’s worst-case exposure is, to
first order, the product of two shares. The first is the share of its activity funneled through its
most binding corridor, a property of the incidence matrix Γ and the reroutability. The second is
42


## Page 43


the inverse breadth of the within-sector substitutes that survive that corridor’s closure, a property
of Z∗’s bipartite geometry, governed by τ and the CES factor. Both shares are themselves highly
skewed across countries, corridor dependence because export geography concentrates a narrow
commodity basket on one sea lane, and substitute breadth because the same economies that depend
on one corridor tend also to source narrowly. And the two are positively associated, with the
Gulf, Caspian-rim, and African economies sitting high on both. Whether that positive association
deepens the tail is not automatic but depends on how the two shares combine. Because worst-case
exposure is, to first order, their product, the association compounds rather than averages out, and
in this cross-section the product is more skewed than either factor. The equilibrium then multiplies
the upper tail further through the CES feedback, which is convex precisely where retention is
lowest (equation (50)). The deep tail of the worst-case distribution is the cross-country image of
this composition. The next subsection exhibits the part of it that no rescaling of the mean profile
could reproduce.
4.5
Mean, tail, and concentration
The mean and the tail of a country’s exposure profile capture two different kinds of vulnerability.
The mean ¯λc is the average GDP loss under a randomly chosen single-chokepoint closure with
uniform prior over the eleven distinct corridors (Suez and Bab-el-Mandeb, identical columns by
construction, enter once). The tail λmax
c
is the worst-case loss. A country with a high mean but
a low tail-to-mean ratio is broadly exposed, with many chokepoints hurting it moderately. A
country with a high tail and a low mean has, instead, a single Achilles heel: one chokepoint hurts
it catastrophically while the others barely register.

4.6
Exporter–importer asymmetry
A chokepoint closure is a supplier-side shock: a set of intermediate-input flows is severed at the
passage. The buyers at the importing end are not directly disrupted. They lose part of their
input mass, retain the unaffected suppliers, and substitute through the buyer-side margin of the
two-sided balancing of Definition 1. The exporters at the supplying end lose foreign demand for
whatever output they ship through the closed passage. And although the balancing lets them
place part of that idle output on the buyers they can still reach, the reallocation friction and the
short-run capacity ceiling cap how much, so their gross activity in the fixed point of equation (18)
still contracts. With the CES bundle factor eKω(ρ) in place, the contraction is amplified by the
proportional input loss at each buyer’s column.
The existence of an asymmetry between the two sides of a chokepoint is therefore mechanical.
Its magnitude, however, is an empirical matter, and it is bounded, because a severed exporter is
not forced to absorb its entire lost sale. Under closure of the Strait of Hormuz, the three Persian
Gulf entries (ARE, SAU, OtherGulf) lose on average about 13% of GDP, while the four major Asian
importers (CHN, JPN, KOR, IND) lose on average about 1%, more than a tenfold asymmetry. For
the Turkish Straits, the Caspian and Black Sea rim economies on the supply side (OtherCentralAsia,
KAZ, UKR) lose an average of about 10% against the Mediterranean importers (ITA, GRC, ESP)
at about 0.9%, a roughly elevenfold asymmetry. For the Panama Canal, Pacific Latin American
exporters (CHL, PER) lose about 2.3% against East Asian importers (JPN, KOR, CHN) at 0.3%,
a roughly eightfold asymmetry. (These three ratios compare a corridor’s leading exporters with
46


## Page 47


its leading importers; the corridor-wide ratios in Figure 6 below, which weight every net-side
economy by its disrupted trade mass, are milder, 1.6× for Panama and 2.6× for the Turkish Straits.)
The cascade still propagates a non-trivial share of each closure’s loss to the importer side, so the
gap stays bounded. When Gulf suppliers cannot fulfill orders, Asian refiners face genuine input
shortages, and the resulting bundle penalty enters their own value-added loss alongside the larger
exporter-side loss.
Figure 6 reports, for all twelve closures, the disrupted-mass-weighted mean GDP loss of each
corridor’s net-exporter economies against that of its net-importer economies, with each economy
assigned to the side carrying its larger disrupted intermediate-trade mass. Supply-side loss exceeds
demand-side loss at all but one of the twelve corridors. Hormuz carries by far the largest supply-
side loss in level, its exporters losing about 12% of GDP on this basis against the importers’ under
1%, a loss ratio of about 17×. The most lopsided gates are those whose importers can bypass the
passage almost entirely: Kerch (about 150×) and the Korea Strait (about 30×). There the severed
exporters lose a few percent of GDP while their importers, free to detour around Japan or out
of the Azov, lose almost nothing, so the denominator nearly vanishes. Malacca (4.7×), Suez and
Bab-el-Mandeb (4.1×), and Dover (3.1×) sit in between, with the Turkish (2.6×) and Panama (1.6×)
corridors milder still. The asymmetry is smallest at the Danish Straits (1.4×) and Gibraltar (1.1×),
and it reverses at the Taiwan–Luzon Corridor (0.6×), the gates that enclose a diversified industrial
basin rather than a commodity-export region. The economies behind such a gate are at once its
principal exporters and its principal importers, and are caught on both sides at once. The lesson
we draw is that the asymmetry is a property of commodity-concentrated export geography rather
than of any single passage. Wherever a narrow export basket funnels through one corridor while
the buyers at the far end re-source within sector, the seller bears the multiple, and only where
the gate traps a diversified basin does the loss approach an even split. The map itself takes no
side: the balancing is neutral between its two margins (Lemma 2), so were the bipartite geometry
around a chokepoint symmetric, the corridor’s loss would split evenly. And transpose the geometry,
diversifying the sellers and concentrating the buyers, and the model would return the mirror
image, with importers bearing the larger loss. The gradient across Figure 6 is therefore read off the
geography of supply, not off the map that processes it.

## Page 48


Figure 6: Exporter–importer loss asymmetry, by chokepoint. For each single closure the bars give
the disrupted-mass-weighted mean proportional GDP loss of the corridor’s net-exporter economies
(supply side) and net-importer economies (demand side): every economy is assigned to the side on
which its net disrupted intermediate-trade mass falls and weighted by that mass, and the annotated
ratio is the supply-side mean divided by the demand-side mean. The asymmetry is positive at
all but one of the twelve corridors (it reverses at the Taiwan–Luzon Corridor, the gate enclosing a
diversified industrial basin) and smallest at Gibraltar and the Danish Straits. Baseline calibration
(τ,ρ,ϱ) = (0.3,−1,−1) with uniform leakage δ = 0.10.
The asymmetry is read off the two diagonal scalings of the balancing. A severed exporter’s
supplier-side scaling ui rises but cannot absorb the whole displaced sale, because ports, refineries,
loading terminals, and trade relationships are fixed at the disruption horizon. So the friction τ
writes off the unplaced remainder, and the autonomous-demand share f is itself attenuated by the
productivity factor eKω(eh). The buyers at the other end lean their scaling vj on unaffected suppliers
of the same input and absorb the residual shortfall at the bounded CES margin. The gap between
ui on the supply side and vj on the demand side is therefore the model’s structural measure of who
bears the loss. The rule it distills is blunt: when a strait closes, it is better to be the buyer.
It is worth being concrete about how a Gulf exporter “redirects” output when its only sea
exit is shut, since the phrase invites the objection that there is nowhere for the output to go. The
balancing never invents a trading partner, since it is support-preserving, so a severed supplier can
lean only on relationships already present in the benchmark. The reason the UAE is cushioned
is that the Strait of Hormuz accounts for a minority of its benchmark intermediate sales. The
rest is carried by intra-Gulf trade (which never leaves the basin), the ADNOC Habshan–Fujairah
pipeline to the Gulf of Oman, and other routes that do not transit the strait. The two-sided push
reconstitutes the recoverable part of the severed share onto those surviving routes. That the
48


## Page 49


recovery is genuine, rather than an artifact of the residual leak through the closed passage, can
be checked by tightening the leakage. Even at the uniform baseline δ = 0.10, with only a tenth of
a severed flow still transiting, the UAE’s Hormuz loss is about 17%, not the near-total loss a lost
sole export outlet would imply. The intra-Gulf, pipeline, and non-strait routes carry the rest, and
relaxing the leakage to the commodity reading δenergy = 0.45 lowers the loss only to about 13%.
The exporter, in other words, is cushioned by its clear routes and not by the leak through the closed
passage.17
This asymmetry is the structural source of the heavy-tail result of Section 4.4. A heavy tail
of cross-country exposure cannot arise if every chokepoint distributes its loss broadly across
many economies. Because each chokepoint’s incidence is concentrated on its supplier rim, each
chokepoint’s loss is borne by a small set of supplier economies, and the union over the twelve
chokepoints concentrates the loss on a set of economies that remains small relative to G∗= 87.
4.7
Decomposing the loss: severance, network-propagation, bundle
The four-channel identity (28) of Section 2.4 allows the country-level GDP loss λc under each
chokepoint closure to be apportioned to three economically distinct mechanisms. These are direct
severance of trade links by the closure, network propagation of the resulting demand reduction
through the post-disruption matrix Aω, and CES bundle-efficiency loss on both autonomous output
(ζ) and intermediate sales (ξ). There is no capacity-rationing channel: by Lemma 3 the physical
constraint is exhausted by the margin caps of the balancing and contributes no separate term at
the equilibrium stage.18
Figure 7 reports the decomposition for the fifteen most-exposed economies under closure of the
Strait of Hormuz at the baseline calibration. Two patterns dominate.
The two sides of the chokepoint absorb the loss through fundamentally different channels.
For the Persian Gulf trio (ARE, SAU, OtherGulf), direct severance is the single largest channel,
accounting for 38 to 53% of the country-level value-added loss. These three economies sit at the
supply end of the intermediate flows that physically transit Hormuz, so when the strait closes a
large part of their outbound sales is cut at the source. The two-sided balancing places what it can
of that idle output on the buyers they still reach, but the reallocation friction and the short-run
capacity ceiling cap how much, and the friction τ books the unplaced remainder as direct loss.
17Because the balancing constrains row and column totals but not individual cells, it can in principle lean harder on a
thin surviving link than a strict per-link physical capacity would permit. The exporter-side cushioning should therefore
be read as an upper bound on what the geography of surviving routes makes possible. The δ-insensitivity just reported
indicates the bound is not driven by the closed passage’s residual.
18The four-channel decomposition treats the receive-side demand D(ω)
i
(ex) that suppliers actually face after closure as
the reference level for the ζ,ξ split. The difference between D(ω)
i
and the benchmark demand Di(x) is absorbed into the
direct and propagation channels. Direct severance and propagation thus capture the network-channel loss, while ζ,ξ
capture the fulfillment-channel loss, and the four sum to the total per identity (28).

Network propagation through Aω carries a further 24 to 39%, and the CES bundle penalty the
rest: about a tenth of Saudi Arabia’s loss and a fifth of the UAE’s, rising to two-fifths for the
OtherGulf block, whose surviving suppliers least reproduce its benchmark input mix. For the
Asian energy importers (CHN, JPN, IND, KOR), the direct-severance component is essentially
zero, because these economies ship nothing through Hormuz and (A −Aω) is empty at their rows.
The two bundle-penalty channels ζ and ξ instead account for 23 to 51% of the country-level
value-added loss. The importers’ input bundles are degraded by the loss of Gulf intermediates,
their CES productivity factor eκi falls, and the reduction propagates to both autonomous output (ζ)
and intermediate sales (ξ), with network propagation contributing the remainder. The capacity
constraint appears in no column of the decomposition, and its absence is a theorem rather than
a finding. By Lemma 3 the order-book row cap and the delivery ceiling are the same benchmark
flow ¯di = P
j zij, so a supplier’s post-closure dollar demand never exceeds its capacity anywhere on
the state space. The economics is that a closure is a demand-destroying shock: the order-book cap
already holds every supplier’s realized demand at or below its benchmark intermediate sales. The
capacity constraint’s economically active appearance is thus in the margin caps of the balancing,
where it binds throughout and shapes the exporter-side losses documented above.

The Japanese case is worth working through in detail because it isolates the bundle-penalty
mechanism at scale. Japan loses about 0.5% of value added under closure of the Strait of Hormuz,
notable for an economy that ships nothing through the strait. The dark-blue direct-severance bar
at the left of Japan’s row in Figure 7 is essentially empty: Japan exports nothing that physically
transits Hormuz, so (A −Aω) has no Japanese rows. Network propagation accounts for about 47%
of Japan’s country-level loss, and the two bundle-penalty channels together for most of the rest.
Behind those numbers lies a single structural fact. Japan imports more than 85% of its primary
energy, and roughly 80% of its crude-oil imports come from Persian Gulf suppliers, Saudi Arabia,
the UAE, Qatar, and Kuwait, every barrel of which transits Hormuz (U.S. Energy Information
Administration, 2024a). There are no overland pipelines into Japan, and the country retains
no operating nuclear capacity at pre-Fukushima levels. And the rebalancing options that exist
over a multi-year horizon (LNG diversification, Russian Far-East crude, demand destruction) lie
outside the short-run window the model covers. In the extended ICIO matrix this dependence
shows up as a heavy weight on Gulf-supplied intermediates in Japan’s columns for refining (C19),
petrochemicals (C20A–C20C), basic metals (C24), and downstream manufacturing. When Hormuz
closes, the Gulf rows of Aω collapse, the realized retention ratios ef (ω)
rj (h) in those Japanese columns
fall sharply, and the CES productivity factor eκ(ω)
i
at Japanese refining and downstream suppliers
contracts accordingly. The contraction transmits to both their autonomous output (ζ) and their
intermediate sales (ξ), yielding the large purple and tan segments at the right of Japan’s bar. Korea
and India follow the same logic with comparable magnitudes (1.1 and 1.0% of GDP respectively)
because their import basket is somewhat more diversified across Gulf and non-Gulf origins, but
the channel decomposition is essentially identical.
Figure 8 reports, for each of the twenty largest economies by value added and each of the
twelve single-chokepoint closures, the share of network propagation in the country’s country-level
value-added loss. Dark cells indicate an economy whose loss is second-hand, in that the closure
52


## Page 53


neither severs the country’s outbound trade nor degrades its own input bundle, but its trading
partners contract and that contraction reaches the country through the input-output network.
Light cells indicate an economy whose loss arrives directly through severance or through bundle
degradation.
Figure 8: Network-propagation share of country-level value-added loss for the top-twenty
economies by value added, across the twelve single-chokepoint closures.
The figure sorts the top-twenty economies into two regimes: propagation-driven loss, in which
the closure touches neither the economy’s own trade nor its own inputs and the loss arrives second-
hand, and buyer-side bundle degradation, in which imperfect reallocation leaves the importer with
a less productive input mix. We take the two in turn.
In the propagation-driven regime, the closure neither severs the economy’s outbound trade nor
degrades its own input bundle, and the loss arrives only as its trading partners contract. The United
53


## Page 54


States sits here on every column (about 55 to 100%), softening most on Panama, where its Atlantic-
to-Pacific flows of refined products, gases, and bulk chemicals are imperfectly substitutable by
rail or the Cape Horn detour. Since the shale revolution, little US intermediate input transits any
listed strait. Russia is likewise dark everywhere except the Danish Straits (61%), because its oil
and gas leave by pipeline (Druzhba west, Power of Siberia and ESPO east) or through Arctic and
Far-East ports. Only the Baltic outlets at Primorsk and Ust-Luga run through a listed gate. Brazil,
Mexico, Canada, Indonesia, Switzerland, and the Netherlands belong to the same regime for the
same reason, bulk or Atlantic-basin trade that bypasses the chokepoint set. Each softens only on
the single closure that touches a genuine route (Mexico on Panama, Brazil on Malacca and Panama).
In the bundle-degradation regime, by contrast, the loss is first-hand: reallocation replaces the
severed input only imperfectly, and the importer’s own productivity falls. Japan is the leading
instance, at 47% network propagation on Hormuz. Japanese refiners are configured for Gulf crude
grades such as Arabian Light, Arabian Medium, Murban, and Kuwait Export Blend. And although
the (1 −τ) = 70% within-sector recovery shifts displaced demand toward Russian ESPO, US Light
Sweet, and Brazilian crude, the substitutes differ in sulfur, API gravity, and residue balance. The
reallocated volume therefore does not enter Japanese refining columns at the productivity of the
lost Gulf barrels. Korea (54% on Hormuz) is the same case at smaller scale, and India (52% on
Hormuz) adds a second rerouting around the Cape once its Russian-Urals corridor through the
Red Sea is also severed. Italy and Spain are the European analogues, reallocating Atlantic-basin
chemicals and refined products onto imperfect in-basin Mediterranean substitutes.
Saudi Arabia, at 53% direct severance and 35% network propagation on Hormuz, is the supplier-
side mirror of Japan: where the importer’s loss is almost all propagation and bundle degradation,
the exporter’s runs mostly through the direct severance of its own sales. When Hormuz closes, a
severed Gulf exporter is not simply stranded with its lost sales. The supplier-side margin of the
balancing redirects the recoverable part of its displaced output onto the buyers it can still reach,
through the East–West pipeline to Yanbu, intra-Gulf customers, and other routes that do not transit
the strait. But those clear routes carry less than half of its benchmark sales, so the friction τ writes
off the larger unplaced remainder as direct loss, and that residual is the 53% direct bar. The rest
of Saudi Arabia’s loss arrives through network propagation and the bundle penalty on its own
import-dependent activity. The UAE is cushioned somewhat more, its direct share 43%, because the
ADNOC Habshan–Fujairah pipeline and a larger clear-route share give it more surviving outlets to
absorb redirected output. Australia is the small-net-exposure case. Its bulk exports to East Asia
reroute through the Lombok and Sunda passages that the model treats as bypassing Malacca, and
the substitution of Asian buyers toward Australian bulk partly offsets even the residual loss.
Figure 9 reports the direct-impact share, the complement of network propagation, for thirteen
African economies across the twelve closures. The three ROW Africa aggregates (North, West,
54


## Page 55


and East Africa) hold around 60% direct-impact share across every column, the inverse of the
global top-twenty pattern. Cameroon, Tunisia, Morocco, Egypt, and Senegal carry 50 to 66% direct-
impact shares on their binding chokepoints (Hormuz, Turkish, Suez, Bab-el-Mandeb, Gibraltar).
Angola is the only Sub-Saharan oil-exporter for which Malacca and Taiwan are also high-direct
closures, because Angolan crude shipments to Chinese refineries cross the Strait of Malacca and
through-routes to Taiwanese petrochemical buyers. South Africa is the closest African analogue
to a Western European exposure profile, at 41% on Hormuz and single digits elsewhere except
Gibraltar and Taiwan, reflecting its more diversified industrial base and its longer Cape-route
export pattern. Where the global majors absorb closure losses through propagation from elsewhere,
African economies absorb them directly, because their commodity-narrow export baskets and
import-dependent downstream production have no comparable within-sector substitution margin.
Figure 9: Direct-impact share of country-level value-added loss for thirteen African economies,
across the twelve single-chokepoint closures. The direct-impact share is one minus the network-

5
Joint Chokepoint Closures
Geopolitical risk rarely arrives one chokepoint at a time: a regional escalation or a sharp rise in
war-risk premia typically impairs several corridors at once, and the single-closure exercises of
55


## Page 56


Section 4 are silent on such episodes. This section therefore closes chokepoints jointly, in three
scenarios drawn from recognizable international-political fault lines, a Middle East escalation, an
East Asian security crisis, and a Russia–Europe confrontation.20 The question a joint closure poses
is whether it costs the world more or less than the sum of its parts, and the answer turns on what
the constituent closures sever. A joint closure costs more than the sum when its constituents starve
common downstream buyers of distinct, complementary inputs, and less when they duplicate one
another on shared corridors. The Middle East and Russia–Europe scenarios fall on the first side of
the line, the East Asian scenario on the second. Three formal results discipline the comparison:
country-sector losses need not be monotone in the closure set, the world aggregate is nonetheless
always sub-additive in the linear part of the model, and any super-additivity must therefore be
carried by the CES bundle cascade.
5.1
Scenario design and the detour-class correction
A scenario Q ⊆K is a set of chokepoints that close simultaneously. The disrupted fraction dij
was defined for a single closure in (2) as the union over per-chokepoint indicators. The same
formula extends to joint closures, but with a caveat. The union form treats each chokepoint failure
as a physically independent event, which is correct when the closed chokepoints have disjoint
detour routes but a source of double counting when two chokepoints share the same detour. A
flow that has already paid the Cape-of-Good-Hope detour for one closure does not pay it again
when a second chokepoint on the same Cape route also closes. Among the twelve chokepoints
of K, Suez, Bab-el-Mandeb, and Gibraltar share the Cape of Good Hope as their physical detour
for Asia–Europe traffic (Online Appendix OA.2). The other six chokepoints each have their own
detour, or, in the case of the three terminal gates, none at all.
We accommodate this structure by partitioning K into detour-equivalence classes: two choke-
points lie in the same class when a closure of either forces the same physical detour on flows that
transit both. At K = 12 the partition is
D =

{Hormuz}, {Turkish}, {Danish}, {Kerch}, {Dover},
{Suez, BAM/Aden, Gibraltar}, {Malacca–Singapore},
{Panama}, {Taiwan–Luzon}, {Korea}

(38)
The first four singletons are the terminal gates, whose detour route is non-existent (Hormuz,
Turkish, Danish, Kerch). The Cape class {Suez,BAM/Aden,Gibraltar} collects the Asia–Europe

nodes to deliberate restriction. The central nodes here are physical maritime corridors rather than financial clearing
systems or digital platforms.

around-Japan respectively) that are not themselves chokepoints in K. Each therefore forms its own
class. The Luzon Strait is consolidated into the Taiwan–Luzon class rather than constituting an

with the convention max∅= 0. Within each detour class, the worst single closure pays for the shared
detour and additional closures in the same class do not compound the loss. Across detour classes,
the loss aggregates by the independent-union form of (2). The product across classes, rather than a
sum, is what sequential exposure implies. The flow surrenders the share e of its mass at one class,
and only the surviving remainder is exposed to the next, so the share that survives the scenario is
the product of the class-level survivals. A sum of the e’s would sever the overlapping mass twice
and can exceed one, severing more than the whole flow. Equation (39) reduces algebraically to the
union form whenever |Q| = 1, or whenever Q places at most one chokepoint in each class, so the
single-chokepoint results of Section 4 are unaffected. The correction binds only on scenarios that
place two or more chokepoints in the Cape detour class, and among the three scenarios we examine
only the Middle East scenario does so, through the {BAM,Suez} pair.
5.1.1
Middle East regional escalation
The first scenario closes the Strait of Hormuz together with Bab-el-Mandeb and the Suez Canal,
QME = {Hormuz,Bab-el-Mandeb,Suez}. The combination is a conjunction of two distinct shocks.
Hormuz is the sole exit route of Persian Gulf energy exports, and closing it removes the bulk of
crude, refined products, and petrochemical feedstocks that supply Asian and European refining
and chemical chains. Bab-el-Mandeb and Suez govern the Asia–Europe corridor through the
Red Sea, and closing them severs the route through which Asian electronics, machinery, and
intermediate goods reach Europe and through which European chemicals and capital goods reach
Asia. A regional Middle East escalation can connect the two theaters through alliance politics,
proxy conflict, naval deployments, or retaliation against commercial shipping. The economic event
need not be a legally declared blockade. Once war-risk premia rise enough, or once major carriers
refuse to transit the affected corridors, the corridor ceases to be part of the commercial network
for a large share of firms. The detour-class correction (39) bites here precisely because BAM and
57


## Page 58


Suez share the Cape detour. On Asia–Europe pairs the disrupted fraction is max(eBAM,eSuez) ≈0.27
rather than the spurious union value of ≈0.45 the independence assumption would produce.
5.1.2
East Asian security crisis
The second scenario closes three East-Asian chokepoints together:
QEA = {Taiwan–Luzon Corridor,Malacca–Singapore,Korea Strait}
The constituents span the Pacific facade of the East Asian arc. The Taiwan–Luzon Corridor
carries the cross-Strait CHN–TWN intermediate-input flow, the short-haul container traffic of
North-East Asia, and the transpacific deep-water route from North-East Asia to the US West Coast.
(The Taiwan and Luzon passages are not separable at our regional resolution and are pooled into a
single chokepoint.) The Malacca–Singapore corridor carries the long-distance Asia–Indian Ocean
energy and goods flow, including the Gulf–Asia oil corridor and the Asia–Europe container route.
The Korea Strait carries Japan–Korea bilateral intermediates and the Russian Far-East maritime
exit. A Taiwan contingency need not remain geographically narrow. A naval response, export
controls, insurance withdrawal, or precautionary rerouting would draw the wider Indo-Pacific
into the scenario, and the three chokepoints enter the set together because they belong to the same
strategic–economic system. The three lie in distinct detour classes, so (39) reduces to the union
form for this scenario.
5.1.3
Russia–Europe maritime escalation
The third scenario closes three chokepoints adjacent to Russia’s European exposure: QRE =
{Turkish Straits,Danish Straits,Kerch Strait}. The combination captures simultaneous pressure
on the maritime gates that govern Russia’s western and southern seaborne trade. The Turkish
Straits connect the Black Sea to the Mediterranean and are governed by the Montreux Convention,
which gives Turkey a distinctive legal role during war. The Danish Straits connect the Baltic Sea
to the North Sea and sit inside a Baltic security environment in which the risks come less from
formal closure than from sabotage and increased naval inspection. The Kerch Strait is the sole
maritime exit of the Sea of Azov, and its closure disables the Azov-port outlet of southern Russia
(Rostov-on-Don, Taganrog) and eastern Ukraine (Mariupol, Berdyansk) for grain, fertilizer, basic
metals, and refined products. The three mechanisms differ, but a Russia–Europe escalation can
impair maritime access at all three at once. Unlike QME and QEA, this is not a global container-
shipping shock but a Europe-centered shock to energy, food, fertilizers, metals, and industrial
inputs. All three gates are terminal, so the reroutability matrices are ETurkish = EDanish = EKerch = 1
on their respective supports and the loss is bounded above only by the share of benchmark flows
58


## Page 59


that transit each passage. The three gates also sit in distinct detour classes, so (39) again reduces to
the union form.
We emphasize that the three scenarios are structured counterfactuals rather than forecasts.
Their purpose is to discipline the analysis of joint closures by international-political logic rather
than by arbitrary combinatorics. Together, they span the three main geopolitical regions in which
maritime chokepoints are simultaneously economically central and politically exposed: the Middle
East, East Asia, and Russia–Europe.
5.2
Super-additivity and the role of nonlinear feedback
The natural object of comparison is the joint loss versus the sum of its constituent single losses.
Define the super-additivity index
S(Q;E,τ,ρ) :=
LW (Q;E,τ,ρ)
X
k∈Q
LW ({k};E,τ,ρ)
.
(40)
A value S(Q) > 1 means the joint shock costs more than the sum of its parts, and S(Q) < 1 means
it costs less. The single-chokepoint exercises of Section 4 cannot generate this object, so the joint
scenarios put us in a position to study it for the first time.
The sign of the departure from additivity has a topological reading. When two gates lie in
series on a common corridor, so that a shipment must clear both to reach its destination, closing
either already forces the detour. The joint disruption of that flow is then the maximum of the two,
not their sum, and the second closure is pure duplication. This is the Middle East configuration:
Bab-el-Mandeb and Suez sit in sequence on the Red Sea route and Hormuz lies upstream of
the same Gulf oil, which is why that joint falls short of the sum of its parts. When instead the
gates lie on distinct corridors delivering different inputs to a common buyer, each closure is
individually reroutable and singly survivable. But closing both exhausts the buyer’s slack on
two complementary inputs at once, and under the CES curvature the bundle is governed by its
scarcest component, so two small shortfalls compound into a loss larger than their sum. This is the
East Asia configuration. Reroutability separates the regimes: in series it cannot help the second
gate, so duplication dominates; in parallel it rescues each gate alone, which is exactly why the
complementarity bites only jointly.
One might expect, from a Leontief intuition, that adding chokepoints to Q cannot improve any
country-sector’s situation. Closing more routes removes outbound flows for some country-sectors
and degrades incoming bundles for others, so the activity of each i should fall, at least weakly,
as Q grows. This intuition is correct in a Leontief specification without intra-sector substitution.
It fails in the present specification, however, because the two-sided balancing of Definition 1
59


## Page 60


redistributes displaced demand toward surviving same-sector suppliers. As more same-sector
suppliers become exposed, the buyer-side scaling vj on a clear supplier’s column rises and its
intended order a(ω)
ij

weight) can rise with it. This is a positive trade-redirection externality, the displaced demand of
severed same-sector suppliers redistributed onto the clear supplier’s column and solved jointly
with every supplier’s own row target. And it can dominate the cascade contraction for individual
country-sectors. The next lemma makes this precise. The two propositions that follow then
state what remains true about the aggregate world loss in spite of the failure of country-sector
monotonicity.
Lemma 4 (Failure of entrywise monotonicity). There exist scenarios Q ⊊Q′ ⊆K and country-sectors i

The source of the non-monotonicity is the two-sided reallocation of Definition 1. For a supplier
i whose link to buyer j is clear under both scenarios (so baij = aij), the intended order is a(ω)

where the buyer-side scaling vj rises as same-sector rivals of i on column j are severed, since the
capped column target αsj −τℓsj must then be met by fewer surviving suppliers. The supplier-side
scaling ui likewise rises as i’s own competing outlets close. The product can therefore increase from
Q to Q′ even as more chokepoints shut, which is what (41) records. The configuration requires that
the newly severed rivals retain outlets away from j and that the block’s support graph contain a
cycle through i, since on a tree the margins pin every entry; both conditions are generic in the data.
Lemma 4 draws a sharp line between the aggregate and the entrywise behavior of the propaga-
tion operator. Column sums of the operator M(Q) := Aω(Q)(I−β) are monotonically non-increasing
in Q. Adding chokepoints strictly lowers column j’s sum by (1 −βj)τ times the newly displaced
sectoral mass, because the reallocation friction τ > 0 ensures that the unrecoverable share is per-
manently lost from the column. The individual entries of M(Q), in contrast, can shift in either
direction, as severed links lose mass and clear links gain it through the reallocation term. The
redistribution within a column preserves the column-sum contraction but breaks entrywise mono-
tonicity, and the failure of entrywise monotonicity carries through to country-sector monotonicity
at the equilibrium.
60


## Page 61


The aggregate world loss is nonetheless sub-additive in the linear part of the model, up to a
remainder that is exactly the aggregate footprint of the trade-redirection externality of Lemma 4,
and that remainder vanishes on the data. The key to the result is an accounting identity. When
value added is measured by its input–output definition, the benchmark value-added multiplier of
every column is exactly one. So the world loss depends on the disrupted matrix only through its
column sums, which are pinned by the targets of the balancing and are submodular in the closure
set, and on the post-closure activity vector. The within-column redistribution that breaks entrywise
monotonicity enters the aggregate only through that activity vector, which is what the remainder
isolates.

The proof isolates two distinct sources of dissipativity, both operating in the direction of sub-
additivity. The first is column-sum submodularity (47), which traces to the geographic overlap of
disrupted mass on shared routes. The logic is that of the Bonferroni inequality, P (A∪B) ≤P (A)+P (B).
A flow severed by one closure cannot be severed again by another, so the mass a joint scenario
disrupts is the union of the constituent masses, and it falls short of their sum by exactly the mass the
constituents disrupt in common. We call this shortfall the Bonferroni overlap. When two chokepoints
close flows that already shared a detour the overlap is large, and the joint column-sum loss sits
well below the sum of the single losses. The second is the unit-multiplier identity v⊤(I −M0)−1 = 1⊤
(Lemma 6): a dollar of final delivery placed anywhere in the benchmark network generates exactly
one dollar of value added along its supply chain. So the projection that aggregates losses to world
value added weights every row equally, and an equal-weighted projection is exactly what the
61


## Page 62


within-column redistribution cannot move. The redistribution breaks entrywise monotonicity
at the country-sector level, as Lemma 4 showed, and it re-enters the aggregate only through the
post-closure activity at disrupted columns, which is the remainder R. The remainder is supported
on the clear-supplier boosts of Lemma 4 landing on disrupted columns, and on the extended
ICIO data it is quantitatively small. For every scenario we compute, R is one to two orders of
magnitude below the sub-additivity margin it bounds, a factor of thirteen at the tightest scenario
and thirty-three at the next, so S lin(Q) ≤1 holds with room to spare.
Linear sub-additivity is thus the baseline against which the full model must be read. Any value
of S(Q) strictly above one that the equilibrium of (18) returns must therefore be carried by the
CES bundle factor eκ(ω)

under which the bundle channel dominates the linear sub-additivity and produces super-additive
aggregate loss.
Proposition 6 (Existence of super-additivity for strongly complementary inputs). Fix any scenario Q
with |Q| ≥2 whose constituent chokepoints disrupt distinct sectoral inputs at some common downstream
buyer column j. By this we mean that there exist sectors s1 , s2 and singletons k1,k2 ∈Q for which
the column-sector retention ratios ef (ω)

Proof. See Appendix A.4.
One caveat on the proposition’s logical form should be stated plainly, because the threshold
and the stability region pull in opposite directions. The cascade term that delivers (44) diverges
as ρ →−∞, but the stability region of Assumption 2 shrinks in the same limit, so the proposition
is an existence result about the mechanism. It guarantees a complementarity threshold beyond
which the bundle cascade overturns the linear sub-additivity, not that this threshold falls inside
the maintained calibration rectangle for every scenario. Whether it does is a property of the data,
verified scenario by scenario. For the East Asia joint the threshold is interior, since S(QEA) sits
above one at the baseline ρ = −1: its constituents impair distinct inputs at common buyers, so
ρ⋆(QEA) > −1. For the Middle East joint the hypothesis itself fails, because its constituents lie in
series on shared corridors and impair overlapping flows rather than distinct inputs, and the linear
sub-additive regime prevails. Sub-additivity thus prevails where the Bonferroni overlap dominates,
and super-additivity emerges where distinct complementary inputs are starved at common buyers.
The next subsection reports which of the three politically coherent scenarios sits in each regime at
the baseline calibration.

The East Asian scenario, with three chokepoints (Taiwan–Luzon Corridor, Malacca–Singapore,
Korea), costs 0.17% of world value added against the sum of its three singletons, 0.13%. The index
is S(QEA) = 1.3, in the super-additive regime of Proposition 6. The Russia–Europe scenario costs
0.85% against a constituent sum of 0.80%, an index of S(QRE) = 1.1, also super-additive. The
Middle East scenario, by contrast, is sub-additive: its joint loss of 0.94% falls short of the constituent
sum of 1.13%, an index of S(QME) = 0.8. The split of Section 5.2 is thus borne out across the three
63


## Page 64


scenarios, each index stable in sign across the calibration rectangle (Online Appendix OA.4.5), and
the paragraph below traces each to the series-versus-parallel geometry of its gates.
The origin of the split is structural, in the topology of Section 5.2. In the Middle East scenario
the gates lie in series: Bab-el-Mandeb and Suez sit in sequence on the Asia–Europe route (one
detour class), and Hormuz severs at the wellhead the Gulf crude that would in any case transit
them. Closing all three therefore disrupts little more than closing the binding one, so the linear sub-
additivity of Proposition 5 dominates whatever crude-versus-manufactured complementarity the
scenario also carries. The Russia–Europe and East Asian scenarios sit on the other side, and there
the sectoral detail is what matters. In the Russia–Europe scenario, the Turkish-Strait closure severs
Black Sea exports of grain, fertilizers, metals, and energy while the Danish-Strait closure severs
Baltic energy and industrial inputs. The Central and Eastern European buyers between them lose
distinct sectoral inputs from both corridors at once, precisely the configuration of Proposition 6. In
the East Asian scenario the three passages lie in distinct detour classes and carry distinct flows: the
cross-Strait and transpacific traffic of the Taiwan–Luzon Corridor, the Gulf–Asia and Asia–Europe
traffic of Malacca, and the Japan–Korea intermediates of the Korea Strait. Their closures therefore
starve common East Asian producers of complementary inputs, and the CES amplification carries
the joint above the sum.
The geography of cross-country exposure is reported in Figure 10, with each scenario painted
on a world map at a shared color scale. ROW-block aggregates are expanded onto their constituent
territories using the mapping of Table OA.1.1 so the choropleths fill all major land areas.

## Page 66


The Middle East panel is Gulf-centered. The joint closure QME converges to a differentiated
interior equilibrium, and its deepest losses fall on the supply-side counterparts of the Hormuz
closure: the United Arab Emirates (17%), the OtherGulf block (12%; Iran, Iraq, Kuwait, Qatar,
Oman, Bahrain), and Saudi Arabia (10%), whose Mediterranean and Atlantic trade the Suez–Bab-
el-Mandeb closure severs on top of Hormuz. Beyond the Gulf the losses settle at about 5% each
on oil-dependent developing regions: North, West, and East Africa and Central and South Asia.
The economics is straightforward. These economies export narrowly (crude oil, refined products,
ores, agricultural commodities) on routes that head north and east through the chokepoint set, and
they import most of what their few downstream sectors use. Both sides of their trade therefore
run through the Mediterranean–Suez–Hormuz corridor that QME collectively severs. The major
Asian energy importers (China, Korea, Taiwan, India, Japan) absorb the shock through the bundle
channel: their refining, chemical, and power columns lose Gulf inputs and the CES penalty cascades
to downstream manufacturing. But their diversified economies keep the loss modest, well under
2%. The country-level differentiation that the single closures display is thus preserved: the panel is
a map of Gulf-and-oil-corridor exposure, not a uniform ceiling.
The Russia–Europe panel paints a tight band along the Caspian, the Black Sea, and the Baltic,
with secondary exposure radiating into the rest of Eastern Europe and the Mediterranean. The
deepest cells are OtherCentralAsia (13%), Poland (11%), Ukraine (10%), Bulgaria (9%), Kazakhstan
(8%), Romania (7%), and Turkey (7%). The Baltic littoral (Estonia, Lithuania, Finland, Sweden)
follows at 5–7%. The pattern reflects the specific structure of Russia-adjacent maritime trade.
The Black Sea is the only outlet for Caspian and Ukrainian grain, fertilizer, metals, and oil, and
the Turkish Straits closure makes that outlet inoperable. The Baltic Sea is the principal outlet
for Russian energy via Primorsk and Ust-Luga, and the Danish Straits closure makes that outlet
inoperable. Buyers further west, Germany (1.5%), the Netherlands (1.3%), and the United Kingdom
(0.5%), sit far lower. Their alternative supply routes (Norwegian gas, North American LNG, Middle
Eastern crude via Suez, Mediterranean pipelines) partly bypass the disrupted gates. Russia itself
loses 4.2% of GDP. The same three closures that sever the rim economies’ imports also sever Russia’s
maritime export base: Black-Sea crude and grain through the Turkish Straits, Baltic energy through
Primorsk and Ust-Luga behind the Danish Straits, and Azov-port trade behind the Kerch Strait. It
loses materially less than the Caspian-, Black-Sea-, and Baltic-rim economies (5–13%) only because
of its overland and Pacific alternatives. The pipeline network (Druzhba westward, Power of Siberia
and ESPO eastward) and the Far-East port system carry the large share of its exports that never
touched the closed gates. The economies most damaged by a Russia–Europe maritime escalation are
still not Russia or its principal adversaries, but the transit and rim economies that depend on either
side of the Russian maritime trade for their own intermediate inputs. The production-network
mechanism makes this exposure visible, where a bilateral trade-balance reading would miss it.
66

The East Asian panel is the visually quietest of the three. Most countries are shaded at the
low-loss end of the scale. The deepest cells are Brunei and the United Arab Emirates at about 1.6%
(eastward energy exports through Malacca to North-East Asian buyers), Vietnam at about 1.4%,
the Democratic Republic of the Congo at about 1% (cobalt and copper to Chinese electronics),
and Singapore at about 0.6%. Even the ROW aggregates stay below 1% here, so no economy or
bloc stands out on this panel. Taiwan itself loses only about 0.7% of GDP in the scenario named
after its strait. Taiwan is an endpoint of the maritime network rather than a transit economy. The
closure captures most of its short-haul cross-Strait trade with mainland China (the country-pair
override of Table OA.2.8), but the rest of Taiwan’s trade either uses Pacific routes east of the island
or sits behind diversified non-East-Asian-corridor supply. China sits at 0.2%. Its inbound energy
and Asian intermediate flows that nominally transit Malacca are largely substitutable through the
Lombok and Sunda detours within the model’s reroutability calibration. And its outbound trade
to Europe through the Asia–Europe corridor is diversified across many overlapping routes. The
visual quietness of the panel is not a sign that the East Asian crisis would be economically modest
in practice. It is a sign that the network-structural cost of the Taiwan–Malacca joint closure is small
at our baseline calibration. Behind the small number are the high reroutability through Lombok
and Sunda and the within-sector substitution friction τ = 0.3, which recovers 70% of displaced
same-sector demand on clear suppliers. The detours are, in short, load-bearing. Re-solving the
scenario with the Lombok–Sunda and east-of-Taiwan detours closed raises the loss to about 2.6%,
more than an order of magnitude higher, though still a finite, differentiated equilibrium rather than
a cascade. Scaling every detour distance by ±50%, by contrast, moves the scenario only between
about 0.1% and 0.3% (Online Appendix OA.4). The other two scenarios are insensitive to such
calibration choices because their constituents are terminal gates with no viable detour.
The three scenarios are points in a larger space, and the mechanism that sorts them organizes
that space systematically.21 We re-solve the model for all 66 pairwise closures, which span the
structural configurations (pairs within a detour class, pairs on overlapping corridors, and pairs
severing distinct inputs at common buyers). We report the super-additivity index of each in
Table 9. The gradient is exactly the theory’s. The within-Cape-class pairs are the most sub-additive:
Suez–Bab-el-Mandeb sits at S = 0.5 and Suez–Gibraltar and Bab-el-Mandeb–Gibraltar at S = 0.9,
the detour-class correction holding two gates on one corridor to little more than the larger of
their singles. The distinct-route Pacific pairs are super-additive (Malacca–Taiwan-Luzon at S = 1.2,
21The general problem this raises is a combinatorial optimization problem on the network: choosing which subset of
chokepoints to close, and predicting the loss from the combinatorial structure of that subset (which gates lie in series
on a shared corridor, which sever complementary inputs at a common buyer). It is closest in spirit to the network
interdiction, minimum cut, and shortest path problems, which ask how removing edges blocks or lengthens a flow
(Ford and Fulkerson, 1956; Wood, 1993; Israeli and Wood, 2002), and more loosely to graph coloring and the traveling
salesman problem in the computer science and operations research literatures. It could be formalized along those
lines, for instance as an interdiction problem on the detour class hypergraph; we do not pursue that formalization here,
studying instead a small number of politically coherent scenarios together with the complete set of pairwise closures.

Taken together, the three panels describe a simple economic geography of joint maritime risk. A
69


## Page 70


country is hit hard under a particular scenario when three conditions all hold. Its exports depend
heavily on routes that transit at least one chokepoint in Q, and its imports depend heavily on
suppliers whose routes transit at least one chokepoint in Q. And the within-sector substitutes
for both flows lie outside the maritime network that Q disrupts. The Gulf states under QME, the
Caspian and Black-Sea rim under QRE, the East-Africa ROW under QME, and the Eastern-Europe
rim under QRE all satisfy the three conditions. And these are precisely the economies that show up
darkest on the corresponding panels. Countries that fail any one of them sit in the low-loss tail of
every panel. Examples are the United States (exports and imports largely bypass the chokepoint set),
China (diversified suppliers across overland and maritime corridors), Russia (pipeline alternatives
for energy export and a Pacific port for Asian buyers), and Germany and the Netherlands under
QRE (alternative supply routes that bypass the disrupted gates). The production-network model
identifies the conjunction of the three conditions as the source of large joint-scenario exposure,
and the choropleths make the geographic implications visible without the need for a separate
scenario-level ranking.
6
Model Validation
This section validates the model against the 2023–24 Red Sea disruption, the one sudden, sustained,
and well-measured maritime closure in the sample, by exploiting a separation inside the model
itself. Given a closure, the model does two conceptually distinct things. First it transmits the
shock through the production network. The severed trade propagates from the directly hit country-
sectors to their network neighbors, and onward, until it settles into a particular high-dimensional
footprint over country-sectors, a pattern of who is hit and by how much relative to whom. This
transmission is governed by the topology of the benchmark network and the reroutability of the
closed corridor, and it is silent about the structural parameters. The footprint is built with no
appeal to (τ,ρ,δ), which enter only afterward, to set how large the losses along that footprint are.
Shape, in short, is fixed by the network and scale by the parameters, so the two can be validated
separately and against different standards.
On both standards the model holds, and we state the verdict before the detail. The first test
is entirely parameter-free. Because each gate (Hormuz, Panama, the Danish Straits) generates a
different footprint, we can ask whether the realized country-sector decline after the Red Sea closure
falls on its footprint rather than another’s. And it does, after onset and not before, with no appeal
to (τ,ρ,δ). The model, that is, is right about who is hit. The second test brings the parameters
in, at values disciplined in Section 4.1 from supplier-recovery rates, substitution elasticities, and
historical leakage evidence, none of which mentions the 2024 episode. At those values the model’s
unbuffered envelope brackets the external estimates of the closure’s cost from the correct side and
70


## Page 71


at the correct order of magnitude. And the parameter region consistent with the realized falls lies
inside the independently estimated box, with the baseline calibration at its severe edge. The model,
in other words, is right about how large the hit can be.
Read together, the two are a shape-and-scale validation of the counterfactual, not an indepen-
dent estimate of the closure’s total causal effect. That estimate is foreclosed by the confounded
2023–24 environment of war-risk premia, energy prices, monetary tightening, and exchange-rate
movements, and the country-time fixed effects that clean the footprint test deliberately remove it.
Each stage also carries a limit the other does not cure. The footprint test recovers shape but neither
the total loss nor a separation of shocks with highly correlated footprints. And the scale comparison
is only as credible as its external anchors and the width of the parameter box. Section 6.1 runs the
parameter-free footprint test, and Section 6.2 brings the parameters in against the observed scale.
6.1
The parameter-free transmission: the cross-sectional footprint
The prediction we test is sharp and parameter-free. A closure of the Red Sea corridor implies
not only an aggregate loss but a country-sector pattern of losses. Country-sectors more exposed
through the propagated network should decline more after the closure, even after common country-
time and sector-time movements are removed, whatever the values of (τ,ρ,δ). The test is, by
construction, modest about what a confirmation would establish. It asks only whether the realized
output fall has the model’s predicted shape, not what the closure’s total causal output loss was,
since the country-time effects deliberately absorb the aggregate movements one would need for
that.
The identifying object is not the closure’s date, which the broader crisis shares, but the high-
dimensional country-sector exposure vector the model’s transmission generates for this closure. For
a country-sector i, the network-propagated exposure INDi carries the first-round shock through
the benchmark Leontief inverse (I −A(I −β))−1 and strips out i’s own direct term. It is thus the
contraction i inherits purely because its network neighbors’ trade was severed, even when i ships
nothing through the passage. It is computed from the benchmark network and the reroutability
alone, with no structural parameter. Table 10 reports the predicted Red Sea footprint. It is
geographically exactly what the corridor implies (North Africa, the Caspian rim, the Gulf, South
Asia, East and West Africa), heavy-tailed, with a standard deviation larger than its mean, and
substantially propagated rather than direct. The same operator applied to a different gate returns a
sharply different pattern, which is what makes the test specific. A Hormuz closure concentrates
on Gulf energy and its importers, a Panama closure on Pacific–US-East-Coast trade. And these
patterns are nearly uncorrelated with the Red Sea’s, as the placebo comparison below exploits.

We take this footprint to the OECD quarterly national accounts (value added by activity at
the ten-industry breakdown, chain-linked volumes, forty-two economies, with the United States
recovered from the BEA value-added-by-industry series on FRED) over 2022Q1–2024Q4. On that
panel we estimate a continuous-treatment event study whose treatment intensity at each country-
sector is its model-predicted Red Sea exposure. With country-by-quarter, sector-by-quarter, and
country-sector effects absorbed and standard errors clustered by country, the coefficient is identified
from the within-country-quarter, within-sector-quarter covariance between the realized decline
and the footprint. And it is consistent for the footprint’s effect as long as no other contemporaneous
shock carries the same country-sector footprint, the shift-share exclusion restriction, set out with
the full specification in Online Appendix OA.6.1. The test establishes three things about the shape
of the fall.
The first concerns incidence: country-sectors the model predicts to be more exposed decline
more after the closure. The coefficient on propagated exposure is negative throughout and sharpens
as the window narrows to the acute phase. It reaches −0.66 percentage points of value-added
growth per one-standard-deviation of exposure (t = −3.1) on 2023Q2–2024Q4, with a null-imposed
wild-cluster-bootstrap p of 0.02.
The second concerns timing: the decline appears after the closure, not before it. The event-study
profile (Figure 11) shows no pre-decline, with a pre-onset average that is positive and statistically
flat against a post-onset average of −0.26. Exposed country-sectors were thus not falling before the
closure, and the profile turns negative only after onset.
The third concerns specificity: the decline loads on the Red Sea footprint, not on generic network
72


## Page 73


centrality. Enter the closed corridor’s footprint alongside the footprints the same operator generates
for other gates. The genuinely orthogonal placebos, Panama and Korea, whose exposure correlations
with the Red Sea pattern are 0.03 and 0.10, are statistically null (t = 0.4 and 0.6), while the Red Sea
coefficient survives at −0.67 (t = −2.9) (Figure 12). Hormuz, whose footprint shares the corridor’s
energy-importer geography (correlation 0.56), is not an orthogonal placebo and loads marginally
when entered (t = 2.0), exactly as a correlated pattern should. We group it with the Mediterranean-
axis gates in the horse-race below. This network-footprint orthogonalization is the design’s core, and
it is exactly the parameter-free family of footprints at work. The alternative chokepoint patterns are
structured placebos, sharper than arbitrary ones because the same propagation operator generates
them, so a null on them is informative. Replication across independent episodes is selective in the
way the design predicts. The transient 2021 Ever Given grounding and the gradual, low-exposure
Panama drought are null, and the 2022 Black Sea disruption, whose footprint is entangled with
the contemporaneous European energy-price shock, is statistically null as well. That leaves the
sudden, sustained Red Sea closure as the one episode the panel can and does detect (Online
Appendix OA.6.1). The design’s honest limit is the mirror image of its strength: footprints that
genuinely overlap cannot be separated by it. Horse-raced against the correlated gates, the Danish
Straits and Hormuz, with footprint correlations of 0.34 and 0.56, the Red Sea point estimate stays
negative and significant (−1.04, t = −2.7) while the correlated placebos load marginally with the
opposite sign. The method identifies the Red-Sea-shaped footprint, not a physically pure closure
distinct from another shock that would percolate to the same pattern.
These three claims, incidence, timing, and specificity, are what a parameter-free footprint test
can establish, and they hold. Exposed country-sectors decline after the closure, not before, and
the decline has the shape of the Red Sea corridor rather than of unrelated maritime footprints.
Whether the cells fall by as much as the model predicts is a question of scale rather than of shape. It
depends on (τ,ρ,δ), and we take it up in Section 6.2, where the model’s scale is disciplined against
the external anchors. The footprint test is also silent on the catastrophic tail the model emphasizes,
the Gulf and rest-of-world blocks of Table 10, which is unmeasured at quarterly frequency. So the
test runs on the data-rich majors, and the scale of the loss is carried not here but by the external
anchors and the parameter region of the next subsection.
6.2
Parameters and the observed scale
With the shape now fixed, the second validation brings the parameters in and asks whether the
model is right about how large the loss is. We first fix the target, what the closure actually cost,
from external evidence. We then ask whether the model, evaluated at parameters disciplined
independently of the episode, reproduces it, and whether the parameters that do so lie inside the
box Online Appendix OA.5 supports.
