74


## Page 75


The scale target is the realized output fall, a quantity buffers and macro noise conceal. Most
container tonnage rerouted around the Cape of Good Hope, the residual was met from inventory
over months, and what remained is a fraction of a percent of GDP buried under the 2023–24
cycle. The independent assessments agree it was real but modest. The OECD judged that, were the
elevated shipping costs to persist, they would add about five percentage points to OECD members’
import costs and roughly 0.4 percentage points to consumer-price inflation after a year. World
trade would be lower by between 0.2 and 1.3 percentage points in 2024 depending on persistence.
It emphasized that the realized hit to activity was attenuated by spare shipping capacity, light
port congestion, subdued global demand, and adequate inventories (OECD, 2024). The European
Central Bank likewise judged the effect on euro-area activity “limited” (European Central Bank,
2024). UNCTAD documents the mechanism rather than an output number. Container transits
through the corridor fell by some 90% at the trough, with above 60% of container tonnage diverted
around the Cape (United Nations Conference on Trade and Development, 2024). The shipping-
cost-shock literature supplies the reduced-form macro response this implies. K¨anzig and Raghavan
(2026), instrumenting global shipping costs with narratively exogenous chokepoint disruptions,
find that an adverse shipping-cost shock lowers industrial production and GDP and raises consumer
prices persistently. The largest effects are in energy- and material-intensive manufacturing.
The one clean, large, and directly measured number sits at the mouth of the corridor itself.
Egypt’s Suez Canal receipts, about $9.4 billion in 2023, fell by some 60–70% over 2024, as ship
transits roughly halved and the high-value container traffic that pays the largest tolls fled the
corridor first. The IMF puts the loss at about $6 billion of foreign-exchange inflows for the year
(Suez Canal Authority, 2024; Kamali et al., 2024). Canal receipts are very nearly pure value added,
so this component alone is of order 1.5% of Egyptian GDP, and it is unbuffered by construction.
The rerouting that holds the rest of the world’s losses down is exactly what empties the canal, and
a transit foregone cannot be drawn from inventory.
One conceptual point must be put squarely before the receipts can be used, because it changes
what they can and cannot anchor. Canal tolls are a service export, the output of a transport sector
that does not travel by ship, which means that they lie outside the goods incidence of Section 3
and that the model never severs a toll. The model’s Egyptian loss λEGY is generated by the goods
network, by Egypt’s imported intermediates and corridor exports and the propagation they set
off, and is therefore a component of Egypt’s loss disjoint from the receipts. The two numbers do
not measure the same object, and comparing them as if they did would be a category error. In a
full accounting, rather, the two components add. The receipts consequently anchor two things,
neither of which is (τ,ρ) directly. First, they are a sharp, two-sided check on the severity input of
the counterfactual, the closure intensity δ on the container corridor, since tolls fall one-for-one with
transits. The ∼60% annual receipts fall, bracketing a trough transit decline near 90%, is exactly
75


## Page 76


what the δbox ∈[0.05,0.15] reading of Online Appendix OA.5.1 encodes. Second, summed with
the model’s network component they bound Egypt’s total unbuffered exposure, a sum the realized
Egyptian experience of 2024 must sit below once buffers operate.
With the anchors so understood, we read the model against them. At the baseline calibration
the model places the Red Sea closure at a world value-added loss of about a quarter of a percent,
unbuffered and sustained, and Egypt’s goods-network component at about 0.4% of GDP. The
closure is Q = {Suez,BAM}, graded by the reroutability of Online Appendix OA.2 so that flows
able to take the Cape are already netted out, and detour-corrected so that closing both gates on
the corridor does not double-count (Online Appendix OA.6). Both figures meet the data from the
correct side. At the aggregate level the model over-predicts, exactly as an unbuffered envelope must.
The unbuffered steady state sits above the realized world fall; the buffers push the realized outcome
well below it. The difference is the inventory drawdown, spare shipping capacity, and subdued
demand that the external assessments name and that the model deliberately omits (Section 4.1).
At Egypt, the model’s network component, added to the directly measured receipts loss, gives a
total unbuffered exposure of order 2% of GDP. That envelope is consistent with, and sits above,
the slowdown the IMF program reviews attribute to the episode, exactly as an unbuffered ceiling
should.
The question is then sharper than whether the baseline number matches. It is for which (τ,ρ,δ)
the model’s envelope is consistent with the anchors, and whether that region is the one Online
Appendix OA.5 independently supports. We answer it by solving the Red Sea closure across the
calibration rectangle, holding the geometry and the reroutability fixed and varying only the three
structural parameters. The full surface is in Online Appendix OA.6 and summarized in Figure 13.
Two features make the region well defined. First, the world loss is modest and slowly varying across
the estimated rectangle, running from about 0.1% at the mild corner (τ,ρ) = (0.2,−0.25) to 0.2–0.4%
across the τ-center [0.3,0.4], and rising smoothly to about 0.5% at the high-friction, near-Leontief
corner with no discontinuity. It is also nearly flat in the leakage across the identified container-
corridor range δ ∈[0.05,0.15], because the Cape detour, not the residual leak, carries the displaced
trade. Second, the Egyptian surface is sharp in the parameters. The network component climbs
roughly eightfold across the rectangle, from about 0.1% to 0.75% of GDP, against a roughly fourfold
range for the world loss. So the requirement that the unbuffered total, the network component plus
the measured receipts, remain within the order of the realized Egyptian experience cuts a genuine
region rather than admitting everything.
That region is the content of Figure 13. The consistency band favors the milder half of the
estimated box, with more reallocation friction trading against less substitutability along the familiar
iso-loss diagonal. And the baseline calibration sits at its severe edge, exactly where a deliberately
conservative (loss-maximizing) reading should sit, since the envelope it produces is the upper
76


## Page 77


edge of what the realized episode can accommodate, not the center. The aggregate moment, looser
because it is buffered, is consistent with the same region. The unbuffered world loss across the
center of the box reads down under any plausible buffer to the realized fraction of a tenth of a
percent, and stays modest across the whole box, steepening only toward the high-friction corner.
The scale check is therefore passed in the sense appropriate to an envelope. The parameter region
whose unbuffered exposures bracket the realized falls from above, without absurdity, is the region
the firm-level and production-function evidence already singled out, with the baseline calibration
at its severe boundary. We do not present this as a sharp rejection trial, since the box is wide, the
binding moments are bounds, and the cleanest anchor disciplines the closure severity δ rather
than (τ,ρ). But it is a real out-of-sample hurdle, and a calibration fixed entirely from unrelated
micro-evidence clears it.
Figure 13: The parameters whose unbuffered envelopes are consistent with the observed falls
coincide with the independently estimated box. World value-added loss (a) and Egypt’s goods-
network loss component (b) from the Red Sea closure across the calibration rectangle τ ∈[0.2,0.5],
ρ ∈[−1,−0.25] at the container-corridor leakage δ = 0.10 (make coherence figure.py). Green
rectangles mark the parameter box estimated in Online Appendix OA.5 from evidence independent
of the 2024 episode (solid: the τ-center [0.3,0.4]; dashed: the robustness range). The blue band
in panel (b) is the region at which the network component, added to the measured canal-receipts
loss of 1.3–1.6% of GDP, stays within the order of the realized Egyptian experience; the baseline
calibration (star) sits at its severe edge, inside the box. The surface is finite and smooth across the
whole rectangle, steepening toward the high-friction, near-Leontief corner.
77


## Page 78


7
Concluding Remarks
What does it cost the world economy when one of its critical maritime chokepoints closes? The
simple answer is the value of tonnage passing through the strait. That answer, however, is far
from the truth. Much of maritime trade involves intermediate inputs, and their disruption can
generate significant contraction in downstream production. We have, in this paper, developed a
model to assess the size of this contraction. It treats the world input-output table as a production
network of country-sectors. The model not only allows rerouting intermediate inputs around
closed straits, but also permits buyers to find alternate sellers and sellers to find alternate buyers.
Put simply, it lets economic actors circumvent the closure and, even more so, the problems that
arise from it. This flexibility, though, is bounded by the realities of economic geography and
short-run capacity constraints. One cannot circumvent the only exit from a bay, nor can alternate
suppliers expand production overnight however they might wish to do so. Nor, in the short run,
can the complementarity between cross-sector intermediate inputs be wished away. Interacting
with the production network, these rigidities make the cost of a closure a nonlinear equilibrium
object which is neither proportional to the transiting tonnage nor a smooth function of it. A passage
that carries little can hold a productive economy hostage, when what little it carries has no near
substitute and feeds much downstream. A passage that carries a great deal, by contrast, can be
shrugged off, when the same goods arrive by other routes and sellers.
That cost cannot be read off the tonnage, so we must compute it. We do so with a constrained
general equilibrium model of the world economy, applied to 12 critical chokepoints, one at a time
and then several at once. The model is built on the world input-output table. Because the economies
most exposed to chokepoints are otherwise buried in that table’s rest-of-world composite, we first
rebuild it into an eighty-seven-economy matrix that resolves the Gulf states, the Red Sea rim,
and much of Africa in their own right. We discipline the model’s three parameters by estimating
them from evidence outside the closures themselves. The first governs how much trade still slips
through a closed passage, and we estimate it from the transit record of eleven historical disruptions.
The second governs how readily one input substitutes for another, and we take it from empirical
estimates of how substitutable inputs have proved in production. The third governs how fully
displaced trade re-matches onto surviving partners, and we read it from evidence on how far firms
have recovered after a supplier is cut off. Because none of the three is fitted to the closures we
study, what the model returns is a genuine counterfactual, not a curve drawn through the answer it
was meant to explain.
Three regularities emerge from our empirical counterfactual exercises. The first is that choke-
point exposure is heavy-tailed across countries. A closed Strait of Hormuz takes about 1/6 of the
United Arab Emirates’ GDP, and about 1/10 from each of its Gulf neighbours. A closed Danish
Straits takes about 1/10 of Poland’s. Yet most of the world loses about 1% or less, even under its
78


## Page 79


worst single closure. A tail this deep is not the misfortune of a few exposed economies. It is built
into the structure of world production. The economies most dependent on one corridor tend also
to be those left with the fewest substitutes when it shuts, so the two sources of exposure line up
across countries rather than offset. The tail also reaches economies a map of shipping lanes would
not flag. Japan loses about half a percent of its GDP behind a closed Hormuz, without a single
Japanese hull crossing the strait. Its refineries are built around Gulf crude grades that reallocation
can replace in volume but not, over the short-run horizon, in kind. The loss is modest in level but
almost entirely indirect, and Japan illustrates another feature of the network, that a country’s tail
risk can be very different from its mean risk. About half of its chokepoint risk is lodged in that one
gate. Extreme as this is, it is not an aberration. For many economies, the closure that would ruin
them is seldom the one that troubles them most often.
The second regularity is that the two ends of a severed corridor do not share its loss evenly. The
imbalance is large. At all but one of the twelve gates, the supplier-side economy loses more than the
buyer-side economy. Along the major corridors the factor runs from a few-fold to more than tenfold
at the energy gates. What gives the finding its force is that the asymmetry is nowhere written
into the model. The balancing is neutral between its two margins. It grants buyers and severed
suppliers the identical formal opportunity to re-match, so any tilt in the outcome is inherited from
the trade data and not manufactured by the map. What supplies the tilt is economic geography.
Ours is a world where the economies selling through a chokepoint tend to be commodity exporters
with a narrow basket, often a single major good such as energy. The economies buying through
it are usually more diversified, drawing the same input from several sources. When the corridor
closes, that asymmetry decides who pays. The severed exporter has few off-corridor buyers for
its stranded output, and whatever it cannot place is a market simply lost. The importer, meeting
the same closure from the other side, re-sources across the suppliers it keeps and carries only the
residue, a productivity loss in bundles it can no longer assemble in benchmark proportions.
The third regularity concerns chokepoints forced shut together, as political conflict rarely
strikes a single corridor alone. Here the model yields not a magnitude but a split. A joint closure
can cost more than the sum of its single-closure parts, or less, and what it severs decides which. By
simple accounting it can only cost less. A flow lost to one closure cannot be lost again to another, so
the joint disrupts the union of what its parts disrupt, not their sum. Two gates on a shared corridor
thus largely duplicate one another. Anything beyond the sum comes from one further channel.
When two closures starve a common buyer of two complementary inputs, the damage to its bundle
exceeds their sum and spreads downstream. For inputs complementary enough, the joint then
overruns the sum. Our three scenarios fall on either side of this seam. A Middle East escalation
would close Hormuz with Bab-el-Mandeb and Suez, severing Gulf energy and the Asia–Europe
corridor at once. But Suez and Bab-el-Mandeb share the Cape-of-Good-Hope detour, and Hormuz
79


## Page 80


has already severed at the wellhead much of the Gulf oil the other two would carry, so the three
largely duplicate one another and the joint costs less than the sum of its parts. An East Asian crisis,
a Taiwan contingency, would close the Taiwan, Malacca, and Korea straits. These lie on distinct
routes and starve common buyers of complementary inputs, so the bundle feedback overruns the
accounting saving and the joint costs more than the sum. A Russia–Europe confrontation would
shut the Turkish, Danish, and Kerch Straits, the terminal gates of Russia’s seaborne trade; starving
Central and Eastern Europe of fuel, food, and materials, it too costs somewhat more than the
sum. Indeed, closing more passages need not injure every producer, since re-matching leans the
displaced demand of severed suppliers onto the survivors, leaving a handful of country-sectors
busier under a joint than under a constituent single closure.
The principal empirical findings of our paper run against the grain of how chokepoint risk is
ordinarily assessed. What matters is not how much trade crosses a passage, but how badly the world
re-sources and re-sells around it once it is gone. Irreplaceability, not tonnage, is the exposure. That
is why the deepest losses settle on economies with narrow export baskets and import-dependent
downstream sectors. It is also why an economy can be devastated by a strait through which it
itself ships almost nothing. For the economy that would insure against such a shock, the counsel
is not to take itself off the map. Geography is seldom so obliging. It is to hold several routes and
several suppliers, and to keep its distance from single-gate dependence. That dependence is the
very concentration the exposure matrix records, implicitly, in every one of its rows. It divides
the economies with a single Achilles heel from those exposed more broadly and more forgivingly.
And for the study of economic coercion and weaponized interdependence at large, the exercise
suggests a shift of attention. The objects worth monitoring are not the bilateral volumes around
which public argument is usually organized. They are the network positions in which the exposure
actually resides, namely corridor dependence, substitute breadth, and downstream centrality. The
central nodes here are physical straits rather than clearing houses or data conduits. But the logic
by which a network can be severed at a point, and its damage read off the topology, is the same. It
is a logic no ledger of flows will ever reveal.

A.2
Proof of the Failure of Entrywise Monotonicity in the Closure Set (Lemma 4)
The lemma is an existence claim, so it suffices to exhibit one configuration in which a clear supplier’s
intended order strictly rises when a second chokepoint closes, and to show that the equilibrium
activity inherits the sign. We first explain the mechanism, then establish it exactly in a four-seller,
four-buyer economy, and close with the count of such configurations in the data.
To see the mechanism, take a buyer j with three same-sector suppliers i1,i2,i3, and let k1 sever
i1 →j under Q := {k1} while k2 additionally severs i2 →j under Q′ := {k1,k2}, leaving i3 →j clear in
both. In the balancing a(ω)

heuristic, since the full balancing iterates the row and column steps jointly, and two features of
the support are needed for the sign to survive the iteration. The severed suppliers must retain

through their own leaked links. And the support graph of the block must contain a cycle through
i3, since on a tree the margins pin every entry and no redistribution can occur. Both features are
generic in the data and absent only in deliberately degenerate examples.

To pass from orders to activity, note that a boost in intended orders need not raise equilibrium
activity, and the short-run ceiling is the reason. At the benchmark every supplier’s intended
demand exactly equals its capacity, Di(x) = ¯di, and by Lemma 3 Di(ex) ≤¯di thereafter, so a supplier
already at capacity cannot convert a further order boost into activity above benchmark. The
violation (41) therefore arises when a clear supplier’s demand has fallen below capacity under
Q, its buyers’ activity having contracted. The additional closure in Q′ then redirects enough
demand toward it to lift activity back toward, but not above, benchmark. Whether the redirection
dominates is a property of the network, settled by computation on the economy of record. Under
QEA, seven of the 4,350 country-sectors carry strictly higher post-closure activity than under at
least one constituent single closure, though the largest such gain is below 0.01% of benchmark
output: the flow balancing redistributes displaced demand in proportion to buyer size, so the
redirection boost survives only as a trace effect on this economy.

The second ingredient is an accounting identity that removes the row-composition of the
disrupted columns from the aggregate entirely. It deserves to be stated separately, because it is
where the within-column redistribution of the balancing, which breaks entrywise monotonicity,
drops out of the world projection.
Lemma 6 (Unit benchmark value-added multipliers). Measure value added by its accounting share
vj = βj, so that VAj = xj −µj. Then

Proof. Because each column of A sums to one, the column sums of M0 = A(I−β) are 1⊤M0 = (1−βj)j,
so 1⊤(I −M0) = (βj)j = v⊤. Multiply on the right by (I −M0)−1.
The economics of the lemma is the Leontief national-accounting identity. A dollar of final
delivery placed at any country-sector generates, along its benchmark supply chain, exactly one
dollar of value added economy-wide: the direct value-added content plus the value embodied in
intermediates, summing telescopically. (The measured value-added row of the ICIO differs from
βjxj only by the net-tax wedge, which the data carry as the benchmark slack documented in Online
Appendix OA.3, and the proposition is stated for the accounting shares.)

which is (43). The remainder R is supported exactly on the configurations of Lemma 4. A column
j contributes only if it is disrupted under some constituent (δj(Ql) > 0) and the joint closure
nonetheless raises its linear activity above the constituent level, a clear-supplier boost landing on a
disrupted buyer. If no such configuration occurs, R = 0 and sub-additivity is exact.
Two remarks close the proof. First, the bound is unconditional and exact, and invokes no
monotonicity of the reduced-network multipliers [v⊤(I −M(Q))−1]j in Q. Such monotonicity would
in any case fail, by the same breakdown of entrywise monotonicity. Second, the remainder is
computable on the data scenario by scenario, by comparing the linear activity vectors of the joint
and constituent closures on the disrupted columns. For every scenario reported in the paper
the clear-supplier boosts land on hundreds to thousands of disrupted columns but each is small,
and their sum, valued in world value added, is one to two orders of magnitude below the sub-
additivity margin it bounds. For the East Asia joint, R = 1.1 × 10−4 percentage points of world
value added against a margin of 1.4 × 10−3, and for Russia–Europe, 3.4 × 10−4 against 1.1 × 10−2
(verify subadditivity.py in the replication archive).
A.4
Proof of the Super-additivity Existence Result via the CES Cascade (Proposition 6)
The proof proceeds in four steps. We first smooth the closure into a continuous intensity so that the
world loss is twice differentiable, which reduces super-additivity to the positivity of a cross-partial
derivative. Second, we show the CES bundle factor is strictly supermodular in sectoral shortfalls
but that this per-column effect is sub-additive, so the super-additivity cannot come from a single
column. Third, we apply the implicit function theorem to the equilibrium and decompose the
cross-partial into a non-positive direct term and a cascade term carried through the network
resolvent. Finally, we show the cascade term diverges as ρ →−∞and extend the resulting strict
positivity from the origin to the scenario corner by continuation.
We work with closure intensities χ ∈[0,1]K, with χk = 1 corresponding to the full closure of
chokepoint k and χk = 0 to its remaining open. Replace the indicator product in (2) with the

By (49) this is the statement S(Q;ρ) > 1.
Two honest caveats delimit what is proved. First, the divergence (51) operates as ρ →−∞while
the stability region of Assumption 2 shrinks in the same limit. And nothing in the argument
guarantees that the threshold ρ⋆falls inside the maintained calibration rectangle for a given
scenario. For scenarios with three or more constituents, moreover, the higher-order interaction
terms of the expansion grow polynomially in |ρ| themselves, so the domination that defines ρ⋆is a
joint property of the scenario and the data rather than an asymptotic automatism. The proposition
should accordingly be read as an existence result about the mechanism, sharp for constituent pairs.
Whether the threshold is interior is an empirical property of the scenario, settled by computation: at
the baseline calibration the Middle East joint returns S > 1 well inside the stable region, so ρ⋆(QME)
is interior. The East Asia joint fails the proposition’s hypothesis outright, since its constituents
impair largely overlapping flows on shared corridors and fs1j and fs2j are typically degraded by
the same chokepoint within each singleton. The linear regime of Proposition 5 therefore prevails.
Second, the smoothed intensity path crosses the within-class tie set only at finitely many points
(see the construction above), so the segment integration is legitimate.
In one sentence: the super-additivity is not a per-column effect, which is sub-additive, but
a network one, carried by the bundle-factor cross-partial that enters the fixed-point operator
multiplicatively and propagates a local degradation at column j through the resolvent (I −M0)−1 to
every downstream column, with an amplification that grows without bound as ρ falls.

101


## Page 102


OA.1
The Extended Input-Output Matrix
This section builds the bilateral intermediate-flow matrix Z∗that drives the model of Section 2.
The matrix must resolve trade at the country-sector level with enough geographic granularity
to assign flows to physical chokepoints, and the standard input-output table cannot do so. The
OECD Inter-Country Input-Output database (Yamano et al., 2025) provides the natural anchor: its
2025 edition covers 81 economies and 50 ISIC Revision 4 sectors for reference year 2019, giving
a 4,050×4,050 flow matrix with well-documented accounting consistency. We anchor on 2019
even though the 2025 edition carries reference years through 2022, because every later year is
contaminated for our purpose. The benchmark should describe the world economy in its normal
state. But 2020 is the pandemic cross-section, 2021 the rebound and container-market dislocation,
and 2022 carries the full-scale war in Ukraine, which rearranged exactly the energy flows whose
severance the counterfactuals study. The closures are therefore read against the last normal-times
network. But the OECD table aggregates all economies outside the 80 individually identified
countries into a single Rest of World (ROW) composite. And that aggregation hides exactly the
regions a chokepoint analysis needs: the Gulf states (Qatar, Kuwait, Oman, Bahrain, Iran), the Red
Sea rim, and much of Africa, precisely the economies whose trade is most exposed to maritime
chokepoints.
We resolve this by constructing an extended matrix Z∗∈RG∗N×G∗N
+
with G∗= 87 economies:
the 80 individually identified OECD economies plus seven maritime-relevant ROW blocks, at
the same N = 50 sectors. The construction uses the GTAP 10 database (Aguiar et al., 2019) as a
satellite source: GTAP provides bilateral intermediate-flow data for 141 regions and 65 sectors
(base year 2014) at a geographic resolution that resolves the chokepoint-relevant parts of ROW.
The OECD table supplies the correct levels, and GTAP supplies the within-ROW shares used to
disaggregate each ROW cell. This hybrid approach inherits the accounting quality of OECD ICIO
while exploiting GTAP’s superior geographic coverage.
OA.1.1
The OECD ICIO 2025 baseline
Let C = {1,2,...,G} be the set of G = 81 economies in the OECD ICIO 2025 baseline — 80 individu-
ally identified countries plus the ROW composite — and let S = {1,2,...,N} be the set of N = 50 ISIC
Revision 4 sectors. The baseline intermediate-flow matrix is Z ∈RGN×GN
+
, with element z(c,s),(c′,s′)
the value (current USD millions, year 2019) of output of sector s in country c used as intermediate
input by sector s′ in country c′. Rows and columns carry labels of the form ISO3 SECTORCODE:
for example, zSAU B06,CHN C19 ≈9,600 million USD records Saudi Arabia’s crude-petroleum sector
supplying roughly USD 9.6 bn to China’s petroleum-refining sector in 2019.

The OECD 2025 edition is the extended edition, which disaggregates China into a processing-

before constructing Z∗, so that the accounting is identical to the standard 81-economy ICIO table.

additional columns in the ICIO file.
OA.1.2
The rest-of-world aggregation problem
The OECD ICIO’s ROW composite aggregates all economies that are not individually identified
in the table. For most input-output applications this is innocuous, but for chokepoint analysis it
conceals exactly the countries whose trade flows are most route-sensitive. Table OA.1.1 lists the
most economically significant economies that are hidden in ROW in the standard OECD table.

103


## Page 104


The ROW problem has a direct consequence for measured chokepoint exposure. Under the
standard OECD table, the Strait of Hormuz appears to involve only SAU and ARE on the supply
side, since all other Gulf suppliers are lumped into an undifferentiated ROW seller. In reality,
Kuwait, Qatar, Oman, Bahrain, and Iran collectively account for a substantial share of global
petroleum exports through Hormuz. Unless their flows are separated from the broader ROW
aggregate, the model assigns them either zero exposure or an undifferentiated ROW exposure that
cannot be mapped to a specific transit route.
OA.1.3
Using GTAP to disaggregate the rest-of-world block
We use the GTAP 10 Data Base (Aguiar et al., 2019) to construct within-ROW bilateral shares.22
GTAP provides a complete global social accounting matrix with 141 regions and 65 sectors for
base year 2014. Its geographic scope individually resolves all the Gulf states, the main African
economies, and most of the rest-of-world regions that OECD ICIO aggregates into ROW.
The construction draws on three arrays from the GTAP basedata.har file:
• VIWS(i,r,c): the value of imports of commodity i from source region r to destination region
c, valued at world prices (USD millions, 2014). Dimensions: 65 × 141 × 141.
• VIFM(i,j,c): firms’ total demand for imported commodity i by industry j in region c (USD
millions, 2014). Dimensions: 65 × 65 × 141.
• VDFM(i,j,r): firms’ demand for domestic commodity i by industry j in region r (USD
millions, 2014). Dimensions: 65 × 65 × 141.
GTAP records VIFM at the destination level. It tells us how much imported oil industry j in
China uses, but not which fraction came from Saudi Arabia versus Kuwait. The bilateral dimension
is captured by VIWS. We combine the two via the import-proportionality assumption standard in
the input-output literature (Tukker et al., 2013): within each destination-commodity cell, the share
of imports sourced from region r is the same across all using industries.
22We use GTAP 10 rather than GTAP 11 or 12 because GTAP 10 is the most recent release made freely available to
academic users without an institutional license. And the within-ROW shares we read off it should be substantially
insensitive to which post-2010 GTAP release supplies them. The OECD ICIO 2025 levels we anchor to are fixed by the
2019 reference year, and GTAP supplies only the geographic disaggregation of the OECD ROW composite into our seven
blocks. And the relative bilateral distribution of intermediate flows within each disaggregated block (e.g., Iran versus
Kuwait versus Qatar inside the OtherGulf composite) typically evolves slowly relative to the aggregate level. Releases 11
(base year 2017) and 12 (base year 2017, refined) update the levels but leave the within-block sourcing patterns close
to the GTAP 10 values for the maritime-relevant economies in the seven ROW sub-aggregates. A formal sensitivity
check across GTAP releases is outside the scope of this paper. We report the GTAP 10 results. The qualitative ranking of
chokepoint exposures and the exporter–importer asymmetry of Section 4.6 turn, we note, on the OECD-anchored levels
and the chokepoint incidence matrix rather than on the choice of share-donor release.

shares of VIWS enter the construction.)
OA.1.4
The seven-block partition and sector concordance
We partition the 141 GTAP regions into 87 extended economies. The 75 GTAP regions that
correspond to economies individually identified in OECD ICIO 2025 are mapped one-to-one
to their ISO3 codes. The remaining 66 GTAP regions — those that fall inside the OECD ROW
composite — are aggregated into seven geographic blocks chosen to isolate the economically and
geographically distinct parts of ROW that are relevant to maritime chokepoints:

Note that the GTAP aggregate region xws (Rest of Western Asia) encompasses Yemen, Djibouti,
and Eritrea — Red Sea transit states that would ideally form their own block — but these cannot
be separated from the Gulf aggregate at GTAP’s geographic resolution. We therefore merge what
would otherwise be an OtherRedSea block into OtherGulf.
GTAP’s 65 sectors must be mapped to OECD ICIO’s 50 ISIC Rev. 4 sectors before the bilateral
flows can be used as share donors. The concordance assigns each GTAP sector to one or two ICIO
sectors with non-negative weights that sum to one. Most mappings are one-to-one (e.g. GTAP
oil →ICIO B06, i s →C24A, and wtp →H50). Split mappings arise where GTAP aggregates
sectors that ICIO 2025 disaggregates. For example, oxt (other mining) splits equally between B07
(metal ores) and B08 (other quarrying), and otn (other transport equipment) splits 70/30 between
C302T309 and the newly disaggregated C301 (shipbuilding).

The OECD ICIO reference year is 2019 and the GTAP 10 base year is 2014. We use GTAP
only for within-ROW shares, not for levels, so the critical assumption is that the relative bilateral
distribution of flows across ROW blocks is sufficiently stable between 2014 and 2019. This is
107


## Page 108


standard practice in the IOT extension literature (Tukker et al., 2013): production network shares
within a geographic sub-aggregate typically change slowly relative to the aggregate level. The
resulting extended matrix Z∗has dimensions G∗N ×G∗N = 4,350×4,350, with G∗= 87 economies at
N = 50 sectors. This matrix, together with the derived input-coefficient matrix A∗and value-added
shares β∗, is the empirical object that supplies the structural parameters to the model of Section 2.
OA.2
The reroutability matrices
This appendix documents the construction of the chokepoint reroutability matrices used in the
model of Section 2.
OA.2.1
What the reroutability matrix encodes
For each maritime chokepoint k, the reroutability matrix Ek = [ek,cc′] is a G × G matrix over the
G = 81 economies of the OECD ICIO 2025 database (80 economies plus a Rest-of-the-World
aggregate). The entry ek,cc′ ∈[0,1] is the share of a benchmark intermediate-input flow from
country c to country c′ that is lost when chokepoint k is completely closed, after the shipment has
exhausted any detour around the closed passage. A value of 1 means the flow cannot be salvaged,
and 0 means it can be fully rerouted. The model of Section 2 reads the matrix as homogeneous
across sectors, so the country-sector entry for a flow (c,s) →(c′,s′) is simply ek,cc′. The matrices are
symmetric, ek,cc′ = ek,c′c, because reroutability is a property of the shared sea route. This appendix
states the rule that generates the matrices and reports them at the resolution of maritime regions.
OA.2.2
The reroutability rule
Reroutability is governed by a single geographic fact: whether a closed chokepoint can be detoured.
A chokepoint that is the sole sea passage out of an enclosed basin has no detour, while a chokepoint
on a longer corridor can be bypassed by a route that goes around the obstruction, at the cost of
extra distance. We therefore set, for a flow from c to c′ whose benchmark route transits chokepoint
k,
ek,cc′ =
∆k
Dcc′ + ∆k
,
where Dcc′ is the benchmark voyage distance from c to c′ in nautical miles and ∆k is the extra
distance the detour around k adds in the same units. For a terminal chokepoint, the sole gate of an
enclosed basin, no detour exists, ∆k = ∞, and ek,cc′ = 1. For a bypassable chokepoint a finite detour
exists and ek,cc′ ∈(0,1): the lost share is the detour’s share of the total detoured voyage. A flow
whose benchmark route does not transit k has ek,cc′ = 0. The rule delivers the heterogeneity the
108


## Page 109


paper requires. A short route loses more to a given detour than a long one, since e falls as D rises,
and any flow trapped behind a terminal gate loses everything.
OA.2.3
From distance to lost share
A voyage detour increases the cost of moving cargo through several channels. Fuel and crew
time scale with distance and days at sea, capital tied up in inventory at sea bears interest, vessel
asset utilization falls when the rotation is reshuffled, and the cargo is exposed for longer to
perishability and contract-deadline penalties (Stopford, 2009; Hummels, 2007). Hummels and
Schaur (2013) estimate that each day at sea is worth a tariff of 0.6–2.1% of cargo value, with higher
figures for time-sensitive manufactured goods. Djankov, Freund and Pham (2010) document the
corresponding elasticity of bilateral trade volume to transit days, and Brancaccio, Kalouptsidi and
Papageorgiou (2020) model the endogenous response of freight rates to demand and routing shocks.
The functional form ek,cc′ = ∆k/(Dcc′ + ∆k) is the simplest one consistent with these facts. It satisfies
e = 0 when ∆k = 0, e →1 as ∆k/Dcc′ →∞, and equals the share of the total detoured voyage devoted
to the detour, the “wasted” share of transport effort. The level is in the right ballpark. At a service
speed of about 14 knots, a Cape-of-Good-Hope detour of ∆≈3,500 nautical miles adds about ten
days at sea. This implies a time cost of about 14% of cargo value at the central Hummels and Schaur
(2013) estimate, and roughly double that once fuel, capital, and perishability components are
added. The rule returns e ≈0.22–0.27 on the long Asia–Europe corridors (Tables OA.2.4–OA.2.5),
inside this band.
OA.2.4
Classification of the chokepoints
The Persian Gulf, the Black Sea, the Baltic Sea, and the Sea of Azov are fully enclosed basins, each
with exactly one sea gate. The Strait of Hormuz, the Turkish Straits, the Danish Straits, and the
Kerch Strait are therefore terminal. The Strait of Dover is a bypass gate, since a Channel transit
can be salvaged by the around-Scotland route, a detour of roughly 800 nautical miles that exists
physically even though it carries no benchmark mass and therefore does not itself enter K. Being
a detour rather than a chokepoint is precisely the operational distinction of Section 3.1; absence
from the chokepoint set, that is, is not absence from the map. The remaining seven chokepoints
likewise lie on through-routes with a navigable detour, listed in Table OA.2.1 together with the
extra distance ∆k the detour adds.

Maritime regions
Voyage distances are taken at the resolution of fourteen maritime regions. Each economy is assigned
to the region of its principal seaboard (Table OA.2.2), and landlocked economies are assigned to
the region of the ports through which their seaborne trade passes. Because the rule depends on c
and c′ only through their regions, the country matrix is constant within each region block.

OA.2.7
Reroutability values by chokepoint
Applying the reroutability rule of Section OA.2 to the routes of Table OA.2.3 yields the reroutability
of every region pair. The terminal chokepoints (Hormuz, Turkish, Danish) return e = 1 on their
whole support, as does Kerch on its Sea-of-Azov support. The remaining chokepoints (Suez, Bab-
el-Mandeb, Gibraltar, Malacca, Panama, Taiwan, Dover, Korea) return graded values. Suez and
Bab-el-Mandeb share the Cape-of-Good-Hope detour and return identical values, so we report
them in a single table. Dover’s support is the Baltic–Atlantic corridor, the flows that have already
exited the Danish Straits and transit the Channel en route to the Mediterranean, the Americas, or
beyond. At ∆= 800 nautical miles around Scotland the rule returns e between 0.06 (Baltic–Oceania,
D = 13,000) and 0.19 (Baltic–Mediterranean, D = 3,500) on that support.

The Korea Strait carries Japan–Korea bilateral intermediates and the Russian Far-East maritime
exit. At a detour of ∆≈1,500 nautical miles around the eastern coast of Japan, the rule returns e in

transit the strait. Values are taken from the country matrix directly.
OA.2.8
Notes and simplifications
The rule is deliberately simple and rests on geography alone. The following simplifications should
be borne in mind.
• Region resolution. Distances and routings are specified between fourteen regions, not
between individual ports, so the country matrix is constant within each region block. Cross-
Strait China–Chinese Taipei trade, the single largest bilateral intermediate flow, is the one
country-pair exception (Table OA.2.8).
• Terminal gateways. Hormuz, the Turkish Straits, the Danish Straits, and the Kerch Strait are
treated as having no detour (e = 1). Bypass pipelines (for Gulf crude) and the Kiel Canal (for
small Baltic vessels) provide limited relief that the rule ignores. The energy leakage δs of
Assumption 1 is what carries the pipeline-and-persistence margin instead.
116


## Page 117


• Persian Gulf coverage. Of the Gulf economies Saudi Arabia and the United Arab Emirates
are separate economies in ICIO 2025 (the UAE was added in the 2025 edition). Qatar, Kuwait,
Iraq, Iran, Bahrain, and Oman are grouped inside the OtherGulf block of the extended matrix
Z∗of Online Appendix OA.1.
• Russia. Russia trades through several basins. Its westbound flows are routed via the Baltic
(Danish Straits) and its southern flows via the Black Sea (Turkish Straits), while Pacific flows
transit no listed chokepoint.
• Shared detours. Suez, Bab-el-Mandeb, and Gibraltar lie on the same Europe–Asia corridor
and share the Cape of Good Hope as their detour. When a scenario closes more than one
of them, the detour-class form of the disrupted fraction in Section 5 corrects the resulting
double-counting at source.
• Rest of the World. The ROW aggregate is geographically heterogeneous, so its routings are
nominal and should be read with caution.
OA.3
Numerical stability of the unshocked fixed point
The Sinkhorn balancing
The intended-order matrix Aω of Definition 1 is computed, sector block by sector block, by the
Sinkhorn iteration. Holding the disrupted transactions kernel bZ fixed, alternately rescale the rows
of the sector-s block to the row targets r(ω)

The S blocks are independent and solved in parallel, and each block iteration is a pair of sparse
divisions. On a block whose surviving support is complete, so that every entry of the block kernel
is positive, Birkhoff’s theorem makes the iteration a strict contraction in the Hilbert projective
metric, with per-sweep coefficient tanh
1
4∆(bZ)

< 1. The projective diameter is invariant under
the column rescaling bzij = baijµj, so ∆(bZ) = ∆(bA) = log(1/δ) + O(1) as δ →0. On a block whose
support carries zeros the projective diameter is infinite and the Birkhoff bound is uninformative.
There, convergence follows from the strict Gale–Hoffman feasibility established in the proof of
Proposition 1(i) together with the monotone divergence-descent property of the alternating scaling
(Bacharach, 1970). And the observed rate follows the same log(1/δ) law. This is the rate behind
Proposition 1(ii), degrading only logarithmically in 1/δ and degenerating at full severance. Because
117


## Page 118


the targets are benchmark quantities, independent of the activity vector, Aω is a one-time pre-
computation ahead of the fulfillment fixed point and the activity solve. We verify on the data that
at the baseline δ = 0.10 the balancing converges to a sup-norm residual below 10−9 for every single
and joint scenario, that the support of Aω equals the benchmark support, and that no column total
exceeds one. At δ = 0 the iteration fails to converge and the column cap is violated, the numerical
signature of the well-posedness frontier of Proposition 1.
The balancing also has the decision-theoretic foundation invoked in Section 2.2: it is the
minimal revision of the disrupted trade pattern consistent with the two capacity caps.
Remark 2 (The balancing as a minimum-divergence adjustment). On each disrupted sector block,
the intended-order matrix of Definition 1 is the unique solution of

shadow price of seller i’s sales-flow (capacity) cap and vj the shadow price of buyer j’s budget.
Finiteness across scenarios and leakage
Under the two-nest fulfillment rule of Section 4.1 the non-intermediate (value-added) block holds
the delivery loop below unit gain, so the model returns a finite, differentiated interior equilibrium
at every single and joint closure, across the whole calibration rectangle and at every leakage level
down to a near-total blockade δ = 0.01. There is no shock-size frontier: no scenario, corner, or
leakage value drives the iteration to a saturated or trivial state. In particular the Middle East
joint, the most demanding scenario, remains finite throughout, at 0.94% of world value added at
the baseline uniform leakage δ = 0.10. The saddle-node behavior documented under the earlier
unit-pass-through rule does not arise; it was an artifact of that rule and is absent once the value-
added block cushions the loop. The commodity-differentiated reading of the leakage (δenergy = 0.45
on the energy commodities, δbox = 0.10 elsewhere) is retained as a robustness exercise in Online
Appendix OA.4, not as the baseline, and it too returns a finite differentiated equilibrium for every
scenario.
118


## Page 119


The fulfillment fixed point
The baseline calibration (τ,ρ,ϱ) = (0.3,−1,−1) adopted in Section 4.1 sits comfortably inside the
spectral bound ρ ≥−3 derived in Section 2, and there is no question of numerical stability at
this point. The interest of this appendix is to characterize how far below the analytic bound the
damped-Picard iteration of (17) remains stable in practice. The analytic obstruction is that the
Lipschitz factor of the CES bundle map κ(h) in h contains a term proportional to ˜f ρ−1, which
at ρ = −10 grows like ˜f −11. Any sector whose realized retention ˜f falls toward zero contributes
an unbounded gradient to the iteration. The theorem is a worst-case statement and does not
characterize the actual behavior of the iteration on the extended ICIO data.
To map the contractivity of the iteration as ρ becomes more complementary, we ran the
unshocked fixed point of (17), the closed-economy benchmark obtained by setting the chokepoint
scenario Q = ∅, at fixed τ = 0.70 and a sweep of ρ ∈{−1,−2,−3,−5,−10}. The unshocked map is the
most demanding test of intrinsic stability of the iteration, since it isolates the CES feedback from
any direct-disruption effect. For each ρ we recorded the full h-residual trajectory rn = ∥hn+1 −hn∥∞
of the damped-Picard scheme with damping factor ω = 0.5, the empirical Picard contraction factor

119


## Page 120


Figure OA.3.1: Left: per-iteration h-residual trajectories of the unshocked damped-Picard scheme
at τ = 0.70, on a logarithmic y-axis. All five values of ρ ∈{−1,−2,−3,−5,−10} produce monotone
exponential decay. Right: empirical Picard contraction factor ˆρemp as a function of ρ. The factor
grows monotonically with |ρ| but stays strictly below the unit boundary throughout, including at
ρ = −10.
Three observations follow. First, on stability: the empirical contraction factor stays strictly
below one for every ρ in the sweep, from ˆρemp ≈0.59 at ρ = −1 to ˆρemp ≈0.64 at ρ = −10. So even
the worst case in the sweep cuts the residual by more than a third at each step, unambiguously
contractive. The Banach iteration is therefore guaranteed to converge from any h(0) ∈[0,1]N to
a unique fixed point, and the analytic bound of ρ ≥−3 derived in Section 2 is conservative on
this dataset. Second, on the minima of the feedback factors: minh and minκ both equal 1.000 at
every ρ in the sweep, because the unshocked state is exactly the model’s fixed point under the
two-nest rule, and the capacity factor minc = 0.95 stays below the capacity-lemma ceiling of one
and so never binds. With κmin = 1 the CES Lipschitz amplification ˜f ρ−1 ≤κ−11
min never exceeds
one, so the worst-case sector sees no amplification at all and the damping factor ω = 0.5 absorbs
the iteration without difficulty. Third, on benchmark slack: at every ρ the returned unshocked
activity differs from the benchmark xbench by 0.70% in the ∞-norm. The slack is an input-output
identity residual inherited from the extended ICIO matrix Z∗rather than a model artifact, and it
is essentially independent of ρ. The empirical losses λc({k}) reported in Section 4 are computed
against this unshocked-equilibrium reference, not against xbench directly, so the slack does not
enter the chokepoint-loss measurements.
Taken together, the stability table and figure establish that the damped-Picard iteration is
contractive on this dataset across the full empirically-plausible range ρ ∈[−3,−1] and well beyond,
including the near-Leontief corner at ρ = −10. The analytic upper bound ρ ≥−3 derived in Section 2
is therefore conservative on this dataset, and the qualitative findings of Section 4 are robust to
substantially more complementary CES specifications than the baseline ρ = −1.
120


## Page 121


The geometry of the convergence is worth recording. The clean log-linear decay of Figure OA.3.1
is the one-dimensional shadow of an anisotropic contraction. Near the fixed point the error evolves
as en+1 ≈Jen, each Jacobian eigenmode contracting at its own rate. Within a few sweeps the
increment aligns with the slowest surviving mode and decays geometrically at the dominant |λ|,
which is why ˆρemp is read off the late window. The practical corollary is that low-dimensional
projections of a norm-monotone contraction can overshoot, reverse sign, or stall as fast, slow, and
rotating modes mix, much as a damped pendulum’s displacement crosses zero while its energy
falls monotonically. And it is exactly this gap that the late-window averaging, and the Anderson
acceleration acting on the slow stiff modes, are designed to smooth.
A separate concern is whether the model remains empirically informative across the same
(τ,ρ) range. It does. The single-chokepoint solves with closure scenarios Q = {k} active return
finite, differentiated equilibria at every node of the (τ,ρ) rectangle. As τ rises or ρ becomes more
complementary the world loss grows smoothly and the bundle-efficiency factor mini eκi falls, but it
stays bounded away from zero: it does not fall below 0.62 on the grid of Online Appendix OA.4,
which runs to τ = 0.5, and remains near 0.47 even at τ = 0.7. No closure saturates and no cluster of
closures converges to a common equilibrium; the country-level differentiation that motivates the
paper’s empirical contribution is present throughout. Closures of the Pacific-basin straits (Malacca,
Panama, Taiwan) stay the least consequential at every calibration, because the share of intermediate
flows whose tonnage transits them at incidence ≥τ is materially smaller, while Hormuz stays the
most consequential. The baseline calibration is set to (τ,ρ,ϱ) = (0.3,−1,−1) at the center of mass of
the relevant literatures, not to avoid any frontier, since none is present.
OA.4
Robustness and Parametric Variation
This appendix combines the robustness of the empirical findings with a finding-level parametric
audit. The main claims, the heavy tail of cross-country exposure, the supplier-side concentration
of each chokepoint’s loss, and the exporter–importer asymmetry, are robust to variation in the
structural parameters throughout the calibration rectangle. Under the two-nest fulfillment rule
every (τ,ρ) node returns a finite, differentiated equilibrium: the full friction range τ ∈[0.1,0.7]
is well posed at the baseline ρ = −1 and at every milder ρ, and the leakage may be lowered to a
near-total blockade δ = 0.01 without loss of well-posedness. The CES feedback propagates through
h explicitly, so the bundle penalties deepen as ρ becomes more negative or τ approaches the
no-substitution endpoint, and soften as the leakage δ rises: a larger residual pass-through keeps
realized retention further from zero, exactly as the retention-floor bound of footnote 8 predicts. On
our chokepoint problems we verify the delivery-loop rate condition of Assumption 2, ¯eθ ¯aω < θ 1−ρ,
chokepoint by chokepoint at the baseline δ = 0.10, and it holds with comfortable slack throughout
121


## Page 122


the rectangle. The underlying numerical stability of the damped-Picard iteration is documented
separately in Online Appendix OA.3.
Across the rectangle the structural pattern of which countries are exposed, and through which
chokepoints, is invariant. Only the magnitude scales with the supplier-side friction τ and the CES
complementarity |ρ|. The qualitative findings of Section 4, the dominance of Hormuz, the heavy-
tailed cross-country exposure, the bimodal Herfindahl concentration, and the exporter–importer
asymmetry, do not depend on the calibration. The remainder of this appendix makes this precise.
It reports a (τ,ρ) world-loss grid with the strong-complementarity probe (Section OA.4.1) and the
leakage sensitivity (Section OA.4.2). It then reports the country-level exposure of major economies
and the rank stability of the cross-country distribution (Sections OA.4.3 and OA.4.4), and a closing
audit of the four main findings across the rectangle (Section OA.4.5).

OA.4.2
Leakage sensitivity of world loss
Table OA.4.4 and Figure OA.4.1 report the world value-added loss for Hormuz and the three
joint scenarios as the uniform leakage is varied, holding (τ,ρ,ϱ) = (0.3,−1,−1). World loss falls
monotonically as the leakage rises, because a larger residual pass-through leaves more of the
benchmark flow intact. The Hormuz single closure runs from 0.70% at a near-total blockade
δ = 0.01 through the baseline 0.62% at δ = 0.10 and downward as the leak widens. The dependence
is mild for the small East Asian shock and largest for the Middle East joint, but every scenario
stays finite at every leakage, down to δ = 0.01: the Middle East joint reads 1.06% at the near-total
blockade and 0.94% at the baseline. This is the empirical statement of Proposition 1: a strictly
positive leakage keeps every closure well posed, and for the Gulf’s three corridors the whole
historically admissible leakage range stays in the differentiated regime.

First, the rank ordering of the six economies is essentially invariant across all five calibrations in
the table: the UAE is always by far the most exposed, followed by Korea and India, then Japan,
China, and the USA. Every corner returns a finite, differentiated loss vector, so no calibration in the

The UAE’s exposure to Hormuz closure is set by the fraction of its exports routed through Hormuz,
which is close to one and bounded above, whereas the importers’ exposure is governed by the
substitution elasticity of their input bundle, which is the parameter ρ.

Rank stability of country exposures
A cleaner numerical summary of cross-calibration invariance is the Spearman rank correlation
between the baseline country-by-Hormuz λc vector and the same vector at each corner calibration.
Table OA.4.3 reports Spearman ρrank and Kendall τ over the 87-economy vector λc({Hormuz}). The
rank correlation stays at or above 0.98 at the corners (0.2,−1), (0.4,−1), and (0.5,−0.25). It softens
to 0.945 only at the mildest corner (0.2,−0.25), where the smallest losses make the ranking most
sensitive to reshuffling among near-zero exposures. The empirical ordering of vulnerable economies
reported in Section 4 is therefore not an artifact of the particular (τ,ρ) choice. It is determined by
the topology of maritime trade routes and the geography of Gulf-supplied intermediate inputs.

OA.4.5
The four findings across the rectangle
The grid and figures above fix the world-loss surface. We close by verifying that each of the
paper’s four findings, not merely each magnitude, survives movement across the rectangle, reading
every number off the committed model outputs. Finding 1, the dominance of Hormuz and the
cross-chokepoint ordering, is the content of Table OA.4.1 and the discussion above. The other three
are taken in turn.
Take Finding 4, the joint dichotomy, first. Table OA.4.4 varies the uniform leakage for the Hor-
muz single closure and the three joint scenarios at the baseline (τ,ρ). World loss falls monotonically
as the closure leaks more, and the dichotomy of Section 5 holds at every leakage. The Middle East
joint is the largest scenario at 0.94% of world value added, but it is sub-additive: its three gates
duplicate one another, with Suez and Bab-el-Mandeb in series on the Red Sea route and Hormuz
upstream of the same Gulf oil, so S(QME) = 0.84 < 1. The Russia–Europe joint is intermediate and
super-additive (S(QRE) = 1.07), and the East Asia joint an order of magnitude smaller and also
super-additive (S(QEA) = 1.31). Every scenario stays finite at every leakage down to a near-total
blockade δ = 0.01; the Middle East joint, the most leakage-sensitive, reads 1.06% there and 0.94%
at the baseline. The subadditivity of the Middle East joint and the super-additivity of the other two
are stable across the leakage range.

Findings 2 and 3, the heavy tail and the exporter–importer asymmetry, are properties of the
cross-section of country losses rather than of the world aggregate. And they are preserved across the
rectangle for one reason, that the cross-country exposure vector barely re-orders as the parameters
move. At the baseline calibration the worst-case exposure distribution carries a rank-size (Zipf)
tail coefficient of ˆζ ≈1.1 over the country tail, excluding the constructed ROW blocks. It carries a
cross-country maximum of 17% and a mean of about 3% of GDP. The exporter–importer asymmetry
at Hormuz is 17× (supply-side mean 12% against demand-side 0.7%), and it is positive at all but
125


## Page 126


one of the twelve corridors (Figure 6). Across the corners of the rectangle the Spearman rank
correlation of the country-by-Hormuz exposure vector with its baseline counterpart stays above
0.94 at every corner (Online Appendix OA.4). So the set of tail economies and the direction of the
supply-side concentration are invariant, and only the levels scale with τ and |ρ|. The heavy tail and
the asymmetry are therefore structural, fixed by the topology of maritime trade and the geography
of supply rather than by the calibration.
OA.5
Parameter Estimation
The body fixes the three structural parameters (δ,ρ,τ) at the center of mass of the relevant litera-
tures (Section 4.1). This appendix estimates each one directly from external real-world evidence
and sets out how that evidence should be read into the calibration. It has three subsections, one
per parameter. Section OA.5.1 estimates the closure leakage δ, the residual share of trade that still
crosses a closed passage, from the transit and fleet record of eleven historical maritime-disruption
episodes, where it is a reduced-form ratio directly observable in the data. Section OA.5.2 es-
timates the input-substitution parameter ρ, the curvature of the production technology across
sectoral input bundles, from the structural production-function literature that identifies it from
natural experiments and instrumented price variation. It then reconciles those estimates with
our short-run, country-sector setting. Section OA.5.3 estimates the reallocation friction τ, the
share of severed same-sector trade not re-sourced onto surviving suppliers, from the firm-level
supply-chain recovery-rate literature. Throughout we report ranges rather than point estimates,
and where the baseline calibration sits at an edge of the evidence we read it against the robustness
sweep of Online Appendix OA.4.
OA.5.1
Leakage δ from historical episodes
By Assumption 1, δ is the through-passage residual: the share of a fully exposed link’s benchmark
flow that still crosses a closed chokepoint, the retained weight 1 −(1 −δ)dij evaluated at dij = 1.
It is orthogonal to the reroutability e of Section 2.1, the around-passage salvage a longer voyage
recovers: e sends a shipment around the obstacle, δ lets it through despite the closure. The
empirical counterpart of δ is accordingly a ratio: the transit that continues through a passage
during a closure, divided by its pre-episode benchmark, read at the steady-state trough of the
episode and, wherever the data permit, separately by commodity. No source reports δ directly.
Every figure below is accordingly an inferred quantity, constructed from measured transit counts,
cargo volumes, fleet shares, insurance premia, or canal revenues. We mark at each step what is
measured and what is inferred from it, and we report intervals, not points.
Four features of the data force δ to be reported as an interval bracketed by episodes. First,
126


## Page 127


aggregate throughput conflates the two adjustment margins: a fall in transits at a passage mixes
flows that rerouted (the margin e grades) with flows that were destroyed, and only the residual that
continued is δ. Separating them requires vessel-level Automatic Identification System (AIS) tracks.
The IMF–Oxford PortWatch platform (International Monetary Fund and University of Oxford
Environmental Change Institute, 2026), built on satellite AIS for some 90,000 ships across 28
chokepoints, lets the displaced tonnage be seen reappearing on the detour route rather than have
its fate inferred. We therefore read δ off vessel-class transit ratios rather than monthly tonnage.
Second, the episodes with a clean δ (a terminal gate with no detour, where the entire residual is
leakage) and the episodes with rich data (the reroutable Asia–Europe corridors) do not overlap. The
terminal gates have essentially never been closed, so for them δ is bounded but not point-identified.
Third, leakage is commodity-specific. In every episode boxes flee and tankers persist, which is why
the baseline calibration carries a two-commodity δs and why the intervals below are reported by
commodity rather than pooled. Fourth, the observed δ is conditional on enforcement intensity,
which is endogenous to the episode, and the determined, sustained closure the counterfactual
contemplates sits at the low-δ edge of the historical range or beyond it. The eleven episodes of
Table OA.5.1 therefore serve to bracket δ, commodity by commodity, not to average it.

It caps throughput exogenously and so informs the short-run capacity ceiling ¯di of Section 2.4, not the leakage δ. S Every
observed Hormuz-area episode left Gulf oil largely flowing, so the record bounds δHormuz from below but cannot
point-identify the leakage under a determined, sustained closure, the object the model’s Hormuz column contemplates.
The Houthi campaign against Red Sea shipping from December 2023 is the cleanest available
reading of δ, because it is a genuine war-risk closure observed at vessel-level resolution. Carriers
withdrew once war-risk premia rose, exactly the model’s notion of closure, rather than because
the passage was physically obstructed. What the sources measure is unambiguous. Container
transits through the Bab-el-Mandeb / Suez corridor fell by roughly 90% between December 2023
and the early-2024 trough, with above 60% of container tonnage diverted around the Cape of
Good Hope (United Nations Conference on Trade and Development, 2024; Lloyd’s List Intelligence,
2024). The Suez Canal Authority’s revenue fell from about $9.4 billion in 2023 to roughly $4
billion in 2024, a fall of some 60%, and Egypt reported cumulative canal losses above $8 billion
128


## Page 129


(Suez Canal Authority, 2024; Kamali et al., 2024). From these measured quantities we infer δ for
the container segment as the residual that still transited: δbox ∈[0.05,0.15] at the trough. The
inference is clean here, and only here, because the AIS tracks of PortWatch show the missing 90%
reappearing on Cape voyages rather than vanishing (International Monetary Fund and University
of Oxford Environmental Change Institute, 2026). The decline is detour (e), so the surviving
residual is leakage (δ) rather than destroyed demand. The interval is triangulated across three
independent measurements that converge: the ∼90% transit decline, the >60% rerouting share,
and the ∼35% canal-revenue fall are mutually consistent only with a residual near one-tenth.
We read the steady-state δ off the trough plateau. That the residual drifted back toward 30% by
late 2024 as the war-risk market adapted is itself the signature of δ’s dependence on enforcement
intensity, not a contradiction of the trough reading.
Crude and products tell a different story on the same corridor, and the contrast is the substantive
payoff of resolving δ by commodity. As of late 2024, roughly 55% of oil tankers were still transiting
Bab-el-Mandeb against 30% of container vessels, and the strait’s oil flow had fallen from a pre-crisis
8–9 million barrels per day to 3–4 million, a decline of 55–60% (Lloyd’s List Intelligence, 2024; U.S.
Energy Information Administration, 2025b). From the measured tanker share (residual ∼55%) and
the measured volume decline (residual ∼40–45%) we infer δoil ∈[0.40,0.55]: even under active
attack, oil leaks four to five times more freely than boxes. The mechanism is straightforward:
self-insured, war-risk-priced tonnage keeps energy moving where container lines, answerable to
schedule-reliability and corporate-insurance constraints, withdraw.
The most consequential chokepoint turns out to be the one history identifies least. The Strait of
Hormuz has never been closed. Its benchmark is enormous, some twenty million barrels per day,
about a fifth of global petroleum-liquids consumption and a quarter of seaborne oil (U.S. Energy
Information Administration, 2025a). But the relevant episodes are attacks, not closures, and they
measure δ only from below. During the 1984–88 Tanker War, Iraq and Iran struck on the order of
450 vessels, yet the strait stayed open, since Iran depended on it for its own exports, and Gulf oil
shipments were not significantly disrupted. The United States reflagged Kuwaiti tankers under
Operation Earnest Will rather than see the flow stop (Robert S. Strauss Center for International
Security and Law, 2024). In the 2019 Gulf of Oman attacks, the war-risk premium for a supertanker
transit jumped roughly tenfold, yet traffic continued (Tan, 2019). What these episodes measure
is attack counts and insurance premia. What we infer is a lower bound, δHormuz ≳0.8 under
intense but undetermined pressure, cross-checked by the absence of any sustained Gulf supply
interruption in either episode. This, stated plainly, is the identification ceiling we face. The
determined, sustained closure of a terminal gate, the model’s most consequential counterfactual,
has no historical analogue, so for Hormuz the record disciplines δ from below but cannot pin
it. We therefore treat the baseline δ = 0.10 on the Hormuz column as a deliberately severe (low-
129


## Page 130


leakage, high-loss) reading and report the δ ∈{0.05,0.10,0.20} sensitivity of Online Appendix OA.4
alongside it.
An administrative-friction episode corroborates that energy leakage is high and hard to suppress.
In 2022 Turkey’s proof-of-insurance rule at the Bosphorus queued some twenty to thirty tankers
for days before clearing, delaying but never stopping the oil (Insurance Journal, 2022). Even a
determined administrative obstruction of a crude corridor slowed the flow without halting it,
pointing to the same conclusion as the Red Sea tanker record: for the energy gates the residual is
large.
Three further episodes fix the endpoints of the support rather than an interior value. Physical,
total closures drive δ to zero: the eight-year Suez closure of 1967–75, the model’s e-benchmark
for the Cape detour (Feyrer, 2021), and the six-day Ever Given grounding of 2021 (Notteboom
and Rodrigue, 2021), the latter too brief to register at the disruption horizon. At the opposite
end, a named war-risk without a closure leaves δ ≈1: the Joint War Committee listed the Strait
of Malacca as a war-risk area from 2005 to 2006 with no interruption to traffic (Ho, 2005). The
Panama drought of 2023–24, finally, is a deliberate non-member of this table’s logic. It cut daily
transits from thirty-six to twenty-two by exogenous water rationing (Seatrade Maritime News,
2024), which constrains the short-run capacity ceiling ¯di of Section 2.4 rather than the leakage, and
we flag it here precisely to keep the two margins separate.
The historical record therefore does not support a single universal δ; what it supports is a
commodity-conditioned one. For the container and general-cargo corridors that dominate the
Asia–Europe gates (Suez, Bab-el-Mandeb, Gibraltar, Malacca, Dover), a determined war-risk closure
leaves δ ∈[0.05,0.15], centring the baseline δ = 0.10. For the crude, product, and bulk corridors that
dominate the energy gates (Hormuz, the Turkish and Danish Straits, Kerch), the record puts leakage
substantially higher, at δ ∈[0.40,0.55] under active attack. The body adopts the uniform reading
δs ≡0.10 as its baseline, applying the well-identified container-corridor leakage to every seaborne
good. The commodity-conditioned reading, which sets δs = 0.45 on the energy commodities, the
center of the under-attack tanker interval [0.40,0.55], and δs = 0.10 on every other good, the
center of the container interval [0.05,0.15], is reported alongside it as a robustness exercise in
Online Appendix OA.4. Either reading is admissible without altering any result of Section 2, since
Proposition 1 requires only δs > 0 on the benchmark support, and under the two-nest fulfillment
rule both return a finite, differentiated equilibrium for every single and joint closure. The uniform
baseline is the more conservative of the two on the energy gates, since it applies to tankers a
box-like enforcement that no historical episode has achieved.
130


## Page 131


OA.5.2
Substitution ρ from the production-function literature
Unlike the leakage δ, the substitution parameter ρ cannot be read off observed quantities as a
ratio. It is the curvature of the production technology across a buyer’s sectoral input bundles, the
exponent ρ in the CES bundle factor eκ(ω)
j
of Section 2.3, strictly negative when those bundles are
complements. The real-world evidence on it is the body of structural estimates of how readily a
producer substitutes one sectoral input for another. We take that evidence from the literature that
estimates the elasticity of substitution among intermediate inputs from natural experiments and
instrumented price variation. We translate each estimate into the corresponding ρ (an elasticity
of substitution ε maps to ρ = 1 −1/ε, so any ε < 1 returns ρ < 0). We then make explicit the three
features of our setting that decide which of the many reported elasticities the ρ of this paper should
match: the short-run horizon, the country-sector granularity of the inputs, and the country-sector
granularity of output. We report ρ as a range throughout, since it is the structural parameter on
which the calibration is least sharply pinned.
The natural point of departure is Atalay (2017), who estimates the elasticity of substitution
among intermediate inputs from United States industry input-expenditure shares, instrumenting
relative input-price movements with sector-specific military-procurement shocks. His estimates
place that elasticity below 0.2, so that the implied ρ lies below about −4: strongly complementary,
far from the Cobb–Douglas value ρ = 0. Atalay separately estimates the elasticity between value
added and the intermediate aggregate at roughly 0.2, but that is a different nest from the one the
bundle factor penalizes, which is substitution among the sectoral inputs themselves, and we do
not use it. The firm-level natural-experiment evidence points the same way and is sharper about
the horizon. Boehm, Flaaen and Pandalai-Nayar (2019) treat the 2011 T¯ohoku earthquake as an
exogenous outage of Japanese suppliers and find that the output of affected affiliates falls almost
one-for-one with their lost imported inputs. The relation is close enough to Leontief to drive ρ far
below −1, toward the complementary limit. Carvalho et al. (2021), following the same earthquake
through the Japanese production network, and Barrot and Sauvagnat (2016), exploiting natural
disasters that strike suppliers of specific inputs, both document downstream propagation that only
strong short-run complementarity can rationalize. The network-macro literature that calibrates the
elasticity rather than estimating it, including Baqaee and Farhi (2022), Baqaee and Farhi (2024),
Bonadio et al. (2021), and the cross-disruption assessment of Goldberg and Reed (2023), adopts
short-run values in the 0.2–0.5 band, that is ρ ∈[−4,−1], as its working range. Table OA.5.2 collects
these, each with the real-world variation that identifies it and the ρ it implies.
