Read at face value the structural evidence is concentrated well below the Cobb–Douglas
benchmark: the directly estimated, finely disaggregated elasticities sit near ρ ≤−4, and the
calibrated network-macro range runs to ρ = −1. Our baseline ρ = −1 sits at the upper, less-
complementary edge of that macro range, and matching the estimates to the right object (a
short-run, country-sector elasticity rather than a firm-level or micro one) is what the rest of this
subsection supplies.
Three features of our setting fix which entry in Table OA.5.2 the ρ of this model should match,
and because they do not all push the same way each must be stated.
The first is that the counterfactual is short run. Substitution possibilities widen as producers
re-engineer their input mix, retool, and qualify new suppliers, so the elasticity rises with the
horizon allowed for adjustment and the short-run value is the most complementary one. Ports,
refineries, supplier relationships and product specifications are fixed within the disruption window,
so the estimates that speak to our ρ are the impact and first-year readings, the disaster-based ones
that sit lowest, at and below ρ = −1. A long-run elasticity would answer a different question from
the one the model poses. This feature holds ρ firmly negative.
The second is that inputs enter at the granularity of country-sectors, collected into sectoral
bundles, and ρ is the elasticity across those bundles. It is thus the cross-sector margin alone,
the substitutability of, say, refined petroleum against basic metals in a downstream producer’s
132


## Page 133


bundle. It is not the substitution among the several country suppliers of a single input, which the
within-sector reallocation τ and the balancing of Definition 1 already carry. Two things follow. The
literature value we want is the across-broad-sector elasticity, not the across-origin (Armington)
elasticity, which is several times larger and belongs to τ rather than ρ. The bundle is also coarse,
since each of the forty-odd sectors pools many narrower products that are themselves mutual
substitutes, so the elasticity between bundles exceeds the elasticity between the fine inputs of the
micro studies. Peter and Ruane (2024) make exactly this point, that the aggregate consequences
of intermediate-input substitution depend on the aggregation at which the elasticity is read. The
finely disaggregated ρ ≲−4 of Atalay (2017) is on this count a most-complementary bound on our
ρ rather than its value, and the feature lifts ρ back up.
The third is that output, too, is at the country-sector level. The producing unit is not a plant or
a firm but an aggregate of many heterogeneous producers, and a single CES technology imposed
on that aggregate does not inherit the curvature of its members. Even if each constituent producer
can substitute inputs barely at all, the country-sector as a whole substitutes more, because the
composition of which producers are active tilts toward those better able to absorb a given input loss.
Aggregation across heterogeneous units thus raises the effective elasticity above the plant-level
one. This is the micro-to-macro result of Oberfield and Raval (2021) for the capital–labor margin,
and classically of Houthakker (1955), in which Leontief plants drawn from a Pareto distribution
aggregate to a smooth, substitutable industry technology. The near-Leontief firm-level relation
of Boehm, Flaaen and Pandalai-Nayar (2019) is on this count also a lower bound, measured on
precisely the units our country-sectors aggregate over, and the feature lifts ρ up a second time.
Putting the three together, the short-run horizon holds ρ distinctly below zero, while the two
aggregations, of inputs into sectoral bundles and of output into country-sector producers, lift it
above the micro estimates from both sides. The evidence then supports an effective short-run
country-sector ρ in roughly ρ ∈[−1,−0.25], the calibration rectangle of Online Appendix OA.4.
The baseline ρ = −1 sits at its more-complementary edge, closest to the directly estimated micro
and network-macro elasticities. We do not read this as a clean point identification. A reader who
weights the aggregation-toward-zero corrections more heavily would prefer a ρ nearer −0.25. This
is why ρ is the parameter we vary most widely and why every main result is reported across the full
rectangle down to the mild ρ = −0.25 corner of Online Appendix OA.4. The production-function
literature pins the sign and the short-run strength of complementarity firmly and locates the
country-sector ρ within a band. It does not single out a point inside it.
OA.5.3
Reallocation friction τ from the supply-chain literature
The reallocation friction τ is the share of displaced same-sector trade that is not reconstituted
on surviving suppliers within the disruption horizon. A fraction 1 −τ of a buyer’s severed same-
133


## Page 134


sector demand is re-sourced onto the suppliers it can still reach, and a fraction 1 −τ of a severed
supplier’s idle output is placed on the buyers it can still reach. The residual τ is, on each side, lost
(Section 2.2). It is therefore the within-sector, across-supplier substitution margin, and it must
be kept distinct from the other two adjustments. The first is the route detour e, which salvages a
shipment to the same counterparty by a longer voyage and which the reroutability matrices already
capture. The second is the cross-sector complementarity ρ of Online Appendix OA.5.2, which
governs substitution between sectoral inputs rather than among the suppliers of one. Unlike ρ, τ is
a reduced-form recovery rate, and the firm-level supply-chain literature estimates it directly from
the historical disruptions it studies. We collect those estimates, read each as the complementary
unrecovered share τ = 1 −(recovery rate), and map them into our setting through the short-run
horizon, the supplier-substitution margin just isolated, and the country-sector granularity. We
report τ as a range, with baseline τ = 0.3.
The cleanest estimate comes from the same natural experiment that disciplines ρ, read here for
a different object. Boehm, Flaaen and Pandalai-Nayar (2019) find that in the year after the 2011
T¯ohoku earthquake the downstream customers of disrupted Japanese suppliers recovered only
about 60–70% of their pre-disruption input flows, an unrecovered share τ ≈0.3–0.4 over a one-year
window. Carvalho et al. (2021), tracing the same shock through the Japanese production network,
report comparable first-year unrecovered shares for directly hit suppliers and customers. And
Barrot and Sauvagnat (2016) show that where the severed input is specific or relationship-intensive,
not readily obtained from an alternative supplier, the recovered share is markedly lower, placing
τ toward the upper end of the range. The pandemic studies of Bonadio et al. (2021) and Baqaee
and Farhi (2022) return short-run unrecovered shares in the 0.3–0.5 band depending on sector and
horizon. And the cross-disruption assessment of Goldberg and Reed (2023) places the centroid of
cross-sectoral short-run unrecoverable shares near τ = 0.35. Table OA.5.3 collects these.

Two features of the evidence carry into the calibration. The central tendency of the cross-sector
recovery rate sits near τ ∈[0.3,0.4], with Goldberg and Reed (2023) at 0.35. The friction is also
heterogeneous across inputs, low where the severed good is fungible and re-sourced almost in full
and high where it is specific. So any single τ is a central value standing in for a distribution, the
same simplification the two-commodity δs of Online Appendix OA.5.1 makes for a continuously
varying leak.
Three considerations place our τ within this evidence. The first is the horizon. The recovered
share grows as firms locate, qualify and contract alternative suppliers, so τ falls with the time
allowed, and the short-run, roughly one-quarter-to-one-year window of our counterfactual is the
regime the disaster studies measure. The baseline τ = 0.3 is accordingly read off their one-year
recovery rates rather than off a longer-run value that would understate the friction.
The second is that τ is the supplier-substitution margin alone, and the recovery rates must be
matched to it without absorbing the other two adjustments. The literature measures how much
of a lost input a downstream producer makes good by turning to other sources of the same input,
which is just what τ measures. The longer-voyage salvage that dominates a maritime disruption
is the separate channel carried by the reroutability e, and substitution toward a different sector’s
input is carried by ρ. The estimates we use are therefore the within-sector input-recovery rates net
of any route adjustment, not a composite resilience measure that would blend e, τ and ρ together.
The model applies the recovered share symmetrically to the two sides of a severed link, whereas
the studies identify it on the buyer side, and we impose the same friction on the severed supplier’s
search for replacement buyers.
135


## Page 136


The third is the country-sector granularity, which here pulls in two directions that roughly
cancel. On one hand a country-sector pools many firms and many same-sector suppliers, so its
scope for re-sourcing exceeds a single firm’s and the aggregate recovered share would exceed
the firm-level estimate, pushing τ down. On the other the balancing of Definition 1 is support-
preserving, in that it redistributes displaced mass only onto trading relationships already present
at benchmark, never onto a newly formed one. The measured firm-level recovery, by contrast,
includes precisely such new-supplier formation, which pushes τ back up. With the two effects
offsetting, the firm-level recovery band transfers to the country-sector τ without the systematic
aggregation correction that ρ required, and τ is correspondingly the better-identified of the two.
Taken together the evidence supports τ near [0.3,0.4] at the center, with a heavier-friction tail for
specific inputs. We adopt the baseline τ = 0.3, a seventy-percent reconstitution of displaced same-
sector trade, at the lower, more-recoverable edge of that center, reporting the full τ ∈{0.2,0.4,0.5}
sweep of Online Appendix OA.4 around it. The baseline value thus sits just below the Goldberg and
Reed (2023) centroid. So on the reallocation margin, as on the leakage and substitution margins,
the robustness corners that raise the loss, here the less-recoverable τ = 0.4 and 0.5, bracket the
more pessimistic readings the evidence admits.
OA.6
Validation: the footprint regression, the loss surface, and breadth
This appendix backs Section 6. It sets out the footprint regression behind Section 6.1 and reports
its full battery. It backs the coherence check of Section 6.2 with the full parameter surface it
summarizes and the buffer accounting that relates the model’s unbuffered loss to the realized
aggregate. And it records two negative results, on widening the cross-section and on the canonical
historical closure, that together show the fine sectoral panel of Section 6.1 is the right test and not
merely the available one.
OA.6.1
The footprint regression: design, positioning, and results
This subsection sets out the design behind the footprint test of Section 6.1, places it in the econo-
metric literature, and reports the full battery the body summarizes. The battery comprises the
specification ladder, the multi-episode replication, and the complete network-footprint horse-race.
Consider first the obstacle the design must clear. The 2023–24 window offers no clean exper-
iment: war-risk premia, energy prices, monetary tightening, and exchange-rate movements all
moved output over the same quarters. So a time-series regression of activity on an indicator for
“the closure” would credit the Red Sea with whatever else occurred and would not identify the
aggregate effect. What the closure does deliver is a cross-sectional prediction. The model maps
it into a footprint over country-sectors, a predicted-exposure vector that a different gate would
136


## Page 137


not reproduce. The answerable question is therefore whether the realized post-onset decline is
distributed across country-sectors as the Red Sea footprint implies rather than as the footprint of
Panama, Korea, Hormuz, or the Danish Straits would. Concretely, for country-sector i and quarter
t we estimate
∆yi,t =

where ∆yi,t is the growth deviation of real value added and INDi is the model’s network-propagated
Red Sea exposure (the contraction i inherits through its network neighbors). The regressor DIRi is
its direct exposure, controlled so that the coefficient is orthogonal to i’s own first-round involvement.
The index κt is event time relative to the December-2023 onset, αc,t and θs,t are country-by-quarter
and sector-by-quarter effects, and µi is a country-sector effect. Standard errors are clustered by
country. The object of inference is the response of value-added growth to network exposure. It is
the lead-and-lag path {βk} in the event study, or its single post-onset analogue β in the difference-
in-differences that replaces the event-time interactions with one INDi × Postt term. The fixed
effects are absorbed by within-transformation (pyhdfe) and exposures are standardized to unit
standard deviation. A coefficient therefore reads as the percentage-point value-added response to a
one-standard-deviation increase in network exposure to the closed gates.
Identification rests on one restriction, which the fixed effects make transparent. The country-
by-quarter effects absorb every national macroeconomic path, the sector-by-quarter effects every
global sectoral shock, and the country-sector effects every permanent exposure difference. What
is left to identify β is the within-country-quarter, within-sector-quarter covariance between the
realized decline and the Red Sea footprint. The estimate recovers the footprint’s effect so long as
no other shock over the window carries the same country-sector footprint as the Red Sea exposure
vector. This is the shift-share exclusion restriction (Goldsmith-Pinkham, Sorkin and Swift, 2020;
Borusyak, Hull and Jaravel, 2022), with the model-generated exposure as the share that assigns
treatment intensity across cells. Unlike a conventional exclusion restriction this one is directly
testable, because the same propagation operator generates a footprint for every other gate. Were
the identifying variation common to any network pattern rather than specific to the Red Sea’s, an
orthogonal gate’s footprint would load too. The placebo and horse-race below put exactly this to
the data.
It is worth locating this design in the econometric literature. The regression machinery of fixed
effects, a continuous exposure regressor, event-study leads and lags, and placebo regressors is
entirely standard. The novelty is the source of identifying variation, a model-generated, shock-
specific network footprint, and the use of alternative propagated footprints as structured placebos.
Four connections locate it. First, it is a shift-share (Bartik) design, with the closure as the aggregate
137


## Page 138


shifter and the model-generated exposure vector as the share that assigns treatment intensity
across country-sectors, and the restriction stated above is its exclusion restriction. Second, it is
a continuous-treatment event study: country-sectors are not treated or untreated but differ in
predicted exposure intensity, and the leads trace whether the more exposed begin to fall only after
onset. Third, the alternative chokepoint exposures are structured placebos, or negative controls,
stronger than arbitrary placebos because the same propagation operator generates them from
the benchmark network. So a null on an orthogonal footprint is informative about specificity.
Fourth, the design is related to the production-network literature that builds indirect shock
exposure from input-output linkages (Acemoglu et al., 2012; Carvalho et al., 2021; Barrot and
Sauvagnat, 2016; Boehm, Flaaen and Pandalai-Nayar, 2019). It differs in that the shock is a
maritime-route closure and the exposure vector is not direct input reliance but the post-closure
propagated footprint the model produces, inclusive of reroutability, reallocation, capacity, and
complementarity. Conceptually the test is a partialling-out (Frisch–Waugh–Lovell) exercise. The
fixed effects residualize out country-time, sector-time, and permanent country-sector components,
and the question is whether the residualized decline aligns with the residualized Red Sea footprint.
But the contribution is not the residualization, it is the economic construction of the exposure
vectors. We do not describe the design as a new estimator; rather, it is a familiar design with a
novel, model-generated identifying regressor and a family of alternative footprints that serve as
diagnostic placebos.
Two econometric cautions that attach to modern two-way fixed-effects designs apply here and
are addressed directly. First, with a continuous treatment and potentially heterogeneous effects,
TWFE coefficients can aggregate cell-specific effects with non-convex weights (de Chaisemartin
and D’Haultfœuille, 2020; Callaway and Sant’Anna, 2021; Borusyak, Jaravel and Spiess, 2024).
Our setting avoids the worst of this, since there is a single, common onset date, so the staggered-
adoption negative-weighting problem does not arise, and the event-study leads are estimated freely
rather than imputed. Still, the DiD coefficient should be read as an exposure-weighted average
response rather than a structural constant, and the magnitude question is carried by the scale
diagnostics. Second, inference clusters on 23–42 countries, at the lower edge of asymptotic comfort.
Wild-cluster-bootstrap p-values (Cameron, Gelbach and Miller, 2008) computed for the main
acute-window estimate leave the conclusion unchanged (null-imposed Rademacher bootstrap,
p = 0.02).
The results begin with incidence and the specification ladder. We estimate equation (OA.6.1)
on the OECD ten-industry value-added panel of forty-two economies over 2022Q1–2024Q4. The
coefficient on propagated exposure is negative throughout and sharpens as the window narrows to
the acute phase of the closure. It is −0.18 (t = −0.6) on the full window, −0.46 (t = −1.3) on the post-
2023 window, and −0.66 (t = −3.1, wild-cluster-bootstrap p = 0.02) on the acute 2023Q2–2024Q4
138


## Page 139


window. The event-study profile (Figure 11) has statistically flat leads, a pre-onset average of +0.79
(s.e. 0.72) against a post-onset average of −0.26, and reaches its trough about a year after onset.
Estimating without the United States returns −0.68 (t = −3.3), and adding it back (recovered from
the BEA value-added-by-industry series on FRED) moves the estimate only to −0.66, so the result
is not an artifact of the European core.
The footprint also replicates across episodes. Applying the identical operator to four inde-
pendent episodes, each with the named gate(s) as the closure and the others as placebos, gives
Table OA.6.1 and Figure OA.6.1. The 2023–24 Red Sea closure, the one sudden, sustained closure
of a major corridor in the sample, loads with the predicted negative sign and is the only episode to
do so significantly. The transient 2021 Ever Given grounding and the gradual 2023–24 Panama
drought are null, exactly as a six-day or buffered shock should be. The 2022 Black Sea disruption is
statistically null as well, its footprint entangled with the contemporaneous European energy-price
shock that hit the same energy-intensive cells. The pattern of detection and non-detection thus
follows the episodes’ physical character, which a single episode cannot establish.

This is the exclusion restriction taken to the data. Because the gates’ footprints are themselves
correlated, we ask the sharper question directly. Does the closed gate’s own footprint survive when
every other gate’s footprint enters the same regression (Figure OA.6.2)? Against the orthogonal
gates, Panama and Korea, whose exposure correlations with the Red Sea footprint are 0.03 and 0.10,
the Red Sea coefficient survives at −0.67 (t = −2.9). The distinct placebos are null (t = 0.4 and 0.6).
Against the correlated gates, the Danish Straits and Hormuz, whose footprint correlations are 0.34
and 0.56, the point estimate stays negative and significant (−1.04, t = −2.7). The correlated placebos
load marginally with the opposite sign (Hormuz +0.24, t = 2.0; Danish +0.31, t = 1.0). This marks
the correct limit of the design, not a confound. Network-footprint orthogonalization distinguishes
the Red Sea pattern from orthogonal patterns cleanly, but it cannot mechanically separate shocks

OA.6.2
The Red Sea loss surface across the calibration rectangle
Section 6.2 reads the parameter region off two solved surfaces: the world value-added loss LW (τ,ρ,δ)
and the most-exposed national loss λEGY(τ,ρ,δ) from the Red Sea closure Q = {Suez,BAM}. Both
are computed from the committed model cache by the same solver as the body, holding the
geography, the chokepoint incidence Γ , and the reroutability E fixed and varying only (τ,ρ,δ).
The shared-Cape detour class {Suez,BAM,Gibraltar} is imposed, so that closing two gates on one
corridor takes the per-link maximum disruption rather than compounding it (Section 5.1). Without
that correction the naive union of the two gates would double-count the Asia–Europe links they
share. Every link that transits both gates would carry the disrupted fraction 1 −(1 −e)2 = e(2 −e)
rather than e, overstating the exposed mass by the factor 2 −e, between roughly 1.5 and 1.8 across
the corridor’s country pairs (Table OA.2.4). With the correction, closing the two gates together is
the same experiment as closing either alone, because at the region resolution of the routing every
flow that transits one transits the other (Section 3.4). The Red Sea closure accordingly inherits the
single-gate world loss of Table 4. Table OA.6.2 reports the two surfaces on the (τ,ρ) rectangle at
the baseline leakage δ = 0.10.

The two surfaces have the structure the coherence argument relies on. The world loss is modest
and slowly varying. It runs from about 0.1% at the mild corner to 0.2–0.4% across the estimated
τ-center [0.3,0.4], reaching about 0.5% only at the high-friction corner and varying smoothly across
the rectangle with no steep corner. The qualitative reading, that a sustained, unbuffered Red Sea
closure costs of order a quarter to a half percent of world output, is therefore a property of the center
of the rectangle, not of the baseline node alone. The Egyptian network component, by contrast, is
steep in the parameters, climbing roughly eightfold across the rectangle. So the requirement that
the unbuffered total, this component plus the measured receipts loss of ≈1.5% of GDP, stay within
the order of the realized Egyptian experience cuts a downward-sloping consistency band through
the rectangle. The band traces the same friction–substitution trade-off that governs the world loss,
with the baseline calibration at its severe edge. The contrast between a flat world surface and a
steep national one is not an accident of Egypt. It is the supplier-side concentration of Section 4.6,
by which a closure’s loss is borne disproportionately by the economies on the severed routes. The
most-exposed national loss therefore responds to the parameters far more sharply than the world
average, which dilutes it across the diversified interior. This is what makes the Egyptian surface
informative, with signal where the world aggregate has noise, even though what it anchors is a
bound rather than a point.
Leakage moves the Red Sea loss little, which is the second fact the argument uses. Table OA.6.3
reports LW and λEGY at the baseline (τ,ρ,ϱ) = (0.3,−1,−1) across δ ∈{0.05,0.10,0.15,0.20}. The
world loss falls only from about 0.3% to 0.2% and the Egyptian component stays near 0.4% as
the residual pass-through rises, a gentle dependence, because the Red Sea is a reroutable corridor
in which the Cape detour, not the residual leak, carries the displaced trade. The well-identified
container-corridor leakage of Section OA.5.1, δ ∈[0.05,0.15], therefore leaves the Red Sea loss
in a band under a tenth of a percentage point at the world level. And the parameter region of
Section 6.2 is essentially a (τ,ρ) region that the leakage does not perturb. (This is the opposite of
the energy gates of Online Appendix OA.4.2, whose losses are highly δ-sensitive because little of
142


## Page 143


their trade can reroute. The Red Sea’s insensitivity to δ is exactly why it, and not Hormuz, is the
corridor on which a clean realized validation is possible.)

OA.6.3
Buffers and the aggregate moment
The world loss in Table OA.6.2 is the model’s unbuffered steady state. The realized 2024 world fall
was smaller, and the gap is not slack in the validation but a quantity the body already characterizes.
The model carries no inventory state, no strategic-reserve drawdown, and no price-induced demand
substitution (Section 4.1), so it returns the loss that the topology of supply makes possible before
any of those buffers operates. Three of them separate the unbuffered quarter of a percent from
the smaller realized fall. Rerouting is already netted out, because the reroutability matrices
of Online Appendix OA.2 send the Cape-capable flows around the obstacle before the loss is
computed, so it is not part of the residual wedge. What remains is inventory drawdown over the
months-long adjustment, the spare shipping capacity that absorbed the longer voyages without
a proportional freight-cost pass-through, and the subdued global demand of 2024. All three are
named explicitly in the external assessments of Section 6.2. Writing the realized aggregate as bLW
with a buffer-and-horizon attenuation b ∈(0,1], the modest realized world fall, well under a tenth
of a percent, corresponds to b ≈0.25–0.4. This is of a piece with the roughly seven-tenths deflation
of the effective crude-oil friction that ninety-day reserve coverage implies for the energy gates
(Section 4.1). The container corridor, whose buffer is inventory rather than reserves, sits at the
comparable end of that range. Because b is itself uncertain, the aggregate moment is a one-sided
bound, requiring LW (τ,ρ,δ) to lie above the realized fall and not too far above it. Table OA.6.2
satisfies the bound across the entire box: the unbuffered loss stays within the realized order of
magnitude at every corner of the rectangle. The receipts component needs no such wedge, with
b ≈1 for canal tolls, since the rerouting that drives b below one for everyone else is the very channel
that destroys the receipts. But the receipts are a service export outside the goods incidence, so
they are not compared with λEGY, the model’s goods-network component, which is a disjoint piece
of Egypt’s loss (Section 6.2). Instead the receipts discipline the closure-severity input δ, and the
network component enters the consistency region as the unbuffered remainder added on top of
them. This structure, a buffered one-sided world moment, an unbuffered receipts measurement
that pins the severity, and an unbuffered network envelope at Egypt, is why the region of Section 6.2
143


## Page 144


is a band rather than a point. The baseline calibration sits at the band’s severe edge.
OA.6.4
Breadth and the historical experiment
The cross-sectional test of Section 6.1 runs on the OECD ten-industry value-added panel of forty-
two economies. It is tempting to widen it, adding more economies for breadth or the canonical
historical closure for credibility, and both attempts fail in the same way.
Widening the cross-section to the seventy-eight economies for which the World Bank reports
real value added forces the sectoral detail down to four broad groups, and aggregating to total
GDP removes the sectoral dimension altogether. In both cases the network coefficient loses its sign
or reverses (Figure OA.6.3). At coarse resolution resource-route exposure, the Strait of Hormuz
in particular, coincides with oil-exporter status and loads on the 2021–22 energy boom, exactly
the confound the country-by-quarter and sector-by-quarter fixed effects exist to remove. The
identification lives in the fine cross-sectoral footprint of the closure within each fixed-effect cell,
and only the sector-level panel resolves it. The broader but coarser samples reintroduce the very
macroeconomics the design differences out. The same coarse-resolution confound disposes of the
country-aggregate test directly. Regressing each economy’s realized GDP-growth deviation on its
model-predicted country loss returns a wrong-signed, “significant” slope for the Red Sea (−0.41,
t = −2.6). The economies the model says lose most, the Gulf and fast-growing Asia–Europe traders,
simply grew in 2024 for unrelated macroeconomic reasons. And with no fixed effects possible at
the aggregate level, a sub-half-percent chokepoint hit is buried in that growth. The smaller, finer
panel, in short, is the right test and not merely the available one.24
24The same limitation disposes of the most tempting historical experiment, the eight-year closure of the Suez Canal
in 1967–75 that Feyrer (2021) uses as exogenous variation in sea distance. Re-run as a test of this mechanism on the
value added by activity that reaches back to the 1960s (World Bank constant-price series, some thirty economies at four
broad sectors), both the 1967 closure and the 1975 reopening return wrong-signed estimates. Both show pronounced
pre-trends and loading placebos. The 1973–74 oil shock and the 1974–75 recession fall inside the window and correlate
with Middle-East exposure, and the four-sector resolution reintroduces the energy confound. And the benchmark
network is half a century too young. The episode is an excellent trade-cost instrument but not a clean test of the sectoral
propagation studied here.
